Uniform spanning forest on the integer lattice with drift in one coordinate
By Guillermo Martinez Dibene

Abstract
In this article we investigate the Uniform Spanning Forest ( 𝖴𝖲𝖥 ) (\mathsf{USF}) in the nearest-neighbour integer lattice ℤ d + 1 = ℤ × ℤ d \mathds{Z}^{d+1}=\mathds{Z}\times\mathds{Z}^{d} with an assignment of conductances that makes the underlying (Network) Random Walk ( 𝖭𝖱𝖶 \mathsf{NRW} ) drifted towards the right of the first coordinate. This assignment of conductances has exponential growth and decay; in particular, the measure of balls can be made arbitrarily close to zero or arbitrarily large. We establish upper and lower bounds for its Green’s function. We show that in dimension d = 1 , 2 d=1,2 the 𝖴𝖲𝖥 \mathsf{USF} consists of a single tree while in d ≥ 3 , d\geq 3, there are infinitely many trees. We then show, by an intricate study of multiple 𝖭𝖱𝖶 \mathsf{NRW} s, that in every dimension the trees are one-ended; the technique for d = 2 d=2 is completely new, while the technique for d ≥ 3 d\geq 3 is a major makeover of the technique for the proof of the same result for the graph ℤ d . \mathds{Z}^{d}. We finally establish the probability that two or more vertices are 𝖴𝖲𝖥 \mathsf{USF} -connected and study the distance between different trees. Keywords. Uniform Spanning Tree, Uniform Spanning Forest, Biased Random Walk, Drifted Random Walk, Green’s function, Harmonic Functions, Dirichlet Functions, Liouville Property, Second Moment Method. Acknowledgements. This research was partially supported by an NSERC (Canada) grant.



Contents

1
Introduction

All graphs are assumed directed, connected, without multiple edges or self-loops and denumerable. Given an edge
e,
the direction of
e
is given by a starting vertex
e^{-}
and a ending vertex
e^{+}.
All conductances are assumed strictly positive.

This work is based on the PhD thesis of the author; should the reader need more details, consult
[
MD20
]
. A graph without cycles is called a
tree
; the tree is
spanning
if it contains all vertices of the graph. Given a finite connected graph
\mathrm{G},
the number of subgraphs is also finite and thus, there are only finitely many spanning trees in that graph. The uniform spanning tree is, by definition, the probability measure on the set of spanning trees of
\mathrm{G}
satisfying
\mathds{P}(t)=\left(\#\text{ of spanning trees}\right)^{-1}.
This can readily be generalised to the network case,
\mathds{P}(t)=k\cdot\mathsf{weight}(t),
where
k
is a normalising constant. Any of these measures (or the underlying random object) is called “uniform spanning tree.”

Suppose now that
(\mathrm{G},\mu)
is an infinite network. The first paper to introduce
\mathsf{UST}
on infinite graphs was Robin Pemantle’s
[
Pem91
]
. He considered
\mathds{Z}^{d}
-balls corresponding to the norm
\displaystyle\left\lVert x\right\rVert_{\infty}=\max_{1\leq i\leq d}|x_{i}|.
He showed that the uniform spanning tree measure
\mu_{n}
on the trees of
\mathrm{B}_{n}
converges weakly to some measure
\mu.
This is the uniform spanning measure on
\mathds{Z}^{d}.
We will employ the notation
\mathsf{USF}
to denote it.

Later, Itai Benjamini, Russel Lyons, Yuval Peres and Oded Schramm, in
[
BLPS01
]
and using tools unavailable to Pemantle, extended his result from
\mathds{Z}^{d}
to general networks. They started by considering an exhaustion of the graph created by considering increasing families of finite sets of vertices and then considering the networks induced on these sets. If
(\mathrm{G}_{n})
is any such exhaustion, they showed that the uniform spanning forests measure of
\mathrm{G}_{n}
defines a sequence of measures that converge weakly to some measure
\mu^{F}.
They named
\mu^{F}
as “free uniform spanning forest measure.” The second way to construct the uniform spanning forest measure is by considering constraints in the boundary of
\mathrm{G}_{n}.
Indeed, the considered a sequence
(\mathrm{V}_{n})
of families of finite sets of vertices and then constructed the network
\mathrm{G}_{n}^{W},
which is the network induced on
\mathrm{V}_{n}
with the boundary of
\mathrm{V}_{n}
wired into a vertex. Again, they showed that the uniform spanning forest measures of
\mathrm{G}_{n}^{W}
converge to a measure
\mu^{W}
and they called this measure the “wired uniform spanning forest measure.” Several properties of
\mu^{F}
and
\mu^{W}
were studied, in particular, necessary and sufficient conditions were given as to when
\mu^{F}=\mu^{W}.
We will employ
\mathsf{FSF}
and
\mathsf{WSF}
instead of
\mu^{F}
and
\mu^{W},
respectively.

1.1
Basic definitions

Consider any network
(\mathrm{G},\mu).
For a vertex
v,
we define the
conductance
of
v
by
\mu(v)=\sum\limits_{e^{-}=v}\mu(v).
The network random walk (
\mathsf{NRW}
) in
(\mathrm{G},\mu)
is the Markov chain with state space
\mathrm{V}
(the vertex set of
\mathrm{G}
) and transition probability
p(u,v)=\mu(u)^{-1}\left(\sum\limits_{e^{-}=u,e^{+}=v}\mu(e)\right).
If
(S_{n})_{n\in\mathds{Z}_{+}}
is the
\mathsf{NRW}
of
(\mathrm{G},\mu)
, we will write
\tau_{\mathrm{H}}=\inf\{n\in\mathds{Z}_{+}\mid S_{n}\in\mathrm{H}\}
and
\tau_{\mathrm{H}}^{+}=\inf\{n\in\mathds{N}\mid S_{n}\in\mathrm{H}\}
where
\mathrm{H}
is any set of vertices. For sake of simplicity, we write
\tau_{a}=\tau_{\{a\}},

\tau_{a}^{+}=\tau_{\{a\}}^{+}.

\tau_{\mathrm{H}}
is the
hitting time
of
\mathrm{H}
and
\tau_{a}
the
visit time
of
a;
if
\mathrm{H}=\mathrm{K}^{\complement},
we will say
exit time
of
\mathrm{K}.

Suppose
S=(S_{n})_{n\in\mathds{Z}_{+}}
is the
\mathsf{NRW}
on
\mathrm{G}.
Often we will use
\mathds{P}{{}^{x}}\left(S_{n}=y\right)=\mathds{P}{}\left(S_{n}=y\mid S_{0}=x\right)
and
\mathds{E}{{}^{x}}\left(f(S_{n})\right)=\mathds{E}{}\left(f(S_{n})\mid S_{0}=x\right).
The
Green’s function
“of the network
\mathrm{G}
” is, by definition
G(x,y)=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathds{Z}_{+}}\mathds{P}{{}^{x}}\left(S_{n}=y\right)=\mathds{E}{{}^{x}}\left(\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathds{1}_{\{S_{n}=y\}}\right),
for all
(x,y)\in\mathrm{V}^{2}.
Similarly, define the
Green’s function restricted to leaving
\mathrm{V}_{0}
by
G_{\mathrm{V}_{0}}(x,y)=\sum\limits_{m=0}^{\infty}\mathds{P}{{}^{x}}\left(S_{m}=y,m<\tau_{\mathrm{V}_{0}}^{\complement}\right).

Suppose that
S_{0}=o
is some fixed vertex of the network and suppose that
N=(N_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a Poisson process in
\mathds{Z}_{+}
with unitary constant intensity which is independent of the process
S.
We define the
continuous time
\mathsf{NRW}
to be the process
\tilde{S}=\left(\tilde{S}_{t}\right)_{t\geq 0}
given by
\tilde{S}_{t}=S_{N_{t}}.
Define the
continuous time
\mathsf{NRW}
transition probability
by
q_{t}^{S}(o,v)=\mathds{P}{{}^{o}}\left(\tilde{S}_{t}=v\right),
for
(t,z)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathrm{V}.

Let
\gamma=(v_{j})_{0\leq j\leq m}
be a path of vertices. The
loop-erasure
of
\gamma,
denoted as
\mathsf{LE}(\gamma),
is the path obtained from
\gamma
by deleting cycles in chronological order. More specifically, it is defined as follows. Set
\gamma_{0}=\gamma.

(\mathsf{LE})

Suppose we are at stage
k
and we receive a path
\gamma_{k-1}=\left({u}_{1},\ldots,{u}_{m_{k}}\right).
If no vertex of this path is repeated, then return
\mathsf{LE}(\gamma)=\gamma_{k-1}.
Else, consider
\tau_{k}
to be the
first index

j
such that the vertex
u_{j}
appears twice in
\gamma_{k-1}.
Then, let
\sigma_{k}
to be the
last index

j
such that
u_{j}=u_{\tau_{k}},
consider now the path
\gamma_{k}=\left({u}_{1},\ldots,{u}_{\tau_{k}},{u}_{\sigma_{k}+1},\ldots,{u}_{m_{k}}\right).
Repeat.

A network
\Gamma
is
transitive
if for every pair of vertices
x
and
y
there exists a network automorphism
\varphi
of
\Gamma
such that
\varphi(x)=y.

Let
\mathrm{A}
be a subset of vertices. The
interior
of
\mathrm{A}
is the set of all vertices of
\mathrm{A}
whose all neighbours are vertices of
\mathrm{A}
as well; we denote it with
\mathring{\mathrm{A}}.
The
closure
of
\mathrm{A},
denoted
\overline{\mathrm{A}},
is the set of all vertices of
\mathrm{A}
or that are adjacent to
\mathrm{A}.
The
(exterior vertex) boundary
of
\mathrm{A},
denoted
\partial\mathrm{A},
is the set of all vertices
outside

\mathrm{A}
that are adjacent to
\mathrm{A}.

Let
\mathrm{G}
be a network with vertex set
\mathrm{V}.
A
harmonic
function on the network
\mathrm{G}
is any function
h:\mathrm{V}\to\mathds{R}
such that
h(x)=\sum\limits_{y\sim x}p(x,y)h(y).

Let
(\mathrm{G},\mu)
be a network with vertex set
\mathrm{V}.
A function
f:\mathrm{V}\to\mathds{R}
is said to be a
Dirichlet
function if
\mathscr{D}\left(f\right)=\sum\limits_{x,y}\mu(x,y)(f(x)-f(y))^{2}<\infty.
The finite or infinite number
\mathscr{D}\left(f\right)
is known as
Dirichlet energy
of
f.

1.2
Wilson’s algorithm

David Wilson published his algorithm in
[
Wil96
]
. (This algorithm is for finite networks.)

(\mathsf{WA})

Order the vertices and set
t_{0}
to be the tree consisting solely of the first vertex. Having defined
t_{k-1},
start an independent
\mathsf{NRW}
from the first vertex not in
t_{k-1}
and run this random walk until it hits
t_{k-1},
consider the
\mathsf{LE}
of this path and call
t_{k}
the tree obtained as the union of this
\mathsf{LE}
-path with
t_{k-1}.
When all vertices have been searched, return
\mathfrak{T}
the final tree constructed.

It is known that
\mathfrak{T}
follows the
\mathsf{UST}
law of the given (finite) network. A typical way of using Wilson’s algorithm is via a more intricate structure; while Wilson introduced this structure, a more detailed exposition can be found in
[
Bar16
]
. We refer to this version of Wilson’s algorithm as “Wilson’s algorithm with stacks.” See also
[
LP16
]
, Ch. 4. With the definitions of these references, we have the following result.

Proposition ( 1.1
)

Assume
(\mathrm{G},\mu)
is a finite network. Fix a root
r\in\mathrm{V}.
With probability one, there are finitely many cycles that can ever be popped. Any two ordering of the searches of the vertices in
\mathrm{V}\setminus\{r\}
will pop all these cycles and the popping procedure will leave the same visible graph for the two orderings. This visible graph sitting on top of the stacks is a spanning tree and the distribution of this random tree is that of the
\mathsf{UST}
of the given network.

Observe that Wilson’s algorithm run with stacks on a finite graph produces always the same tree provided the root is fixed in advance.

We mention now the usual way to construct
\mathsf{WSF}
on an infinite network
(\mathrm{G},\mu).
Consider an exhaustion
\mathrm{V}_{q}
of the vertex set and denote by
(\mathrm{G}_{q},\mu)
the network induced on
\mathrm{V}_{q}
with its boundary
\partial\mathrm{V}_{q}
wired into a vertex. Run Wilson’s algorithm with stacks taking
\partial\mathrm{V}_{q}
as root. This produces a random tree
\mathfrak{T}_{q}.
Then,
\mathfrak{T}_{q}\xrightarrow[q\to\infty]{\mathrm{weakly}}\mathfrak{F},
where
\mathfrak{F}
has distribution
\mathsf{WSF},
see
[
LP16
]
, sect. 10.1. There exists an alternative more dynamic (it provides a.s. convergence as opposed to weakly) method to sample the
\mathsf{WSF}
-distributed random object, which was first introduced in
[
BLPS01
]
.

Suppose
\mathrm{G}
is any network. Denote by
\mathrm{V}
the set of vertices of
\mathrm{G}
and by
\mathrm{E}
that of edges. Suppose
\xi:\mathds{N}\to\mathrm{V}
is a bijection (an “ordering” of the vertex set). Let
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathds{P})
be a probability space in which is possible to define an independent family
(S^{v})_{v\in\mathrm{V}}
of
\mathsf{NRW}
s of this network, such that
S^{v}
is a random walk started at
v.
Define inductively the following random spanning subgraphs of
\mathrm{G}:

Start.

L_{1}^{\xi}=\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\xi}=\mathsf{LE}\left(S^{\xi(1)}_{m}\right)_{m\in\mathds{Z}_{+}}.

Inductive step.

Having defined
L_{k}^{\xi}
and
\mathfrak{F}_{k}^{\xi},
define
L_{k+1}^{\xi}=\mathsf{LE}\left(S^{\xi(k+1)}_{m}\right)_{m=0,\ldots,\tau_{k}}
and
\mathfrak{F}_{k+1}=\mathfrak{F}_{k}\cup L_{k+1}^{\xi},
where
\tau_{k}=\tau_{\mathfrak{F}_{k}^{\xi}}\left(S^{\xi(k+1)}\right),
that is,
\tau_{k}
is the hitting time of the forest currently present
\mathfrak{F}_{k}^{\xi}
by the random walk
S^{\xi(k+1)}.

Consider finally the random spanning subgraph of
\mathrm{G}:

\mathfrak{F}^{\xi}=\bigcup\limits_{k\in\mathds{N}}\mathfrak{F}_{k}^{\xi}.
What Wilson’s algorithm rooted at infinity
[
BLPS01
, Theorem 5.1]
states is that
\mathfrak{F}^{\xi}\sim\mathsf{WSF}_{\mathrm{G}},
in other words, the distribution of
\mathfrak{F}^{\xi}
is that of the wired spanning forest of the network
\mathrm{G},
and this happens independently of the choice of ordering
\xi.

1.3
Definition of
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)
and summary of this paper

In this paper we will study
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)=(\mathds{Z}^{d+1},\mu),
where
\mu
is given by

(1.2)

\mu((n,x),(n^{\prime},x^{\prime}))=e^{\lambda\max(n,n^{\prime})},\quad\text{ for all }(n,x)\sim(n^{\prime},x^{\prime}).

It can easily be seen that this assignment of conductances makes the underlying
\mathsf{NRW}
to have a uniform drift to the right of the first axis. We will then investigate the main basic properties of the forest. In the remainder of this section we provide an overview of the results of this paper.

In sect.
2
we will prove the following theorem

Theorem
.

(Theorem (
2.22
))

There exist four constants
c_{i}
(
i=1,\ldots,4
) such that for all vertices
z=(n,x)\neq 0
of the network
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda),

\textstyle G(0,z)\leq c_{1}\begin{cases}e^{-c_{2}\left\lVert z\right\rVert}&\text{ if }\left\lVert x\right\rVert>n,\\
e^{-c_{2}\frac{\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}}{n}}\left\lVert z\right\rVert^{-\frac{d}{2}}&\text{ if }\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq n,\end{cases}

and

\textstyle G(0,z)\geq c_{3}\begin{cases}e^{-c_{4}\left\lVert z\right\rVert}&\text{ if }\left\lVert x\right\rVert>n,\\
e^{-c_{4}\frac{\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}}{n}}\left\lVert z\right\rVert^{-\frac{d}{2}}&\text{ if }\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq n,\end{cases}

To prove this bounds we will employ a decomposition of the Green’s function using the continuous-time
\mathsf{NRW}
and then use several “well-known” estimates on the latter.

In sect.
3
we will establish is the following.

Theorem
.

(Theorem (
3.8
))

On
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda),
we have
\mathsf{WSF}=\mathsf{FSF}.

This is done using the Liouville property and a coupling argument. This theorem then shows that
\mathsf{USF}
is well defined on
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda).
Next, using Fourier inversion theorem together with Plancherel’s theorem, we will prove the following result, which we call “bubble condition.”

Theorem
.

(Theorem (
3.10
))

The Green’s function
z\mapsto G(0,z)
of
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)
belongs to
\mathscr{L}^{2}
if and only if
d\geq 3.

Shortly after, as a corollary of the bubble condition, we will also show that
\mathsf{USF}
consists of a single tree when
d=1,2,
and it consists of infinitely many infinite trees for
d\geq 3.

Theorem
.

(Theorem (
3.9
))

In
d=1,2,

\mathsf{WSF}
of
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)
is a.s. one tree; in
d\geq 3,
there are a.s. infinitely many trees.

In sect.
4
we estimate the frequency at which two independent
\mathsf{NRW}
of
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)
will cross each other. We will prove the following result

Theorem
.

(Theorems (
4.1
) and (
4.2
), and Corollary (
4.15.2
))

Let
S
and
S^{\prime}
be two independent network random walks in
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)
started at
0.

(a)

If
d=1,
then a.s. there exists infinitely many
n
such that
S_{n}=S_{n}^{\prime}=0.

(b)

If
d=2,
then a.s. there exist infinitely many pairs
(n,n^{\prime})
such that
S_{n}=S_{n^{\prime}}^{\prime}.

(c)

If
d\geq 3,
there there exists a positive probability that for no pair
(n,n^{\prime}),

S_{n}=S_{n^{\prime}}^{\prime}.

In fact, in
d=2,
we will estimate the
probability
that they will cross is roughly
\log n
if they walk
n
steps (a precise formulation of this is Theorem (
4.15
)). The proofs of dimensions
d\neq 2
are relatively easy. The proof of dimension
d=2
is rather involved. We explain the gist of the idea now. We will consider a paraboloid given by the vertices
z=(n,x)\in\mathds{Z}_{+}\times\mathds{Z}^{d}
such that
\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq n^{2}
and inside each of these, we will consider cylinders (
4.7
) of appropriate dimensions. We will show that for certain regions (
4.10
), the
\mathsf{NRW}
started in the boundary of these regions will not go back to the testing cylinder (except with a small probability that can be controlled) and therefore, the Green’s function virtually does not change value when we restrict it to leave these regions. We then employ a second-moment method to estimate the aforementioned probability. The calculations are quite tight and require a careful set up.

In sect.
5
, we prove

Theorem
.

(Theorems (
5.1
) and (
5.15
) and (
5.16
))

In
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda),
a.s. every tree in
\mathsf{USF}
has one end.

The proofs of dimensions
d=1,

d=2
and
d\geq 3
need to be done separately. In
d=1
we use planar duality and the rather easy-to-establish fact that
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)
is essentially its own dual; the idea is a makeover of the proof of the same result for the graph
\mathds{Z}^{2}.
In
d\geq 3,
we followed closely the proof of one-endedness for the graphs
\mathds{Z}^{d}
given in
[
LP16
]
. We made adaptation according to our conveniences but the underlying idea is that should a tree have two ends, there will be strong conductivity in the boundary of boxes. The proof of case
d=2
is perhaps the most innovative contribution of this paper and was introduced from scratch. The first property we establish is that Wilson’s algorithm rooted at infinity has some stochastic-stability (the law remains unaltered) if we fix the first steps and reorder future vertices (a stronger result for finite-graphs is proposition (
1.1
)). This will show that probabilities of future events depending on the past do not actually depend of the past. The next step is to show that it suffices to prove that the component of zero is one-ended. To this aim, we employ Wilson’s algorithm rooted at infinity and take the first step of the algorithm to be the origin. Call
L_{0}
this part of the forest. If the component at zero had at least two ends, then it would cross the boundary of each cylinder (centred at the origin) in at least two vertices. The aforementioned invariance of the law from reordering future searches having fixed the past will allow us to fix
L_{0}
and then prove that, in a given a large cylinder
\mathrm{C}=\left\{|n|\leq r,\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq r\right\},
most vertices on
\partial\mathrm{C}
have infinitesimal probability to connect to zero, this will reduce the work to study a part of the left base of
\mathrm{C},
namely
\mathrm{C}_{r,0}=\left\{n=-r,\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq r^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\}.
We will then take a sparse subset here
(\mathrm{C}_{r,0}^{\prime})
and construct the branches from its vertices. The subset
\mathrm{C}_{r,0}^{\prime}
needs to be sparse enough so that the probability that the
\mathsf{NRW}
started from its vertices create a second end from the origin is small. If now
z
is any vertex in
\mathrm{C}_{r,0}\setminus\mathrm{C}_{r,0}^{\prime},
then we will show that the
\mathsf{NRW}
started at
z
has an overwhelming probability to hit the branch created by some
z^{\prime}\in\mathrm{C}_{r,0}^{\prime}
and since this branch does not create a second end from the origin,
z
will also not create a second end. The proof will conclude by gluing together all the estimates. Admittedly, the proof is elaborate and to ease the reading, we have provided well-detailed arguments.

In sect.
6
we will show

Theorem
.

(Theorems (
6.2
) and (
6.5
))

There exists a metric
\eta(z)=\max\left(|n|^{\frac{1}{2}},\left\lVert x\right\rVert\right)
such

\mathds{P}{}\left(z\text{ and }z^{\prime}\text{ are in the same }\mathsf{USF}\text{-component}\right)\asymp\eta(z-z^{\prime})^{-(d-2)}.

To prove this, we follow similar techniques as those established in
[
BKPS04
]
. In a nutshell, we will need to investigate bounds of the convolution
\displaystyle\sum_{z^{\prime\prime}\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}}G(z,z^{\prime\prime})G(z^{\prime},z^{\prime\prime}).

In sect.
7
we will establish the following

Theorem
.

(Theorem (
7.13
))

Let
D(z,z^{\prime})
be the minimal number of
\mathds{Z}^{d+1}
-edges outside the
\mathsf{USF}
-forest that connects the tree at
z
and that at
z^{\prime}.
Almost surely,
\displaystyle\max_{z,z^{\prime}}D(z,z^{\prime})=\left\lceil\dfrac{d-2}{4}\right\rceil.

The proof of this is done by adaptation of most of the methods of
[
BKPS04
]
to the network
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda).

1.3.1
Biased random walk

We make special mention of an article appearing in the arXiv.org in 2018. In
[
SSS+18
]
Zhan Shi, Vladas Sidoravicius, He Song, Longmin Wang, Kainan Xiang investigated properties of
\mathsf{USF}
for a random walk they called “biased random walk.” They studied the network
\mathds{Z}^{d}
with assignment of conductances
c_{\lambda}(e)=\lambda^{-|e|},
where
|e|
is the graph-distance from the origin to the edge
e
and
\lambda\in(0,1)
is a fixed parameter. The network considered in this paper and
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)
both have conductances which are far from uniform. However, the two assignments of conduces give rise to very different random walks, and very different properties of the uniform spanning forest.

In
[
SSS+18
]
the
\mathsf{NRW}
has a drift away from the origin, while in our case it has a uniform drift towards the right. Our assignments of conductances make the network transitive (indeed, translation-invariant), while theirs is not. They exploited this fact by noticing that the axes play a special rôle. This also leads to quite different properties of the uniform spanning forest. For example, the
\mathsf{USF}
in
[
SSS+18
]
has
2^{d}
trees if
d=2,3,
while in our case this number is one. Their methods and ours are also different: they used “spectral radius” and “speed” of the random walk, while we will not mention these. Another display of the difference in nature of the results in their paper when compared to this paper is when they count the number of intersections of random walk paths. They estimated these intersections by using a local limit theorem. Typically, local limit theorems have error terms of a polynomial decay in
\left\lVert z\right\rVert.
We will show that the Green’s function in
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)
has exponential decay in some directions. Thus, a local limit theorem did not prove useful for us. Finally, another contrast in the two assignment of conductances. The fact that
c_{\lambda}(e)
is bounded from below allowed them to prove one-endedness using the isoperimetric condition
[
LP16
, Theorem 10.43]
. Our conductances decay exponentially, and so we cannot use the isoperimetric condition for one-endedness (or any other known technique so far); a new technique was derived from scratch to prove it.

2
Green’s function bounds

2.1
Basic statistics of
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)

We will often write
z\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}=\mathds{Z}\times\mathds{Z}^{d}
as
z=(n,{x}_{1},\ldots,{x}_{d})=(n,x)
and we will refer to
n
as the “drifted coordinate” or the “zeroth coordinate.” A unit vector in
\mathds{Z}^{d+1}
has one coordinate equal to 1 or -1 and all the rest equal to zero; for simplicity,
u
and
u_{i}
will denote unit vectors in
\mathds{Z}^{d+1}
and
u_{i}
is such that its
i
th coordinate is non zero (
0\leq i\leq d
). The functions
\mathrm{pr}_{i}:\mathds{Z}^{d+1}\to\mathds{Z},
for
i=0,1,\ldots,d,
are the projections and are defined in the obvious way. We will call
\mathds{Z}u_{i}
the “
i
th factor” as well as “
i
th axis” (
i=0,\ldots,d
). Let
\lambda>0,
fixed throughout the rest of this document. Recall
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)
is the graph
\mathds{Z}^{d+1}
with conductances (
1.2
). From these conductances, one can get the transition kernel for network random walk. The probability that the next step should be
u\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1},
a unit vector with our conventions, is given by

(2.1)

\textstyle p(u)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}(2d+1+e^{\lambda})^{-1}&\text{ if }&u=(-1,0)\text{ or }u=u_{i},i=1,\ldots,d,\\
e^{\lambda}(2d+1+e^{\lambda})^{-1}&\text{ if }&u=(1,0).\end{array}\right.

The
\frac{1}{2}
-lazy version of the above transition density is
p^{L}(0)=\frac{1}{2},

p^{L}(u)=\frac{1}{2}p(u)
for unit
u.

Denote by
\zeta\cdot\varepsilon_{t}
the measure on
\mathds{Z}^{d}
such that it has total mass equal to
\zeta
at the vertex
t.
Notice that if
Y=\left({Y}^{(0)},\ldots,{Y}^{(d)}\right)
is a random element of
\mathds{Z}^{d+1}
with distribution (
2.1
), then (
i=1,\ldots,d
)

(2.2)

\textstyle Y^{(0)}\sim(2d+1+e^{\lambda})^{-1}\Big(\varepsilon_{-1}+2d\cdot\varepsilon_{0}+e^{\lambda}\cdot\varepsilon_{1}\Big),\quad Y^{(i)}\sim(2d+1+e^{\lambda})^{-1}\Big(\varepsilon_{-1}+(2d-1+e^{\lambda})\cdot\varepsilon_{0}+\varepsilon_{1}),

which immediately allows one to conclude that

(2.3)

\textstyle\mathds{E}{}\left(Y\right)=(a,0)=\left(\frac{e^{\lambda}-1}{2d+1+e^{\lambda}},0\right),\quad\mathds{C}\mathrm{ov}\left(Y\right)=\left[\begin{matrix}\sigma_{0}^{2}&0\\
0&\sigma^{2}I_{d}\end{matrix}\right]=\left[\begin{matrix}\frac{e^{\lambda}+1}{2d+1+e^{\lambda}}&0\\
0&\frac{2}{2d+1+e^{\lambda}}I_{d}\end{matrix}\right],

where
I_{d}
is the identity on
\mathds{R}^{d}.
From the Central Limit Theorem, it follows immediately that if
(Y_{n})
is a family of independent random elements following (
2.1
),

\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}(Y_{j}-(a,0))\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{\text{weakly}}\mathsf{Norm}\left(0;\left[\begin{matrix}\sigma_{0}^{2}&0\\
0&\sigma^{2}I_{d}\end{matrix}\right]\right).

The Fourier transform of
Y
is

(2.4)

\textstyle\textstyle\varphi_{Y}(h)=\mathds{E}{}\left(e^{i\left(h\middle|Y\right)}\right)=\frac{e^{\lambda}e^{ih_{0}}+e^{-ih_{0}}}{2d+1+e^{\lambda}}+\frac{2}{2d+1+e^{\lambda}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{d}\cos h_{j},

where
h=({h}_{0},\ldots,{h}_{d})\in[-\pi,\pi]^{d+1}.
Observe also
\varphi_{Y}(h)=1
if and only if
1=e^{ih_{0}}=\cos h_{j}
for
j=0,\ldots,d,
and this is the same as saying that
h_{0}=\ldots=h_{d}=0.

2.2
Continuous time network random walk

Recall the definition of the continuous time network random walk. In
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda),
we can write

(2.5)

\textstyle\tilde{S}_{t}=\left(\tilde{B}_{t},\tilde{X}_{t}\right),\quad(t\geq 0).

The following properties come from well-known facts regarding Poisson processes or are otherwise given a reference. Set
p=\mathds{P}{}\left(Y=u\right)=(2d+1+e^{\lambda})^{-1}.

(a)

\tilde{B}
and
\tilde{X}
are independent continuous-time Markov processes.

(b)

\tilde{X}_{t}=X_{M_{t}},
where
X
is a standard random walk in
\mathds{Z}^{d}
and
M=(M_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a Poisson process in
\mathds{Z}_{+}
with constant intensity equal to
2dp=\frac{2d}{2d+1+e^{\lambda}}.

(c)

\tilde{B}_{t}=B_{L_{t}},
where
B
is a biased random walk in
\mathds{Z}
with jumps to
\pm 1,

L=(L_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a Poisson process in
\mathds{Z}_{+}
with constant intensity equal to
\sigma_{0}^{2}=(1+e^{\lambda})p=\frac{1+e^{\lambda}}{2d+1+e^{\lambda}},
and the probability of
B
having a positive jump is
\frac{e^{\lambda}}{1+e^{\lambda}}.

(d)

Let
q^{X}
be the
transition probability
of
\tilde{X},
that is,
q^{X}(t,y)=q_{t}^{X}(y)=\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tilde{X}_{t}=y\right).
Then
[
Bar17
, Theorem 5.17]
provides upper bounds

(2.6)

\textstyle q^{X}(t,y)\leq\begin{cases}\frac{2}{d}\exp\left(-\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert^{2}}{2dpe^{2}t}\right)&\text{ if }\left\lVert y\right\rVert\leq 2dpet,\\
\frac{1}{2d}\exp\left(-2dpt-\left\lVert y\right\rVert\log\left(\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert}{2dpet}\right)\right)&\text{ if }\left\lVert y\right\rVert\geq 2dpet;\end{cases}

and lower bounds, valid for
\left\lVert y\right\rVert\geq 2dpt>0,

(2.7)

\textstyle q^{X}(t,y)\geq c\exp\left(-c^{\prime}\left\lVert y\right\rVert\left[1+\log\left(\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert}{2dpt}\right)\right]\right).

(e)

By Theorem 6.19 bearing in mind Definition 3.28, Corollary 3.30 and Definition 5.19 of
[
Bar17
]
there exists four constants
c_{i}>0
(
i=1,\ldots,4
) such that for all
(t,y)
for which
2dpt>\left\lVert y\right\rVert,

(2.8)

\textstyle\begin{split}q^{X}_{t}(y)&\leq c_{1}t^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{-c_{2}\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert^{2}}{t}},\\
q^{X}_{t}(y)&\geq c_{3}t^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{-c_{4}\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert^{2}}{t}}.\end{split}

(f)

There exists a pair of universal constants
c,c^{\prime}>0
such that for all
r,T\geq 1,

\mathds{P}{}\left(\sup\limits_{0\leq s\leq T}\left\lVert\tilde{X}_{s}\right\rVert\geq r\right)\leq ce^{-c^{\prime}\frac{r^{2}}{T}}.
This follows from Theorem 4.33 of
[
Bar17
]
applied to the standard random walk (so
\beta=2
), you will need to recall Definitions 4.14 and 4.18, and use Lemma 4.20, which holds with
\alpha=d
and
\beta=2
for the standard random walk on
\mathds{Z}^{d}.

We conclude this section with the following easy remark.

Remark ( 2.9
)

Let
c>0.

(a)

If
z=(n,x)
satisfies
|n|\leq c\left\lVert x\right\rVert,
then
\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert z\right\rVert\leq\sqrt{1+c^{2}}\left\lVert x\right\rVert.

(b)

If
z=(n,x)
satisfies
\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq c|n|,
then
|n|\leq\left\lVert z\right\rVert\leq\sqrt{1+c^{2}}|n|.

(c)

There exists
c^{\prime}>0
such that for all
z\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1},

e^{-c\left\lVert z\right\rVert}\leq c^{\prime}\left\lVert z\right\rVert^{-\frac{d}{2}}.

2.3
Some estimates of sums

We collect together here some estimates of sums which will be used later. All proofs can be found in
[
MD20
]
, sect. 2.3.

Proposition ( 2.10
)

Let
L\in\mathds{N}
and
f:[L,\infty)\to\mathds{R}_{+}^{*}
be a monotone function such for a pair
a,b>0,
we have
af(j+1)\leq f(j)\leq bf(j+1)
for integers
j\geq L.
Let
r>L+1.
Then,
\sum\limits_{L\leq\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq r}f(\left\lVert x\right\rVert)\asymp\int\limits_{L\leq\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq r}dx\ f(\left\lVert x\right\rVert)=\sigma_{d}\int\limits_{L}^{r}dt\ f(t)t^{d-1},
where
\sigma_{d}
is the “surface area” of the
d
-dimensional sphere and any implicit constant may depend solely on dimension and on the pair
(a,b).

Proposition ( 2.11
)

Let
a\geq 0,

b,\gamma>0
and
r>2.

(a)

If
a>0,

\sum\limits_{\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq r}\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{a}e^{-\gamma\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{b}}\asymp\min\left(r^{b},\gamma^{-1}\right)^{\frac{a+d}{b}}.

(b)

If
a=0,

\sum\limits_{0<\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq r}e^{-\gamma\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{b}}\asymp\min\left(r^{b},\gamma^{-1}\right)^{\frac{d}{b}};
thus, if
r^{-b}\leq\gamma\leq 1,

\sum\limits_{\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq r}e^{-\gamma\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{b}}\asymp\gamma^{-\frac{d}{b}}.

(Any implicit constant may depend on
a,b
and dimension but not on
\gamma
or on
r.
)

Proposition ( 2.12
)

Let
a,b>0.
There exists a pair of constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that for all
t\in\mathds{Z}^{d}
and all
m\in\mathds{N},

(a)

if
\left\lVert t\right\rVert>bm,

e^{-a\left\lVert t\right\rVert}\leq\sum\limits_{\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq bm}e^{-a\left(\frac{\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}}{m}+\left\lVert x-t\right\rVert\right)}\leq c_{1}e^{-c_{2}\left\lVert t\right\rVert};

(b)

if
\left\lVert t\right\rVert\leq bm,

e^{-a\frac{\left\lVert t\right\rVert^{2}}{m}}\leq\sum\limits_{\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq bm}e^{-a\left(\frac{\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}}{m}+\left\lVert x-t\right\rVert\right)}\leq c_{1}e^{-c_{2}\frac{\left\lVert t\right\rVert^{2}}{m}}.

Proposition ( 2.13
)

For every
\alpha>1
and all integers
m\geq 2,

\frac{1}{\alpha-1}m^{-(\alpha-1)}\leq\sum\limits_{k\geq m}k^{-\alpha}\leq\frac{2^{\alpha-1}}{\alpha-1}m^{-(\alpha-1)}.

Proposition ( 2.14
)

For every
a\in\mathds{R}
and
b\geq 1,
there exists a constant
c=c(a,b)>0
such that for all
\gamma>1,

\sum\limits_{1\leq j\leq b\gamma}j^{a}e^{-\frac{\gamma}{j}}\leq c\gamma^{a+1}.

Proposition ( 2.15
)

There exists a universal constant
c>0
such that for all systems
(a,p,q,r,t)
with
r>2,

a,p,q>0
and
t\in\mathds{Z}^{d},

\textstyle\sum\limits_{\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq r}e^{-a\left(\frac{\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}}{p}+\frac{\left\lVert x-t\right\rVert^{2}}{q}\right)}\leq\begin{cases}\displaystyle c\min\left(r^{2},\frac{p}{a}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}e^{-\frac{a}{4}\frac{\left\lVert t\right\rVert^{2}}{q}}&\text{ if }\left\lVert t\right\rVert>2r\\
\displaystyle c\left[\min\left(r^{2},\frac{p}{a}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}e^{-\frac{a}{4}\frac{\left\lVert t\right\rVert^{2}}{q}}+\min\left(\frac{\left\lVert t\right\rVert^{2}}{4},\frac{q}{a}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}e^{-\frac{a}{4}\frac{\left\lVert t\right\rVert^{2}}{p}}\right]&\text{ if }\left\lVert t\right\rVert\leq 2r.\end{cases}

2.4
Some properties of (network) random walk

These properties are well-known and are included for completeness of the exposition. We denote by
S=(S_{n})
the network random walk of a network
\Gamma
and
G
will denote its Green’s function.

Proposition ( 2.16
)

Let
\Gamma
be any network. For any two vertices
o
and
v
of
\Gamma,

G(o,v)=\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}dt\ q_{t}^{S}(o,v).
If
\Gamma
is transitive and
v
is any vertex,
G(o,v)=G(o,o)\mathds{P}{{}^{o}}\left(\tau_{v}<\infty\right),
where
\tau_{v}=\tau_{v}\left(\tilde{S}\right)=\inf\left\{t\geq 0;\ \tilde{S}_{t}=v\right\}.

Proposition ( 2.17
)

Consider a random walk
S
on
\mathds{Z}
with step-lengths distributed as
q\cdot\varepsilon_{-1}+r\cdot\varepsilon_{0}+p\cdot\varepsilon_{1}.
Assume
1>p+q>p>q>0.
Consider the events
\mathrm{C}_{u}=\{\text{for exactly one }n,S_{n}=u\}=\bigcup\limits_{n\in\mathds{Z}_{+}}\bigcap\limits_{m\neq n}\{S_{n}=u,S_{m}\neq u\}
and
\mathrm{E}_{u}=\{\text{for at least one }n,S_{n}=u\}=\bigcup\limits_{n\in\mathds{Z}_{+}}\{S_{n}=u\}.
Then, for
u\geq 0
and
v<0,

(a)

\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\mathrm{C}_{u}\right)=\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{0}^{+}=\infty\right)=p-q,

(b)

\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\bigcup\limits_{u=0}^{n^{2}}\mathrm{C}_{u}\right)\geq 1-(1-(p-q))^{n}-n\left(\frac{q}{p}\right)^{n},

(c)

\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\mathrm{E}_{v}\right)=\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)^{v}.

Proof.

Items (a) and (c) are well-known. For convenience of the reader, we provide details of item (b).

Consider
\mathrm{D}_{u}
the complement of
\mathrm{C}_{u}.
It suffices to show
\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\bigcap\limits_{u=0}^{n}\mathrm{D}_{un}\right)\leq(1-(p-q))^{n}+n\left(\frac{q}{p}\right)^{n}.
With respect to
\mathds{P}^{0},
the events
\mathrm{D}_{u}
(
u\geq 0
) are a.s. the events where the state
u
is visited twice or more. Introduce
\theta_{t}
defined by “the time of first return to
t
”:
\theta_{t}=\inf\{k>\tau_{t}\mid S_{k}=t\}\quad(t\in\mathds{Z}_{+}).
It is clear that
(\tau_{un})_{u=1,\ldots,n}
is strictly increasing, that
\tau_{t}<\theta_{t}
for all
t\in\mathds{Z}_{+},
and also that the complement of the event
\bigcap\limits_{j=1}^{n}\{\theta_{(j-1)n}<\tau_{jn}\}
is contained in the event
\bigcup\limits_{j=1}^{n}\{\tau_{jn}<\theta_{(j-1)n}\}.
Therefore,
\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\bigcap\limits_{u=0}^{n}\mathrm{D}_{un}\right)\leq\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\theta_{0}<\tau_{n}<\theta_{n}<\ldots<\tau_{n^{2}}<\theta_{n^{2}}<\infty\right)+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{jn}<\theta_{(j-1)n}<\infty\right).
By the strong Markov property,
\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{jn}<\theta_{(j-1)n}<\infty\right)=\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\mathrm{E}_{-n}\right)=\left(\frac{q}{p}\right)^{n}.
Set
\mathscr{E}_{n}=\mathscr{F}_{\tau_{n}}^{S}=\sigma(S_{k};0\leq k\leq\tau_{n})
and

\textstyle\gamma_{k}\mathop{=}\limits^{\mathrm{def.}}\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\theta_{0}<\tau_{n}<\theta_{n}<\ldots<\tau_{kn}<\theta_{kn}<\infty\right).

Observe that the strong Markov property implies

\displaystyle\gamma_{n}

\displaystyle=\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\theta_{0}<\tau_{n}<\theta_{n}<\ldots<\tau_{n^{2}}<\theta_{n^{2}}<\infty\right)

\displaystyle=\mathds{E}{{}^{0}}\left(\mathds{1}_{\{\theta_{0}<\tau_{n}<\infty\}}\mathds{E}{{}^{0}}\left(\mathds{1}_{\{\theta_{n}<\ldots<\tau_{n^{2}}<\theta_{n^{2}}<\infty\}}\middle|\ \mathscr{E}_{n}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\theta_{0}<\tau_{n}<\infty\right)\gamma_{n-1}\leq\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{0}^{+}<\infty\right)\gamma_{n-1},

which leads at once to
\gamma_{n}\leq(1-(p-q))^{n}
since
\gamma_{1}\leq 1-(p-q).

∎

2.5
A hitting time for the continuous time network random walk

Again, we compile some properties of certain random times associated with the continuous time random walk (
2.5
). These properties are arguably well-known and are included for completeness of the exposition. In what follows, we use (
2.3
)
p=(2d+1+e^{\lambda})^{-1},

a=(e^{\lambda}-1)p
and
\sigma_{0}^{2}=(e^{\lambda}+1)p.

Introduce

\textstyle\tau_{n}^{0}=\inf\left\{t\geq 0;\tilde{B}_{t}=n\right\}\quad(n\in\mathds{N}).

Then,
\tau_{n}^{0}
is an almost surely finite stopping time, it is independent of the process
\tilde{X}
and by successive applications of the strong Markov property at the times
\tau_{j}^{0}
(
1\leq j\leq n-1
), it is immediately seen that the law of
\tau_{n}^{0}
is the
n
th convolution power of the law of
\tau_{1}^{0}.
Let
\zeta
denote the Laplace transform of
\tau_{1}^{0},
that is,
\zeta(t)=\mathds{E}{{}^{0}}\left(e^{t\tau_{1}^{0}}\right).
The following proposition follows from elementary results and its proof is therefore omitted, see
[
MD20
]
, propositions (2.5.1) and (2.5.2).

Proposition ( 2.18
)

(a)

We have
\mathds{E}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{1}^{0}\right)=\frac{1}{a}.

(b)

Then
\zeta(t)<\infty
for
t<(e^{\frac{\lambda}{2}}-1)^{2}p
and
\zeta(t)=\infty
for
t>(e^{\frac{\lambda}{2}}-1)^{2}p.
We have the following bounds

\textstyle\zeta(t)\leq\begin{cases}\frac{2e^{\lambda}p}{2e^{\lambda}p-t}&\text{ for }-\infty<t\leq 0,\\
\frac{2e^{\lambda}p}{\sigma_{0}^{2}-t}&\text{ for }0<t<(e^{\frac{\lambda}{2}}-1)^{2}p.\end{cases}

Proposition ( 2.19
)

(a)

For
\alpha\leq\frac{n}{2e^{\lambda}p},

\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{n}^{0}\leq\alpha\right)\leq\exp\left(-2e^{\lambda}p\alpha+n\log\left(\frac{2e^{\lambda+1}p\alpha}{n}\right)\right).

(b)

For
0<\beta<\frac{1}{2e^{\lambda}p}
there exists a constant
u(\beta)>0
such that for all
n,

\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{n}^{0}\leq\beta n\right)\leq e^{-u(\beta)n}.

(c)

There exists two constants
T_{0},K_{0}>0
such that
\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{n}^{0}\geq\alpha\right)\leq e^{-T_{0}(\alpha-\frac{n}{a})}
valid for
\alpha\geq(K_{0}+\frac{1}{a})n.

Proof.

The bound in (c) is a Corollary of Theorem 1 and Lemma 5, in Chapter III of
[
Pet75
]
, and observe we can apply these results since the previous proposition shows the Laplace transform of
\tau_{1}^{0}
to be absolutely convergent on an interval around zero. We now prove the bound for
\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{n}^{0}\leq\alpha\right).
Markov’s inequality shows, for
t<0,

\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{n}^{0}\leq\alpha\right)=\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(e^{t\tau_{n}^{0}}\geq e^{t\alpha}\right)\leq e^{-t\alpha}\zeta(t)^{n},
where
\zeta
is the Laplace transform of
\tau_{1}^{0}.
From the previous proposition, we know
\zeta(t)\leq\frac{2e^{\lambda}p}{2e^{\lambda}p-t}
for
t\leq 0,
and so
\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{n}^{0}\leq\alpha\right)\leq e^{-t\alpha+n\log\left(\frac{2e^{\lambda}p}{2e^{\lambda}p-t}\right)}
for
t<0.
Minimising gives the minimiser
t=2e^{\lambda}p-\frac{n}{\alpha},
which is valid only when
t<0,
that is, only when
\alpha<\frac{n}{2e^{\lambda}p},
the bound follows readily upon evaluation at the minimiser. When
\alpha=\frac{n}{2e^{\lambda}p},
we reach the trivial bound
\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{n}^{0}\leq\alpha\right)\leq 1,
and so it is also valid in this case. Finally, the substitution
\alpha=\beta n
implies
\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{n}^{0}\leq\beta n\right)\leq e^{-u(\beta)n},
where
u(\beta)=2e^{\lambda}p\beta-\log(2e^{\lambda+1}p\beta).
A standard minimisation shows
u(\beta)>u\left(\frac{1}{2e^{\lambda}p}\right)=0
for
0<\beta<\frac{1}{2e^{\lambda}p}.

∎

Proposition ( 2.20
)

Recall from (
2.16
) the definition of
\tau_{z}
for
z=(n,x)\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}.
There exists
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that, for all
x\in\mathds{Z}^{d},

\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{(0,x)}<\infty\right)\leq c_{1}e^{-c_{2}\left\lVert x\right\rVert}.

Proof.

Let
r=r_{x}
be the smallest integer
k\geq\frac{\left\lVert x\right\rVert}{2}.
Then
\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{(0,x)}<\infty\right)\leq\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{(0,x)}\leq\tau_{r}^{0}\leq cr\right)+\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{(0,x)}\leq\tau_{r}^{0},\tau_{r}^{0}>cr\right)+\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{r}^{0}<\tau_{(0,x)}<\infty\right),
where
c\geq K_{0}+\frac{1}{a}
is chosen so that
cr_{x}\geq(K_{0}+\frac{1}{a})r_{x}
for all
x\in\mathds{Z}^{d},

x\neq 0
and
K_{0}
is as in (
2.19
). Then, recalling
\tilde{S}=\left(\tilde{B},\tilde{X}\right)
and item (
f
) in §
2.2
,

\textstyle\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{(0,x)}\leq\tau_{r}^{0}\leq cr\right)\leq\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\sup\limits_{0\leq s\leq cr}\left\lVert\tilde{X}_{s}\right\rVert\geq\left\lVert x\right\rVert\right)\leq c^{\prime}e^{-c^{\prime\prime}\frac{\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}}{c\left\lVert x\right\rVert}}=c^{\prime}e^{-\frac{c^{\prime\prime}}{c}\left\lVert x\right\rVert},

for two constants
c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime}>0.
From (
2.19
),
\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{(0,x)}\leq\tau_{r}^{0},\tau_{r}^{0}>cr\right)\leq e^{-c^{\prime}r},
for
c^{\prime}=T_{0}(c-\frac{1}{a}).
Applying the strong Markov property at the time
\tau_{r}^{0},
we reach that

\displaystyle\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{r}^{0}<\tau_{(0,x)}<\infty\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{E}{{}^{0}}\left(\mathds{P}{{}^{\tilde{S}_{\tau_{r}^{0}}}}\left(\tau_{(0,x)}<\infty\right)\right)\leq\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tilde{B}\text{ ever reaches }-r\right)

\displaystyle=\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(B\text{ ever reaches }-r\right)\leq e^{-c^{\prime}\left\lVert x\right\rVert},

for some
c^{\prime}>0.

∎

Proposition ( 2.21
)

Recall from (
2.16
) the definition of
\tau_{z}
for
z\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}.
If
z=(n,x),
we have
G(0,z)=G(0,0)\sum\limits_{y\in\mathds{Z}^{d}}\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{(0,x-y)}<\infty\right)\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}q^{X}_{t}(y)\mathds{P}{}\left(\tau_{n}^{0}\in dt\right).

Proof.

By (
2.16
), we have
G(0,z)=G(0,0)\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{z}<\infty\right).
Apply the strong Markov property at the time
\tau_{n}^{0}
and use that
\tau_{n}^{0}
and
\tilde{X}
are mutually independent,

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{z}<\infty\right)

\displaystyle\textstyle=\mathds{E}{{}^{0}}\left(\mathds{P}{{}^{\tilde{S}_{\tau_{n}^{0}}}}\left(\tau_{z}<\infty\right)\right)=\sum\limits_{y\in\mathds{Z}^{d}}\mathds{P}{{}^{(n,y)}}\left(\tau_{(n,x)}<\infty\right)\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tilde{S}_{\tau_{n}^{0}}=(n,y)\right)

\displaystyle\textstyle=\sum\limits_{y\in\mathds{Z}^{d}}\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{(0,x-y)}<\infty\right)\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tilde{X}_{t}=y\right)\mathds{P}{}\left(\tau_{n}^{0}\in dt\right).

Since
q^{X}_{t}(y)=\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tilde{X}_{t}=y\right),
we reach the desired conclusion.
∎

2.6
Statement and proof of the Theorem

Consider the Green’s function
G
of
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda).
By transience, the probability
p_{0}=\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{0}<\infty\right)
that the network random walk started at zero returns to zero is (strictly) less than one. Thus, the number of visits to zero, started at zero, has finite expectation since this number follows a geometric law with probability of success
p_{0},
in other words,
G(0,0)
is some finite number, depending only on
\lambda
(as such, regarded as universal). The next theorem deals with the bounds
G(0,z)
for
z\neq 0.

Theorem ( 2.22
)

There exist four constants
c_{i}
(
i=1,\ldots,4
) such that for all vertices
z=(n,x)\neq 0
of the network
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda),

\textstyle G(0,z)\leq c_{1}\begin{cases}e^{-c_{2}\left\lVert z\right\rVert}&\text{ if }\left\lVert x\right\rVert>n,\\
e^{-c_{2}\frac{\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}}{n}}\left\lVert z\right\rVert^{-\frac{d}{2}}&\text{ if }\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq n,\end{cases}

and

\textstyle G(0,z)\geq c_{3}\begin{cases}e^{-c_{4}\left\lVert z\right\rVert}&\text{ if }\left\lVert x\right\rVert>n,\\
e^{-c_{4}\frac{\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}}{n}}\left\lVert z\right\rVert^{-\frac{d}{2}}&\text{ if }\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq n,\end{cases}

Proof.

The proof is rather lengthy and we divide it into several steps.

Proof of the upper bounds.

We begin with proving the upper bound when
z\in\mathds{Z}_{-}\times\mathds{Z}^{d}.
We start with the following simple observation: bearing in mind Remark (
2.9
), if
z=(n,x)\in\mathds{Z}_{-}\times\mathds{Z}^{d},
the upper bound will be established if we prove
G(0,z)\leq ce^{-c^{\prime}\max(|n|,\left\lVert x\right\rVert)}
for a pair of constants
c,c^{\prime}>0
independent of
z.

We analyse separately the cases
\left\lVert x\right\rVert<|n|
and
\left\lVert x\right\rVert\geq|n|.

Assume first
z\in\mathds{Z}_{-}\times\mathds{Z}^{d}
is such that
\left\lVert x\right\rVert<|n|,
notice
n\leq 0.
We claim there exists a pair of constants
c,c^{\prime}>0
(independent of
z=(n,x)
) such that
G(0,z)\leq ce^{-c^{\prime}|n|}.
To see this, recall (
2.16
) and and (
2.17
), so that

\textstyle G(0,z)=G(0,0)\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{z}<\infty\right)\leq G(0,0)\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(B\text{ ever reaches level }n\right)=G(0,0)e^{c^{\prime}n},

where
\tau_{z}=\tau_{z}\left(\tilde{S}\right)
is the visit time of the point
z
by the continuous time random walk
\tilde{S}
(
2.5
), proving the claim.

Assume now
z\in\mathds{Z}_{-}\times\mathds{Z}^{d}
satisfies
\left\lVert x\right\rVert\geq|n|,
notice
n\leq 0.
We have that
G(0,z)=\frac{\mu(z)}{\mu(0)}G(0,-z),
and since
\mu(z)\asymp e^{\lambda n},
we have the following preliminary result.

Lemma ( 2.22.1
)

If the Green’s function bounds hold on the region of
z\in\mathds{Z}_{+}\times\mathds{Z}^{d}
such that
0\leq n\leq\left\lVert x\right\rVert,
then they also hold on the region of points
z\in\mathds{Z}_{-}\times\mathds{Z}^{d}
such that
|n|\leq\left\lVert x\right\rVert.

Observe that (
2.20
) shows that the Green’s function bounds hold on the set
\{0\}\times\mathds{Z}^{d}.
Thus, with the previous lemma in mind, the case
z\in\mathds{Z}_{-}\times\mathds{Z}^{d}
has been established.

Let us focus now on the case
z=(n,x)\in\mathds{Z}_{+}^{*}\times\mathds{Z}^{d}.
By virtue of (
2.20
) and (
2.21
), we have

(
\ast
)

\textstyle G(0,z)\leq c\sum\limits_{y\in\mathds{Z}^{d}}e^{-c^{\prime}\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}q_{t}^{X}(y)\mathds{P}{}\left(\tau_{n}^{0}\in dt\right),

where
\tau_{n}^{0}
is the visit time of
n
by the walk
\tilde{B}
and
q_{t}^{X}
is the continuous time transition probability of
\tilde{X}.

Lemma ( 2.22.1
)

There exist two constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0,
independent of
z,
such that

\textstyle\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}q^{X}_{t}(y)\mathds{P}{}\left(\tau_{n}^{0}\in dt\right)\leq\begin{cases}c_{1}e^{-c_{2}n}+n^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{-c_{2}\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert^{2}}{n}}&\text{ if }\left\lVert y\right\rVert\leq\frac{d}{e^{\lambda}+1}n\\
c_{1}e^{-c_{2}\left\lVert y\right\rVert}&\text{ if }\left\lVert y\right\rVert>\frac{d}{e^{\lambda}+1}n.\end{cases}

Proof of lemma.

Recall
p=(2d+1+e^{\lambda})^{-1}.
We have
\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}q^{X}_{t}(y)\mathds{P}{}\left(\tau_{n}^{0}\in dt\right)=\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}q^{X}_{\frac{t}{2dp}}(y)\mathds{P}{}\left(2dp\ \tau_{n}^{0}\in dt\right).
We will tackle the two cases.

Case 1:

\left\lVert y\right\rVert\leq\frac{d}{e^{\lambda}+1}n.
We split the integral as follows

\textstyle\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}q^{X}_{\frac{t}{2dp}}(y)\mathds{P}{}\left(2dp\ \tau_{n}^{0}\in dt\right)=\left[\int\limits_{0}^{\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert}{e}}+\int\limits_{\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert}{e}}^{\frac{d}{e^{\lambda}+1}n}+\int\limits_{\frac{d}{e^{\lambda}+1}n}^{2dp(K_{0}+\frac{1}{a})n}+\int\limits_{2dp(K_{0}+\frac{1}{a})n}^{\infty}\right]q^{X}_{\frac{t}{2dp}}(y)\mathds{P}{}\left(2dp\ \tau_{n}^{0}\in dt\right),

where
K_{0}
is as in (
2.19
). In what follows, we will use the bounds for
\mathds{P}{}\left(\tau_{n}^{0}\leq\alpha\right)
and
\mathds{P}{}\left(\tau_{n}^{0}\leq\beta n\right)
of this proposition without further notice. The first two integrals will be bounded using the bounds (
2.6
). In the region
0\leq t\leq\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert}{e},
we have
q^{X}_{\frac{t}{2dp}}(y)\leq\frac{1}{2d}e^{-t-\left\lVert y\right\rVert\log\left(\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert}{et}\right)}
and the function
t\mapsto-t-\left\lVert y\right\rVert\log(\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert}{et})
is easily seen to be increasing on
(0,\left\lVert y\right\rVert),
hence

\displaystyle\textstyle\int\limits_{0}^{\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert}{e}}q^{X}_{\frac{t}{2dp}}(y)\mathds{P}{}\left(2dp\ \tau_{n}^{0}\in dt\right)

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq\frac{1}{2d}e^{-\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert}{e}}\mathds{P}{}\left(\tau_{n}^{0}\leq\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert}{2dpe}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2d}e^{-\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert}{e}}\mathds{P}{}\left(\tau_{n}^{0}\leq\frac{n}{2e^{\lambda+1}p}\right)\leq e^{-\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert+n}{e}}\leq e^{-\frac{n}{e}}.

Similarly, in the region
\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert}{e}\leq t<\infty,
we have the bound
q^{X}_{\frac{t}{2dp}}(y)\leq\frac{2}{d}e^{-\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert^{2}}{e^{2}t}},
and the term on the right is an increasing function of
t>0.
Hence,

\displaystyle\textstyle\int\limits_{\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert}{e}}^{\frac{d}{e^{\lambda}+1}n}q^{X}_{\frac{t}{2dp}}(y)\mathds{P}{}\left(2dp\ \tau_{n}^{0}\in dt\right)

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq\frac{2}{d}e^{-\left(\frac{e^{\lambda-2}}{d}\right)\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert^{2}}{n}}\mathds{P}{}\left(\tau_{n}^{0}\leq\frac{n}{2p(e^{\lambda}+1)}\right)\leq e^{-\left(\frac{e^{\lambda-2}}{d}\right)\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert^{2}}{n}-cn}\leq e^{-cn}

for some constant
c=u\left(\frac{1}{2p(e^{\lambda}+1)}\right)>0.
We use now the bounds (
2.8
). Consider the interval
\frac{d}{e^{\lambda}+1}n\leq t\leq 2dp\left(K_{0}+\frac{1}{a}\right)n,
we have a Gaussian decay
q^{X}_{\frac{t}{2dp}}(y)\leq c_{1}t^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{-c_{2}\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert^{2}}{t}}.
On this interval,
t\asymp n
and so, there is a pair of constants
c,c^{\prime}>0
such that
\int\limits_{\frac{d}{e^{\lambda}+1}n}^{2dp(K_{0}+\frac{1}{a})n}q^{X}_{\frac{t}{2dp}}(y)\mathds{P}{}\left(2dp\ \tau_{n}^{0}\in dt\right)\leq cn^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{-c^{\prime}\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert^{2}}{n}}.
Next, for
t\geq 2dp\left(K_{0}+\frac{1}{a}\right)n,
we use the factor
t^{-\frac{d}{2}}
of the Gaussian estimate for
q^{X}_{\frac{t}{2dp}}(y)
to conclude there exists
c>0
such that
q^{X}_{\frac{t}{2dp}}(y)\leq c_{1}t^{-\frac{d}{2}}\leq cn^{-\frac{d}{2}}.
Thus,

\textstyle\int\limits_{2dp(K_{0}+\frac{1}{a})n}^{\infty}q^{X}_{\frac{t}{2dp}}(y)\mathds{P}{}\left(2dp\ \tau_{n}^{0}\in dt\right)=cn^{-\frac{d}{2}}\mathds{P}{}\left(\tau_{n}^{0}\geq\left(K_{0}+\frac{1}{a}\right)n\right)=cn^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{-T_{0}K_{0}n}\leq e^{-T_{0}K_{0}n},

again by (
2.19
). Putting all the bounds found, this concludes the case
\left\lVert y\right\rVert\leq\frac{d}{e^{\lambda}+1}n.

Case 2:

\left\lVert y\right\rVert>\frac{d}{e^{\lambda}+1}n.
Here we divide

\textstyle\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}q^{X}_{\frac{t}{2dp}}(y)\mathds{P}{}\left(2dp\ \tau_{n}^{0}\in dt\right)=\left[\int\limits_{0}^{\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert}{e}}+\int\limits_{\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert}{e}}^{2\sigma_{0}^{2}(K_{0}+\frac{1}{a})\left\lVert y\right\rVert}+\int\limits_{2\sigma_{0}^{2}(K_{0}+\frac{1}{a})\left\lVert y\right\rVert}^{\infty}\right]q^{X}_{\frac{t}{2dp}}(y)\mathds{P}{}\left(2dp\ \tau_{n}^{0}\in dt\right).

Recall the function
t\mapsto-t-\left\lVert y\right\rVert\log\left(\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert}{et}\right)
is increasing on
(0,\left\lVert y\right\rVert).
As in
Case 1
, we can bound

\textstyle q^{X}_{\frac{t}{2dp}}(y)\leq\begin{cases}e^{-\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert}{e}}&\text{ if }t\leq\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert}{e},\\
\frac{2}{d}e^{-c\left\lVert y\right\rVert}&\text{ if }\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert}{e}\leq t\leq 2\sigma_{0}^{2}(K_{0}+\frac{1}{a})\left\lVert y\right\rVert,\\
c_{1}&\text{ if }t\geq 2\sigma_{0}^{2}(K_{0}+\frac{1}{a})\left\lVert y\right\rVert,\end{cases}

where
c_{1}
is the constant appearing on the Gaussian bound of
q_{t}^{X}
and
c=\big(2\sigma_{0}^{2}e^{2}(K_{0}+\frac{1}{a})\big)^{-1}.
We may assume, should the need arise,
K_{0}
is so large so that
2\sigma_{0}^{2}(K_{0}+\frac{1}{a})\geq 1.
It is obvious then that the first two integrals decay exponentially in
\left\lVert y\right\rVert,
as for the third one, we employ (
2.19
) to obtain

\textstyle\int\limits_{2\sigma_{0}^{2}(K_{0}+\frac{1}{a})\left\lVert y\right\rVert}^{\infty}q^{X}_{\frac{t}{2dp}}(y)\mathds{P}{}\left(2dp\ \tau_{n}^{0}\in dt\right)\leq c_{1}\mathds{P}{}\left(\tau_{n}^{0}\geq\frac{\sigma_{0}^{2}(K_{0}+\frac{1}{a})\left\lVert y\right\rVert}{dp}\right),

the condition
\frac{d}{e^{\lambda}+1}n<\left\lVert y\right\rVert
guarantees
\mathds{P}{}\left(\tau_{n}^{0}\geq\frac{\sigma_{0}^{2}(K_{0}+\frac{1}{a})\left\lVert y\right\rVert}{dp}\right)\leq e^{-T_{0}K_{0}\frac{e^{\lambda}+1}{d}\left\lVert y\right\rVert}.
This completes
Case 2
and the lemma.
∎

With the lemma proven, let us return to the proof of the upper bounds for the Green’s function. The reader is reminded that there only remains to prove the bounds for
z=(n,x)
in
\mathds{Z}_{+}^{*}\times\mathds{Z}^{d}.
The previous lemma and (
\ast
) imply that there exist two constants
c,c^{\prime}>0
such that

\textstyle G(0,z)\leq c\left(\sum\limits_{\left\lVert y\right\rVert\leq bn}e^{-c^{\prime}(\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert+n)}+\sum\limits_{\left\lVert y\right\rVert\leq bn}n^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{-c^{\prime}\left(\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert+\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert^{2}}{n}\right)}+\sum\limits_{\left\lVert y\right\rVert>bn}e^{-c^{\prime}(\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert+\left\lVert y\right\rVert)}\right),

where
b=\frac{d}{e^{\lambda}+1}.

Bearing in mind (
2.9
), we only need to prove there exists a pair of constants
c,c^{\prime}>0
such that
G(0,z)\leq ce^{-c^{\prime}\left\lVert x\right\rVert}
for all
\left\lVert x\right\rVert>bn
and that there exists a pair of constants
c,c^{\prime}>0
such that
G(0,z)\leq cn^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{-c^{\prime}\frac{\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}}{n}}
for all
\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq bn.
We consider first a sum of the form
\sum\limits_{\left\lVert y\right\rVert\leq bn}e^{-c(\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert+\alpha n)}.
Set
c^{\prime}=\min\left(c,\frac{c\alpha}{2b}\right),
then

\displaystyle\textstyle\sum\limits_{\left\lVert y\right\rVert\leq bn}e^{-c(\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert+\alpha n)}

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq\sum\limits_{\left\lVert y\right\rVert\leq bn}e^{-c^{\prime}\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert-c\alpha n}\leq e^{-c^{\prime}\left\lVert x\right\rVert}\sum\limits_{\left\lVert y\right\rVert\leq bn}e^{\frac{c\alpha}{2b}\left\lVert y\right\rVert-c\alpha n}

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq e^{-c^{\prime}\left\lVert x\right\rVert}Ln^{d}e^{-\frac{c\alpha}{2}n}\leq c^{\prime\prime}e^{-c^{\prime}\left\lVert x\right\rVert-\frac{c\alpha}{4}n},

in which
L
is a constant, depending only in dimension, satisfying that
\mathrm{card}\left(\mathrm{B}_{\mathds{Z}^{d}}\left(0;r\right)\right)\leq Lr^{d}
for all
r\geq 1
and
\displaystyle c^{\prime\prime}=\sup_{n\in\mathds{N}}Ln^{d}e^{-\frac{c\alpha}{4}n}<\infty.
Thus, we have proven the following lemma.

Lemma ( 2.22.2
)

Let
\alpha,\beta,\gamma>0.
There exist constants
c,c^{\prime}>0
such that
\sum\limits_{\left\lVert y\right\rVert\leq\beta n}e^{-\gamma(\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert+\alpha n)}\leq ce^{-c^{\prime}\left\lVert(n,x)\right\rVert},
for all
(n,x)\in\mathds{Z}\times\mathds{Z}^{d}.

Next, we consider the sum
\sum\limits_{\left\lVert y\right\rVert>bn}e^{-c^{\prime}(\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert+\left\lVert y\right\rVert)}.
In the case that
\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq bn,
then we may bound
e^{-c^{\prime}(\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert+\left\lVert y\right\rVert)}\leq e^{-c^{\prime}bn}e^{-c^{\prime}\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert},
thus
\sum\limits_{\left\lVert y\right\rVert>bn}e^{-c^{\prime}(\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert+\left\lVert y\right\rVert)}\leq e^{-c^{\prime}bn}\sum\limits_{y\in\mathds{Z}^{d}}e^{-c^{\prime}\left\lVert y\right\rVert}.
In the case that
\left\lVert x\right\rVert>b|n|,
we may bound
\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert\geq\left\lVert x\right\rVert-\left\lVert y\right\rVert,
thus
e^{-c^{\prime}(\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert+\left\lVert y\right\rVert)}\leq e^{-\frac{c^{\prime}}{2}\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert-c^{\prime}\left\lVert y\right\rVert}\leq e^{-\frac{c^{\prime}}{2}\left\lVert x\right\rVert-\frac{c^{\prime}}{2}\left\lVert y\right\rVert}.
Therefore
\sum\limits_{\left\lVert y\right\rVert>bn}e^{-c^{\prime}(\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert+\left\lVert y\right\rVert)}\leq e^{-\frac{c^{\prime}}{2}\left\lVert x\right\rVert}\sum\limits_{y\in\mathds{Z}^{d}}e^{-\frac{c^{\prime}}{2}\left\lVert y\right\rVert}.
We have established the following lemma.

Lemma ( 2.22.3
)

Let
\alpha,\beta>0.
There exists a pair of constants
c,c^{\prime}>0
such that
\sum\limits_{\left\lVert y\right\rVert>\beta n}e^{-\alpha(\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert+\left\lVert y\right\rVert)}\leq ce^{-c^{\prime}\left\lVert(n,x)\right\rVert}
for every
(n,x)\in\mathds{Z}\times\mathds{Z}^{d}.

By virtue of the previous lemmas, it remains to show that

\textstyle\sum\limits_{\left\lVert y\right\rVert\leq bn}n^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{-c^{\prime}\left(\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert+\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert^{2}}{n}\right)}\leq\begin{cases}ce^{-c^{\prime}\left\lVert x\right\rVert}&\text{ if }\left\lVert x\right\rVert>bn,\\
ce^{-c^{\prime}\frac{\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}}{n}}n^{-\frac{d}{2}}&\text{ if }\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq bn.\end{cases}

Assume first that
\left\lVert x\right\rVert>bn>0.
The estimate (
2.12
) shows at once
\sum\limits_{\left\lVert y\right\rVert\leq bn}e^{-c\left(\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert+\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert^{2}}{n}\right)}\leq ce^{-c^{\prime}\left\lVert x\right\rVert}
for a pair of constants
c,c^{\prime}>0.
This concludes the case
\left\lVert x\right\rVert>bn>0.
Assume now that
\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq bn.
By (
2.12
), we also have
\sum\limits_{\left\lVert y\right\rVert\leq bn}n^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{-c^{\prime}\left(\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert+\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert^{2}}{n}\right)}\leq cn^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{-c^{\prime}\frac{\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}}{n}}
for (possibly) another pair
c,c^{\prime}>0.
This concludes the case
\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq bn,
and thus, the proof of the upper bounds by virtue of Lemma (
2.22.1
).

Proof of lower bounds.

These are much simpler. We have
G(0,z)=\frac{\mu(z)}{\mu(0)}G(0,-z).
If
z=-(n,x)
with
n>0,
then
\mu(z)\asymp e^{-\lambda n}
and so
G(0,z)\geq ce^{-\lambda n}G(0,-z)\geq ce^{-\lambda\left\lVert z\right\rVert}G(0,-z).
Therefore, if we prove lower bounds for
n>0,
we will reach the lower bounds for
n<0.
For the case
n=0
observe the following: by (
2.16
),

\textstyle G(0,(0,x))=G(0,0)\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{(0,x)}<\infty\right)\geq G(0,0)\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{(0,x)}<\infty,\tilde{S}_{\sigma_{1}}=(1,0)\right),

where
\sigma_{1}
is the time of the first jump of the Poisson process
N
in the definition of
\tilde{S}.
Now,

\textstyle\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{(0,x)}<\infty,\tilde{S}_{N_{\sigma_{1}}}=(1,0)\right)=e^{\lambda}p\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}dt\ e^{-t}\mathds{P}{{}^{(1,0)}}\left(\tau_{(0,x)}<\infty\right)=e^{\lambda}p\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{(-1,x)}<\infty\right).

Thus, if the correct bounds hold for
n<0,
then
G(0,(0,x))\geq e^{\lambda}pG(0,(-1,x))\geq ce^{-c^{\prime}\left\lVert(1,x)\right\rVert}\left\lVert(1,x)\right\rVert^{-\frac{d}{2}},
and it is obvious that
\left\lVert(1,x)\right\rVert\asymp\left\lVert(0,x)\right\rVert
(
2.9
). Whence, the proof of the lower bounds reduces to the case
n>0.

When
n>0,
we can apply (
2.21
). Now,

\textstyle\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}q_{t}^{X}(y)\mathds{P}{}\left(\tau_{n}^{0}\in dt\right)\geq\int\limits_{\frac{2d}{e^{\lambda}}n}^{4(K_{0}+\frac{1}{a})dp\ n}q_{\frac{t}{2dp}}^{X}(y)\mathds{P}{}\left(2dp\ \tau_{n}^{0}\in dt\right).

Observe that the lower bounds of (
2.7
) and (
2.8
) are of the same type (with possibly different constants) whenever
\left\lVert y\right\rVert\asymp t.
On the interval of integration,
t\asymp n,
therefore

\textstyle q_{\frac{t}{2dp}}^{X}(y)\geq\begin{cases}cn^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{-c^{\prime}\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert^{2}}{n}}&\text{ if }n\geq\left\lVert y\right\rVert,\\
c\left\lVert y\right\rVert^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{-c^{\prime}\left\lVert y\right\rVert}&\text{ if }n<\left\lVert y\right\rVert.\end{cases}

Also, (
2.19
) gives
\mathds{P}{}\left(\frac{2d}{e^{\lambda}}n\leq 2dp\ \tau_{n}^{0}\leq 4(K_{0}+\frac{1}{a})dp\ n\right)\geq c,
for a constant
c>0
independent of
n.
Therefore,

\textstyle\frac{G(0,z)}{G(0,0)}\geq c\left(\sum\limits_{n\geq\left\lVert y\right\rVert}\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{(0,x-y)}<\infty\right)n^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{-c^{\prime}\frac{\left\lVert y\right\rVert^{2}}{n}}+\sum\limits_{n<\left\lVert y\right\rVert}\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(\tau_{(0,x-y)}<\infty\right)\left\lVert y\right\rVert^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{-c^{\prime}\left\lVert y\right\rVert}\right).

We discard all the terms except
y=x,
and reach

\textstyle G(0,z)\geq\begin{cases}cG(0,0)n^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{-c^{\prime}\frac{\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}}{n}}&\text{ if }n\geq\left\lVert x\right\rVert\\
cG(0,0)\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{-c^{\prime}\left\lVert x\right\rVert}&\text{ if }n<\left\lVert x\right\rVert.\end{cases}

This terminates the proof of Theorem (
2.22
).
∎

3
The number of trees in
\mathsf{USF}

In this section we will establish a number of basic properties of the uniform spanning forest.
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)
has the Liouville property, this implies both measures
\mathsf{WSF}
and
\mathsf{FSF}
coincide in this network. Finally, establish when the samples are trees or forests.

3.1
Coupling of random walks

Consider two vertices
x
and
y
in the network
(\mathrm{G},\mu).
We will say that the network random walk started at
x
can be
classically coupled
with the network random walk started at
y
if the following condition holds:

(
\mathsf{CC}
)

There exists a probability space
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathds{P}),
a filtration
(\mathscr{F}_{n})_{n\in\mathds{Z}_{+}}
in this probability space, two Markov processes with respect to this filtration and defined on this probability space, denoted by
(S_{n},\mathscr{F}_{n})
and
\left(S_{n}^{\prime},\mathscr{F}_{n}\right),
both of them having for transition probability that defined by the conductances of the network,
S_{0}=x,

S_{0}^{\prime}=y
and there exists a stopping time
\tau
relative to the filtration
(\mathscr{F}_{n})_{n\in\mathds{Z}_{+}},
such that
\tau<\infty

\mathds{P}
-a.e. and for those
\omega\in\Omega
for which
\tau(\omega)<\infty,

S_{\tau(\omega)+n}(\omega)=S_{\tau(\omega)+n}^{\prime}(\omega)
for any
n\in\mathds{N}.

When this happens, we will call
\tau
the
coupling time
and the condition
\tau<\infty

\mathds{P}
-a.e. is expressed by saying that “
\tau
is successful.” Obviously, for a classical coupling to exist, it is necessary that
x
and
y
should be connected in
(\mathrm{G},\mu),
but this is far from sufficient.

Remark ( 3.1
)

Observe that by definition, a classical coupling is a coupling
T=\left(S,S^{\prime}\right)
of the network random walk
S
started at
x
and the network random walk
S^{\prime}
started at
y
satisfying strong conditions and it does not necessarily exists; for instance, the standard lattice
\mathds{Z}^{1}
with
x=0
and
y=1,
any two standard random walks will always be at distance at least one and so, no classical coupling can exist (the “parity problem”).

Proposition ( 3.2
)

Denote by
u_{i}
(
1\leq i\leq d
) the canonical unit vectors of
\mathds{Z}^{d}.
Let
\rho
be a probability measure on
\mathds{Z}^{d}
such that

(a)

the relation
\rho(x)>0
implies
x
is an integer multiple of some
u_{i};

(b)

\rho(\mathds{Z}^{*}u_{i})>0
for each
i=1,\ldots,d.

Introduce two probability measures, first
\varphi(i)=\rho(\mathds{Z}^{*}u_{i})+\frac{\rho(0)}{d}
(
1\leq i\leq d
) and then
\nu_{i}(k)=\frac{\rho(ku_{i})}{\varphi(i)}
for
k\in\mathds{Z}^{*}
and
\nu_{i}(0)=\frac{\rho(0)}{d\varphi(i)}.
Suppose
F_{j}\sim\varphi
and
X_{j}^{(i)}\sim\nu_{i}
are independent random elements (
1\leq i\leq d
and
j\in\mathds{Z}_{+}
). Set
Y_{j}=X_{j}^{(F_{j})}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{d}X_{j}^{(i)}\mathds{1}_{\{F_{j}=i\}}.
Then, for any
S_{0}
independent of the families
X_{j}^{(i)}
and
F_{j},

S_{n}=S_{0}+Y_{1}+\ldots+Y_{n}
is a random walk on
\mathds{Z}^{d}
started at
S_{0}
with transition probability
\rho:

\mathds{P}{}\left(Y_{j}=x\right)=\rho(x)
(
x\in\mathds{Z}^{d}
).

Remark ( 3.3
)

When
\rho
of (
3.2
) is given by the
\frac{1}{2}
-lazy version of (
2.1
), the appropriate distributions are
\varphi(0)=(1+e^{\lambda})(2(2d+1+e^{\lambda}))^{-1}+(2(d+1))^{-1},
and for
i=1,\ldots,d,

\varphi(i)=(2d+1+e^{\lambda})^{-1}+(2(d+1))^{-1}.
While the
\nu_{i}
are given as
\nu_{0}(-1)=(2(2d+1+e^{\lambda})\varphi(0))^{-1},

\nu_{0}(0)=(2(d+1)\varphi(0))^{-1},

\nu_{0}(1)=e^{\lambda}(2(2d+1+e^{\lambda})\varphi(0))^{-1}
and, for
i=1,\ldots,d,

\nu_{i}(1)=\nu_{i}(-1)=(2(2d+1+e^{\lambda})\varphi(i))^{-1},

\nu_{i}(0)=(2(d+1)\varphi(i))^{-1}.

The following theorem is arguably well-known while a source where it is proven is elusive to find. The reader can find a complete proof in
[
MD20
]
, theorem (3.2.3). The proof-strategy follows by classically coupling each coordinate at a time.

Theorem ( 3.4
)

Let
\rho
be a probability measure on
\mathds{Z}^{d}
such that

(a)

the relation
\rho(x)>0
implies
x=ku_{i}
for some
k\in\mathds{Z}
and some canonical unitary vector
u_{i};

(b)

\rho(\mathds{Z}^{\ast}u_{i})>0
for each
i=1,\ldots,d;

(c)

for every
i=1,\ldots,d,
the probability measures
\nu_{i},
defined in (
3.2
), and
\bar{\nu_{i}}
defined by
\bar{\nu}_{i}:k\mapsto\nu_{i}(-k)
(
k\in\mathds{Z}
) are such that their convolution
\nu_{i}\ast\bar{\nu}_{i}
defines a transition probability kernel for a Markov chain on
\mathds{Z}
that is irreducible and recurrent.

Then, for any pair of vertices
x
and
y
in
\mathds{Z}^{d},
there exists a classical coupling of random walks started at
x
and
y,
respectively.

3.2
The Liouville property

We will say that a network satisfies

(
\mathsf{LP}
)

The
Liouville property
if every
bounded
harmonic function on the network is constant.

(
\mathsf{SLP}
)

The
strong Liouville property
if every
positive
harmonic function on the network is constant.

(
\mathsf{DLP}
)

The
Dirichlet Liouville property
if every
Dirichlet
harmonic function is constant.

Remark ( 3.5
)

(a)

For any network, we have the following implications from the above properties

\textstyle(\mathsf{SLP})\implies(\mathsf{LP})\implies(\mathsf{DLP}).

The first of these implications is trivial for if
h
is a bounded harmonic function, then
h+c
is a positive harmonic function for large enough constant
c,
then condition (
\mathsf{SLP}
) implies
h+c
is constant, and so is
h.
The second of this implications is harder and it fully proved in
[
MD20
]
, appendix D (the author followed closely the steps of Soardi
[
Soa94
]
).

(b)

It is a well-known fact that the graph
\mathds{Z}^{d}
satisfies the strong Liouville property. One proof is given in
[
Bar17
]
. Here, M. Barlow followed an already established strategy: showing that
\mathds{Z}^{d}
satisfies the so-called
elliptic Harnack inequality
(
\mathsf{EHI}
) and also showing that for any network
(\mathsf{EHI})\implies(\mathsf{SLP}).

(c)

The network
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)
does not satisfy (
\mathsf{SLP}
). Consider the function
h(z)=e^{-\lambda n}
(with
z=(n,x)
as usual). Then, with
p=(2d+1+e^{\lambda})^{-1},

\textstyle\sum\limits_{z^{\prime}\sim z}p(z,z^{\prime})h(z^{\prime})=pe^{-\lambda(n-1)}+2dpe^{-\lambda n}+e^{\lambda}pe^{-\lambda(n+1)}=e^{-\lambda n}=h(z),

so that
h
is harmonic, and proving the claim.

(d)

If on a given network and for any pair of vertices, two lazy network random walks, one started at each vertex, can be classically coupled, then this network satisfies the Liouville property. In other words,

\textstyle(\mathsf{CC})\implies(\mathsf{LP}).

First notice that a function
h:\mathrm{V}\to\mathds{R}
is harmonic relative to the
\mathsf{NRW}
if and only if it is harmonic relative to the
\frac{1}{2}
-lazy
\mathsf{NRW}.
Next, consider a bounded harmonic function
h,
any two vertices
x,y
and the two
\frac{1}{2}
-lazy
\mathsf{NRW}
s
S
and
S^{\prime},
started at
x
and
y,
respectively, with successful coupling time
\tau.
Since
h(S)
and
h(S^{\prime})
are bounded martingales, we obtain
h(x)=\mathds{E}{}\left(h(S_{\tau})\right)=\mathds{E}{}\left(h(S_{\tau}^{\prime})\right)=h(y).

Theorem ( 3.6
)

The network
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)
has the Liouville property.

Proof.

By virtue of the previous proposition, it suffices to prove that any two lazy network random walks (started at any two vertices) of
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)
can be classically coupled. In this case, the hypothesis of theorem (
3.4
) are satisfied: the measure
\nu_{i}\ast\bar{\nu}_{i},
where
\nu_{i}
is as in (
3.3
) is clearly symmetric and bounded, it is aperiodic by lazyness, thus
\nu_{i}\ast\bar{\nu}_{i}
is also recurrent and irreducible.
∎

3.3
Liouville property and the uniform spanning forest measure

The Liouville property is relevant to the context of uniform spanning forests due to the following theorem.

Theorem ( 3.7
)

Let
\Gamma
be any network. A sufficient condition for the measures
\mathsf{WSF}
and
\mathsf{FSF},
of the wired and free uniform spanning forest on
\mathrm{G},
to be the same, is that this network should satisfy the Liouville property.

For the proof, see
[
BLPS01
, Theorem 7.3]
. As a corollary of the foregoing.

Theorem ( 3.8
)

\mathsf{WSF}=\mathsf{FSF}
on
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda).

By virtue of the equality between
\mathsf{WSF}
and
\mathsf{FSF}
on
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda),
we will denote this measure as
\mathsf{USF}
from now onwards.

3.4
The number of components

To count the number of components, the reader should recall the definition of transitive network. We know
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)
is transitive by considering translations.

We need the following results:

[
LP16
]
, Theorem 10.24.

In any transient transitive network
(\mathrm{G},\mu),
with vertex set
\mathrm{V}
and Green’s function
G,
the number of intersections of any two independent network random walks is almost surely infinity if
\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{V}}G(0,z)^{2}=\infty
and is almost surely finite otherwise.

[
BLPS01
]
, Theorem 9.2.

On any network, for the number of components in
\mathsf{WSF}
to be one, it is necessary and sufficient that, two independent network random walk started at any two vertices, should intersect (a.s.).

[
BLPS01
]
, Theorem 9.4.

If there exists a vertex
o
such that, for almost every realisation, the number of intersections of two independent copies of the network random walk started at
o
is finite, then the number of components in
\mathsf{WSF}
is infinite.

Theorem ( 3.9
)

Consider the network
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda).
The
\mathsf{USF}
here (
3.8
) consists of a single tree when
d=1,2
and it has an infinite number of components when
d\geq 3.
From now onwards, we use
\mathsf{UST}
in lieu of
\mathsf{USF}
for
d=1,2.

Proof.

By the theorems stated above, it all reduces to show that
\sum\limits_{z\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}}G(0,z)^{2}
diverges for
d=1,2
and that it converges otherwise. In the sake of clarity, we separate the calculations in the next proposition, with them the theorem is proved.
∎

As mentioned in the proof above, the next theorem states when the Green’s function belongs to
\mathscr{L}^{2}.

Theorem ( 3.10
)

(The “bubble condition.”)

(a)

If
d=1,2,

\sum\limits_{z\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}}G(0,z)^{2}=\infty.

(b)

If
d\geq 3,

\sum\limits_{z\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}}G(0,z)^{2}<\infty.

Proof.

Recall the Fourier transform of the step-lengths of the network random (
2.4
). Now, if
(S_{n})
is the network random walk started at zero and
\varphi_{n}
is its Fourier transform of
S_{n},
then
\varphi_{n}(h)=\varphi_{Y}(h)^{n}.
Hence, for all
h\neq 0,

\widehat{G}(h)=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\varphi_{n}(h)=(1-\varphi_{Y}(h))^{-1},
where
\widehat{G}
denotes the Fourier transform of the Green’s function
G.
By Parseval’s relation,

(3.11)

\textstyle\sum\limits_{z\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}}G(0,z)^{2}=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d+1}}\int\limits_{[-\pi,\pi]^{d+1}}dh\ \left|1-\varphi_{Y}(h)\right|^{-2},

where the two integrals will converge to the same value, or they will both diverge.

Using second order expansion in (
2.4
) and with
a
as in (
2.3
) and
p=(2d+1+e^{\lambda})^{-1},
we reach

\textstyle\varphi_{Y}(h)=1+iah_{0}-\frac{a}{2}h_{0}^{2}-p\sum\limits_{j=0}^{d}h_{j}^{2}+\mathrm{o}\left(h_{0}^{2}\right)+\mathrm{O}\left(\left\lVert h\right\rVert^{4}\right),\quad h\to 0.

Denote
\alpha_{j}=p
for
j=1,\ldots,d
and
\alpha_{0}=p+\frac{a}{2},

\varphi_{Y}(h)=1+iah_{0}-\sum\limits_{j=0}^{d}\alpha_{j}h_{j}^{2}+\mathrm{o}\left(h_{0}^{2}\right)+\mathrm{O}\left(\left\lVert h\right\rVert^{4}\right).
Then,

(
\ast
)

\displaystyle\textstyle\left|1-\varphi_{Y}(h)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\textstyle=a^{2}h_{0}^{2}+iah_{0}\sum\limits_{j=0}^{d}\alpha_{j}h_{j}^{2}+\mathrm{o}\left(h_{0}^{2}\right)+\mathrm{O}\left(h_{0}\left\lVert h\right\rVert^{4}\right)

\displaystyle\textstyle-iah_{0}\sum\limits_{j=0}^{d}\alpha_{j}h_{j}^{2}+\left(\sum\limits_{j=0}^{d}\alpha_{j}h_{j}^{2}\right)^{2}+\mathrm{O}\left(\left\lVert h\right\rVert^{6}\right)

\displaystyle\textstyle=a^{2}h_{0}^{2}+\left(\sum\limits_{j=0}^{d}\alpha_{j}h_{j}^{2}\right)^{2}+\mathrm{o}\left(h_{0}^{2}\right)+\mathrm{O}\left(h_{0}\left\lVert h\right\rVert^{4}\right)+\mathrm{o}\left(\left\lVert h\right\rVert^{4}\right).

Divide the right-hand side of (
3.11
) as the sum of
I_{\varepsilon}+J_{\varepsilon},
where
I_{\varepsilon}
has domain of integration
\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}=[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]\times\mathrm{B}_{\mathds{R}^{d}}\left(0;\varepsilon\right)
and
J_{\varepsilon}
has domain of integration
[-\pi,\pi]^{d+1}\setminus\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}.
It follows at once
J_{\varepsilon}<\infty.
We will show the existence of a positive
\varepsilon
such that
I_{\varepsilon}=\infty
for
d=1,2
and
I_{\varepsilon}<\infty
for
d\geq 3.

Assume first
d=1,2.
Then by (
\ast
) for
\varepsilon
small enough,
|1-\varphi_{Y}(h)|^{2}\leq c^{-1}(h_{0}^{2}+\left\lVert h^{\prime}\right\rVert^{4})
for some
c>0.
Then,

\textstyle I_{\varepsilon}\geq c\int\limits_{\mathrm{B}_{\mathds{R}^{d}}\left(0;\varepsilon\right)}dh^{\prime}\ \int\limits_{-\varepsilon}^{\varepsilon}dh_{0}\ \frac{1}{h_{0}^{2}+\left\lVert h^{\prime}\right\rVert^{4}}=2c\int\limits_{\mathrm{B}_{\mathds{R}^{d}}\left(0;\varepsilon\right)}dh^{\prime}\ \int\limits_{0}^{\varepsilon}dh_{0}\ \frac{1}{h_{0}^{2}+\left\lVert h^{\prime}\right\rVert^{4}}.

Changing measures to surface measure on the spheres of
\mathds{R}^{d},
there is a constant
c>0
such that

\textstyle I_{\varepsilon}\geq c\int\limits_{0}^{\varepsilon}d\rho\ \rho^{d-1}\int\limits_{0}^{\varepsilon}dh_{0}\ \frac{1}{h_{0}^{2}+\rho^{4}}=c\int\limits_{0}^{\varepsilon}d\rho\ \rho^{d-3}\arctan\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\rho^{2}}\right)=\infty.

Similarly from (
\ast
), we can assume
\varepsilon>0
is small enough so that for some constant
c>0,

\left|1-\varphi_{Y}(h)\right|^{2}\geq c^{-1}(h_{0}^{2}+\left\lVert h^{\prime}\right\rVert^{4}).
As before, we change to surface measure of spheres and use that
\rho^{d-1}\leq\rho^{2}
since
\varepsilon
can be assumed smaller than unity and
d\geq 3
, then

\textstyle I_{\varepsilon}\leq c\int\limits_{\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}}\frac{1}{h_{0}^{2}+\left\lVert h^{\prime}\right\rVert^{4}}d(h_{0},h^{\prime})\leq c^{\prime}\int\limits_{0}^{\varepsilon}d\rho\ \rho^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\arctan\left(\frac{h_{0}}{\rho^{2}}\right)\Bigg|_{h_{0}=0}^{h_{0}=\varepsilon}<\infty.

Therefore, we reach that the sum (
3.11
) is finite for all
d\geq 3.

∎

4
Crossings of
\mathsf{NRW}

4.1
Dimensions
d=1,3,4,\ldots

Theorem ( 4.1
)

Let
d\geq 3.
Consider two independent network random walks
S
and
S^{\prime}
on
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda).
Suppose they have arbitrary (random) initial points
S_{0}
and
S_{0}^{\prime},
respectively. Then, almost surely, the paths of
S
and
S^{\prime}
will cross finitely many times.

Proof.

By the bubble condition (
3.10
),
\sum\limits_{z\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}}G(0,z)^{2}<\infty
in this range of dimensions. The theorem then follows from
[
LP16
, Theorem 10.24]
.
∎

The previous theorem is not true in dimension
d=1,2.
However, there is an important distinction between the cases
d=1
and
d=2.
The gist of the distinction lies in how frequently the two random paths cross. In this section we consider the case
d=1.

Theorem ( 4.2
)

Let
d=1.
Consider two independent network random walks
S
and
S^{\prime}
on
\Gamma_{1}(\lambda)
with arbitrary starting points,
S_{0}
and
S_{0}^{\prime}.
Then,
S-S^{\prime}
is a recurrent random walk; in particular, for almost every realisation, there will be infinitely many
n
for which
S_{n}-S_{n}^{\prime}=S_{0}-S_{0}^{\prime}.
Thus, if both
S
and
S^{\prime}
start at the same point, then
S
and
S^{\prime}
will collide infinitely often.

Proof.

By means of a translation by
-(S_{0}-S_{0}^{\prime}),
we can assume
S_{0}-S_{0}^{\prime}=0.
The difference
S-S^{\prime}
is a random walk, started at zero and has step-length distribution

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle=p^{2}e^{\lambda}\Big(\varepsilon_{(-2,0)}+\varepsilon_{(2,0)}\Big)+p^{2}\Big(\varepsilon_{(0,2)}+\varepsilon_{(0,-2)}\Big)

\displaystyle+p^{2}(e^{\lambda}+1)\Big(\varepsilon_{(-1,-1)}+\varepsilon_{(1,1)}+\varepsilon_{(-1,1)}+\varepsilon_{(1,-1)}\Big)+p^{2}(e^{2\lambda}+3)\varepsilon_{(0,0)},

where
\varepsilon_{(h,k)}
denotes the measures of unitary total mass at
(h,k)\in\mathds{Z}^{2}
and
p=\frac{1}{3+e^{\lambda}}.
Observe that
S-S^{\prime}
is a symmetric, aperiodic and irreducible random walk with bounded step lengths on the lattice generated by
u_{1}=(1,1)
and
u_{2}=(-1,1).
By
[
LL10
, Theorem 4.1.1]

S-S^{\prime}
is recurrent.
∎

The remainder of this chapter is devoted into
estimating how frequently
there will be a crossing between two network random walk paths on
\Gamma_{2}(\lambda).
Whence, in the upcoming sections we will develop the necessary tools to tackle dimension
d=2.

4.2
Two elementary results

For the sake of reference, we write the following. The “second moment inequality” states that for a non-negative random variable

(4.3)

\textstyle\mathds{P}{}\left(Z>\frac{1}{2}\mathds{E}{}\left(Z\right)\right)\geq\frac{\mathds{E}{}\left(Z\right)^{2}}{4\mathds{E}{}\left(Z^{2}\right)}.

Consider now an increasing sequence of
\sigma
-fields
(\mathscr{E}_{n})_{n\in\mathds{Z}_{+}},
with
\mathscr{E}_{0}
the
\sigma
-field generated by
\varnothing,
on some probability space
(\mathrm{E},\mathscr{E},\mathds{P}).
Suppose that
\mathrm{A}_{n}
is an event measurable up to time
n,
that is
\mathrm{A}_{n}\in\mathscr{E}_{n}.
Set
q_{n}=\mathds{P}{}\left(\mathrm{A}_{n}\mid\mathscr{E}_{n-1}\right)
(so that
q_{1}=\mathds{P}{}\left(\mathrm{A}_{1}\right)
). We have the following result.

Proposition ( 4.4
)

The event
\mathrm{N}=\{\mathrm{A}_{n}\mathrm{\ i.o.}\}\triangle\left\{\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}q_{n}=\infty\right\}
is
\mathds{P}
-null (“Levy’s extension of Borel-Cantelli lemma”).

The proof of this result is a standard application of martingale theory to
X_{n}=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}(\mathds{1}_{\mathrm{A}_{j}}-q_{j}).
See section 7.8 of
[
Ash72
]
(apply theorem 7.8.4).

4.3
Setup for
\Gamma_{2}(\lambda)

As before, we will write
z=(n,x);
any affix, such as a sub or superscript, will be given to the three symbols: if we write
z^{\prime}
at some point, it will be assumed that
z^{\prime}=(n^{\prime},x^{\prime}),
similarly, if we talk about
n_{1}
and
x_{1},
we will be assuming
z_{1}=(n_{1},x_{1}),
etc.

Divide the space
\mathds{Z}\times\mathds{Z}^{2}
into the two sets
\mathrm{S}_{1}=\{z;n\leq\left\lVert x\right\rVert\}
and
\mathrm{S}_{2}=\{z;n>\left\lVert x\right\rVert\}.
Notice
0\in\mathrm{S}_{1}
and
\mathrm{S}_{1}\cap\mathrm{S}_{2}=\varnothing.
To simplify notation, we define (for any
y\in\mathds{Z}^{r},r\geq 1
)

(4.5)

\textstyle\left\langle y\right\rangle=\max(\left\lVert y\right\rVert,1),

and notice
\left\langle y\right\rangle=\left\lVert y\right\rVert
unless
y=0;
also
