\left\langle y\right\rangle\asymp\left\lVert y\right\rVert+1\asymp\sqrt{\left\lVert y\right\rVert^{2}+1}.

Consider the following tessellation (
p,q\in\mathds{N}
and
i=1,2
) of the “positive half-space”
\mathds{Z}_{+}\times\mathds{Z}^{2}
:

(4.6)

\textstyle\begin{split}\mathrm{R}_{1}(p,q)&=\{z:9^{p}<n\leq 2\cdot 9^{p},3^{q-1}<\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq 3^{q}\},\\
\mathrm{R}_{2}(p,q)&=\{z:2\cdot 9^{p}<n\leq 9^{p+1},3^{q-1}<\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq 3^{q}\}\\
\mathrm{R}_{1}(p,0)&=\{z:9^{p}<n\leq 2\cdot 9^{p},\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq 1\},\\
\mathrm{R}_{2}(p,0)&=\{z:2\cdot 9^{p}<n\leq 2\cdot 9^{p+1},\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq 1\},\\
\mathrm{R}_{1}(0,q)&=\{z:0\leq n\leq 2,3^{q-1}<\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq 3^{q}\}\\
\mathrm{R}_{2}(0,q)&=\{z:3\leq n\leq 9,3^{q-1}<\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq 3^{q}\}\\
\mathrm{R}_{1}(0,0)&=\{z:0\leq n\leq 2,\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq 1\}\\
\mathrm{R}_{2}(0,0)&=\{z:3\leq n\leq 9,\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq 1\}\end{split}

For
p\in\mathds{N},
we shall consider the following region

(4.7)

\textstyle\mathrm{D}_{p}=\bigcup\limits_{q=0}^{p}\mathrm{R}_{1}(p,q)=\{z:9^{p}<n\leq 2\cdot 9^{p},\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq 3^{p}\}.

The following easy remark contains all the technical estimates that will be employed later in this section. Consider the Green’s function bounds and fix the constants
c_{i}
(
1\leq i\leq 4
) of (
2.22
).

Remark ( 4.8
)

For
i=1,2
and
(p,q)\in\mathds{Z}_{+}^{2},
we have the following.

(a)

\mathrm{card}\left(\mathrm{R}_{i}(p,q)\right)\asymp 9^{p+q}.

(b)

\mathrm{R}_{i_{1}}(p_{1},q_{1})
and
\mathrm{R}_{i_{2}}(p_{2},q_{2})
are disjoint unless
(i_{1},p_{1},q_{1})=(i_{2},p_{2},q_{2}).

(c)

\mathrm{card}\left(\mathrm{D}_{p}\right)\asymp 9^{p}\sum\limits_{q=0}^{p}9^{q}\asymp 9^{2p}.

(d)

We have
\mathrm{R}_{i}(p,q)\subset\mathrm{S}_{1}
if
2p+3\leq q
and
\mathrm{R}_{i}(p,q)\subset\mathrm{S}_{2}
if
q\leq 2p.

(e)

If
z\in\mathds{Z}_{+}\times\mathds{Z}^{2}
is in
\mathrm{S}_{1}
and
c
is a positive constant, then
0\leq n\leq\left\lVert x\right\rVert,
this implies
\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert z\right\rVert\leq\sqrt{2}\left\lVert x\right\rVert,
and then
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}e^{-\sqrt{2}c\left\lVert x\right\rVert}\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-1}\leq e^{-c\left\lVert z\right\rVert}\left\langle z\right\rangle^{-1}\leq e^{-c\left\lVert x\right\rVert}\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-1}.
Therefore, if
3^{q-1}\leq\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq 3^{q},

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}e^{-\sqrt{2}c3^{q}}3^{-q}\leq e^{-c\left\lVert z\right\rVert}\left\langle z\right\rangle^{-1}\leq 3e^{-\frac{c}{3}3^{q}}3^{-q}.

(f)

If
z\in\mathds{Z}_{+}\times\mathds{Z}^{2}
is in
\mathrm{S}_{2}
and
c
is a positive constant, then
\left\lVert x\right\rVert<n,
this implies
0<n\leq\left\lVert z\right\rVert\leq\sqrt{2}n,
and then
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}e^{-c\frac{\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}}{n}}n^{-1}\leq e^{-c\frac{\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}}{n}}\left\lVert z\right\rVert^{-1}\leq e^{-c\frac{\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}}{n}}n^{-1}.
Therefore, if
9^{p}\leq n\leq 9^{p+1}
and
3^{q-1}\leq\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq 3^{q}

\frac{1}{9\sqrt{2}}e^{-c9^{q-p}}9^{-p}\leq e^{-c\frac{\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}}{n}}\left\lVert z\right\rVert^{-1}\leq e^{-\frac{c}{81}9^{q-p}}9^{-p}.

(g)

Define the function
\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}:\mathds{Z}\times\mathds{Z}^{2}\to\mathds{R}
given by
\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}(z)=c_{1}e^{-c_{2}\left\lVert z\right\rVert}\left\langle z\right\rangle^{-1}
if
z
lies in region
\mathrm{S}_{1}
and
\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}(z)=c_{1}e^{-c_{2}\frac{\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}}{n}}\left\lVert z\right\rVert^{-1}
for
z
within
\mathrm{S}_{2}.
Then
G(0,z)\leq\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}(z).
If
z\in\mathrm{R}_{1}(p,q)\cup\mathrm{R}_{2}(p,q),

\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}(z)\leq\begin{cases}3c_{1}e^{-\frac{c_{2}}{3}3^{q}}3^{-q}&\text{ if }2p+3\leq q,\\
c_{1}e^{-\frac{c_{2}}{81}9^{q-p}}9^{-p}&\text{ if }q\leq 2p+2.\end{cases}

(h)

Define
\varphi_{\mathrm{L}}(z)=c_{3}e^{-c_{4}\left\lVert z\right\rVert}\left\langle z\right\rangle^{-1}
if
z\in\mathrm{S}_{1}
and
\varphi_{\mathrm{L}}(z)=c_{3}e^{-c_{4}\frac{\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}}{n}}\left\lVert z\right\rVert^{-1}
if
z\in\mathrm{S}_{2}.
Then,
G(0,z)\geq\varphi_{\mathrm{L}}(z).
If
z\in\mathrm{R}_{i}(p,q)
(
i=1,2
),

\varphi_{\mathrm{L}}(z)\geq\begin{cases}\frac{c_{3}}{\sqrt{2}}e^{-\sqrt{2}c_{4}3^{q}}3^{-q}&\text{ if }2p+3\leq q,\\
\frac{c_{3}}{9\sqrt{2}}e^{-c_{4}9^{q-p}}9^{-p}&\text{ if }q\leq 2p+2.\end{cases}

(i)

For
z=(n,x)\in\mathds{Z}\times\mathds{Z}^{2}
define
\dot{z}=(-n,x)\in\mathds{Z}\times\mathds{Z}^{2}
(the “reflection” of
z
through the “plane”
\{0\}\times\mathds{Z}^{2}
). Then,
z\mapsto\dot{z}
is a bijection of
\mathds{Z}_{\pm}\times\mathds{Z}^{2}
onto
\mathds{Z}_{\mp}\times\mathds{Z}^{2},
it is the identity when restricted to
\{0\}\times\mathds{Z}^{2}.
For every
z\in\mathds{Z}_{-}\times\mathds{Z}^{2},

\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}(z)\leq\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}\left(\dot{z}\right).

(j)

There are constants
d_{1}=3c_{1},

d_{2}=\frac{c_{2}}{81},

d_{3}=\frac{c_{3}}{18}
and
d_{4}=2c_{4},
such that the relation
z\in\mathrm{R}_{i}(p,q)
(
p,q\in\mathds{Z}_{+},

i=1,2
) imply

G(0,z)\leq\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}(z)\leq\begin{cases}d_{1}e^{-d_{2}3^{q}}3^{-q}&\text{ if }2p+1\leq q,\\
d_{1}e^{-d_{2}9^{q-p}}9^{-p}&\text{ if }q\leq 2p.\end{cases}

and

G(0,z)\geq\varphi_{\mathrm{L}}(z)\geq\begin{cases}d_{3}e^{-d_{4}3^{q}}3^{-q}&\text{ if }2p+1\leq q,\\
d_{3}e^{-d_{4}9^{q-p}}9^{-p}&\text{ if }q\leq 2p.\end{cases}

(k)

G(0,z)\asymp 9^{-p}
with any constant being uniform for
z\in\mathrm{D}_{p}
and
p\in\mathds{N}.

4.3.1
Some preliminary sums of the Green’s function of
\Gamma_{2}(\lambda).

In what follows, the estimates in Remark (
4.8
) will be used, often without referencing to this remark.

Proposition ( 4.9
)

Let
S
and
S^{\prime}
be two independent network random walks started at
0
in
\Gamma_{2}(\lambda).
Then, with any implicit constants being independent of
p\in\mathds{N},

(a)

\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{D}_{p}}G(0,z)^{2}\asymp 1;
and

(b)

\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{D}_{p}-\mathrm{D}_{p}}G(0,z)G(0,-z)\asymp 1.

(c)

Also, there exists a constant
c>0
such that
\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{D}_{p}-\mathrm{D}_{p}}G(0,z)^{2}\leq cp,
for all
p\in\mathds{N}.

Proof.

We know
G(0,z)^{2}\asymp 9^{-2p}
and
\mathrm{card}\left(\mathrm{D}_{p}\right)\asymp 9^{2p}
for
z\in\mathrm{D}_{p}
and the implicit constants independent of
p;
this proves the first assertion
\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{D}_{p}}G(0,z)^{2}\asymp 1.
There remains to show the other two estimates. First, it is clear that
\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{D}_{p}-\mathrm{D}_{p}}G(0,z)G(0,-z)\geq G(0,0)^{2}.
Hence, there remains to prove the existence of constants
c>0
and
c^{\prime}>0
such that
\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{D}_{p}-\mathrm{D}_{p}}G(0,z)G(0,-z)\leq c
and
\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{D}_{p}-\mathrm{D}_{p}}G(0,z)^{2}\leq c^{\prime}p,
for all
p\in\mathds{N}.
Set
\mathrm{Q}^{(p)}=\mathrm{D}_{p}-\mathrm{D}_{p}
and
\mathrm{Q}_{+}^{(p)}=\mathrm{Q}^{(p)}\cap(\mathds{Z}_{+}\times\mathds{Z}^{2}).
Consider the function
\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}
introduced in (
4.8
), we regard the constants
c_{k}
and
d_{k}
(
k=1,\ldots,4
) of (
4.8
) to be fixed during the argument. Then
\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{Q}^{(p)}}G(0,z)G(0,-z)\leq 2\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{Q}_{+}^{(p)}}G(0,z)G(0,-z)\leq 2\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{Q}_{+}^{(p)}}\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}(z)\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}(-z),
and
\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{Q}^{(p)}}G(0,z)^{2}\leq\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{Q}^{(p)}}\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}(z)^{2}\leq 2\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{Q}_{+}^{(p)}}\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}(z)^{2}
where the second inequality follows from considering the reflection
z\mapsto\dot{z}
(
4.8
)(i). To simplify notation, introduce
\mathrm{R}(s,t)
to be the union
\mathrm{R}_{1}(s,t)\cup\mathrm{R}_{2}(s,t),
these sets being defined in (
4.6
). We know that there exists a constant
L>0
such that for all
s,t\in\mathds{Z}_{+},

\mathrm{card}\left(\mathrm{R}(s,t)\right)\leq L9^{s+t}.
Then, one can write
\mathrm{Q}_{+}^{(p)}\subset\bigcup\limits_{(s,t)}\mathrm{R}(s,t),
where the indices run over all choices of
0\leq s,t\leq p+1.
Now, the sets
\mathrm{R}(s,t)
are pairwise disjoint and, therefore, we split
\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{Q}_{+}^{(p)}}\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}(z)\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}(-z)\leq\sum\limits_{(s,t)}\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{R}(s,t)}\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}(z)\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}(-z)=P_{1}+P_{2},
and, in a similar fashion,
\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{Q}_{+}^{(p)}}\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}(z)^{2}\leq T_{1}+T_{2},
where
P_{1}
and
T_{1}
are the sums corresponding to
t<s
and
P_{2}
and
T_{2},
the sums for
t\geq s.
We shall show that
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are bounded by constants independent of
p,
and
T_{1}\leq cp
and
T_{2}\leq c^{\prime}p
for a pair of constants
c,c^{\prime}>0
independent of
p;
having this, we will conclude
\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{D}_{p}-\mathrm{D}_{p}}G(0,z)G(0,-z)\leq c
and
\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{Q}^{(p)}}G(0,z)^{2}\leq c^{\prime}p
for some constants
c>0
and
c^{\prime}>0,
as desired.

We now prove
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are bounded by constants independent of
p.
Observe from (
4.8
)(g) that for all
z\in\mathrm{R}(s,t),
either
\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}(z)\leq d_{1}e^{-d_{2}3^{t}}3^{-t}
or
\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}(z)\leq d_{1}e^{-d_{2}9^{t-s}}9^{-s}.
Also, for
z\in\mathrm{Q}_{+}^{(p)}
we have
\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}(-z)=c_{1}e^{-c_{2}\left\lVert z\right\rVert}\left\lVert z\right\rVert^{-1}.
Therefore (use (
4.8
)(a)),

\displaystyle\textstyle P_{2}

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq\sum\limits_{s=0}^{p+1}\sum\limits_{t=s}^{p+1}\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{R}(s,t)}\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}(z)\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}(-z)\leq 3c_{1}d_{1}L\sum\limits_{s=0}^{p+1}\sum\limits_{t=s}^{p+1}\big(e^{-d_{2}3^{t}}9^{s}+e^{-d_{2}9^{t-s}}3^{t}\big)e^{-\frac{c_{2}}{3}3^{t}}

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq 3c_{1}d_{1}L\sum\limits_{l=0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{c_{2}}{3}3^{l}}\left(\left[\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-d_{2}3^{k}}\right]9^{l}+\left[\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-d_{2}9^{k}}3^{k}\right]3^{l}\right)<\infty.

The sum
P_{1}
is easier,

\displaystyle\textstyle P_{1}

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq\sum\limits_{s=0}^{p+1}\sum\limits_{t=0}^{s-1}\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{R}(s,t)}\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}(z)\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}(-z)\leq c_{1}d_{1}L\sum\limits_{s=0}^{p+1}\sum\limits_{t=0}^{s-1}9^{s+t}e^{-d_{2}9^{t-s}}9^{-s}e^{-c_{2}9^{s}}9^{-s}

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq c_{1}d_{1}L\sum\limits_{s=0}^{p+1}e^{-c_{2}9^{s}}9^{-s}\sum\limits_{t=0}^{s-1}9^{t}=\frac{c_{1}d_{1}L}{8}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-c_{2}9^{k}}<\infty.

We continue with the proof of the bounds of
T_{1}
and
T_{2},
beginning with
T_{2}\leq cp,
for some constant
c>0.
By definition of
T_{2},

T_{2}=\sum\limits_{s=0}^{p+1}\sum\limits_{t=s}^{p+1}\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{R}(s,t)}\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}(z)^{2}.
As before, we have
\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}(z)^{2}\leq d_{1}^{2}\Big(e^{-2d_{2}3^{t}}9^{-t}+e^{-2d_{2}9^{t-s}}9^{-2s}\Big).
Therefore, with
C=d_{1}^{2}L,

\displaystyle\textstyle T_{2}

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq C\sum\limits_{s=0}^{p+1}\sum\limits_{t=s}^{p+1}\left(e^{-2d_{2}3^{t}}9^{-t}+e^{-2d_{2}9^{t-s}}9^{-2s}\right)9^{s+t}=C\sum\limits_{s=0}^{p+1}\sum\limits_{t=s}^{p+1}\left(e^{-2d_{2}3^{t}}9^{s}+e^{-2d_{2}9^{t-s}}9^{t-s}\right)

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq C\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-d_{2}3^{k}}9^{k}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-d_{2}3^{k}}+C(p+2)\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-2d_{2}9^{k}}9^{k}\leq C^{\prime}p,

where
C^{\prime}=C\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-d_{2}3^{k}}9^{k}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-d_{2}3^{k}}+3C\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{-2d_{2}9^{k}}9^{k}<\infty
is independent of
p.
Similarly, notice one can express
T_{1}=\sum\limits_{s=0}^{p+1}\sum\limits_{t=0}^{s-1}\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{R}(s,t)}\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}(z)^{2},
bearing this in mind and that
\varphi_{\mathrm{U}}(z)\leq d_{1}9^{-s}
for
0\leq t\leq s
it follows immediately that, for some positive constants
c
and
c^{\prime},

T_{1}\leq c\sum\limits_{s=0}^{p+1}\sum\limits_{t=0}^{s}9^{s+t}9^{-2s}\leq c^{\prime}p,
as desired.
∎

4.3.2
Estimates on the Green’s function on some regions of
\Gamma_{2}(\lambda).

Denote with
S
and
S^{\prime}
two independent network random walks of
\Gamma_{2}(\lambda).

Consider the following region (
p\in\mathds{N}
)

(4.10)

\textstyle\mathrm{U}_{p}=\left\{(n,x)\in\mathds{Z}^{3}:|n|\leq 4\cdot 9^{pk_{0}},\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq 3^{(p+1)k_{0}}\right\},

where
k_{0}
is a positive integer to be determined later on. Define the “separating cylinders” (creating a barrier between
\mathrm{D}_{pk_{0}}
and
\mathrm{D}_{(p+1)k_{0}}
for
p\in\mathds{N}
) to be the (vertex) boundary
\mathrm{F}_{p}=\partial\mathrm{U}_{p},
and set
\mathrm{F}_{0}=\{0\}.
Having these sets, define (
p\in\mathds{Z}_{+}
)

(4.11)

\textstyle T_{p}=\tau_{\mathrm{F}_{p}}(S)

the hitting time of
\mathrm{F}_{p}
by
S.
If
z\in\mathds{Z}^{3},
let

(4.12)

\textstyle p_{z}=\min\{p\mid z\in\mathrm{U}_{p}\}.

Having defined
p_{z},
observe that when
S_{0}=o,
then a.s. the sequence of random variables
(T_{p})_{p\geq p_{o}}
is increasing and each one of them is finite (by transience).

Next, by definition
\mathrm{F}_{p}\cap\mathrm{U}_{p}=\varnothing.
It will be useful to divide the set
\mathrm{F}_{p}
into several regions. Geometrically, the set
\mathrm{F}_{p}
can be thought as a cylinder and we need to consider the body and the two bases separately. Analytically, if
(n,x)\in\mathrm{F}_{p}
then
|n|>4\cdot 9^{pk_{0}}
or else
\left\lVert x\right\rVert>3^{(p+1)k_{0}}.
We can specialise further for either
|n|=4\cdot 9^{pk_{0}}+1
and
\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq 3^{(p+1)k_{0}}
or else,
|n|\leq 4\cdot 9^{pk_{0}}
and
3^{(p+1)k_{0}}\leq\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq 3^{(p+1)k_{0}}+1.
Thus, the exponential scales being considered permit us to assume that
\left\lVert z\right\rVert,
for
z\in\mathrm{F}_{p},
satisfies :

(a)

|n|=4\cdot 9^{pk_{0}}
and
\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq 3^{(p+1)k_{0}},
or

(b)

|n|<4\cdot 9^{pk_{0}}
and
\left\lVert x\right\rVert=3^{(p+1)k_{0}}.

With this assumption, we introduce the following sets. The “left-hand base of the cylinder
\mathrm{F}_{p}
” is the set of
z\in\mathrm{F}_{p}
such that
n=-4\cdot 9^{pk_{0}};
the “right-hand base of the cylinder
\mathrm{F}_{p}
” is the set of
z\in\mathrm{F}_{p}
such that
n=4\cdot 9^{pk_{0}}.
The points in
\mathrm{F}_{p}
that belong to neither the left nor the right-hand base of the cylinder will be referred to as the “body of the cylinder
\mathrm{F}_{p},
” in other words, the body of the cylinder is the set of points
z\in\mathrm{F}_{p}
such that
|n|<4\cdot 9^{pk_{0}}
and
\left\lVert x\right\rVert=3^{(p+1)k_{0}}.

Define
(T_{p}^{\prime})
as above changing the process
S
for the process
S^{\prime}.
Observe that
(T_{p})
and
(T_{p}^{\prime})
are independent families.

Having the times
(T_{p})
and
(T_{p}^{\prime})
we now consider “sections” of the network random walks and the number of intersections in
\mathrm{D}_{pk_{0}}
of two such sections. Thus, define (
p\in\mathds{N}
)

(4.13)

\textstyle M_{p}=\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{D}_{pk_{0}}}\sum\limits_{m=T_{p-1}}^{T_{p}}\sum\limits_{m^{\prime}=T_{p-1}^{\prime}}^{T_{p}^{\prime}}\mathds{1}_{\{S_{m}=z\}}\mathds{1}_{\{S_{m^{\prime}}^{\prime}=z\}}.

Observe that
M_{p}
counts the number of intersections of the two random walks inside the region
\mathrm{D}_{pk_{0}}
starting in the separating cylinder
\mathrm{F}_{p-1}.
We remark that, if
S_{0}=o,
then
S
cannot be inside
\mathrm{D}_{pk_{0}}
before the time
T_{p-1}
provided
p-1\geq p_{o};
similarly,
S^{\prime}
cannot be inside
\mathrm{D}_{pk_{o}}
before time
T_{p-1}^{\prime}
provided
S_{0}^{\prime}=o^{\prime}
and
p-1\geq p_{o^{\prime}}.

We will study the Green’s function restricted to the region
\mathrm{U}_{p}:

G_{\mathrm{U}_{p}}(0,z)=\mathds{E}{{}^{0}}\left(\sum\limits_{m=0}^{T_{p}-1}\mathds{1}_{\{S_{m}=z\}}\right),
for
z\in\mathds{Z}^{3}.
For consistency we denote
\mathrm{U}_{0}=\{0\}.
For the sake of simplicity, we will write
G_{p}=G_{\mathrm{U}_{p}}.

Proposition ( 4.14
)

For every
p\in\mathds{N},

\mathrm{D}_{pk_{0}}-\mathrm{U}_{p-1}\subset\mathrm{U}_{p}.
Moreover, for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists an index
k_{0}=k_{0}(\varepsilon)
such that for some
p_{0}\in\mathds{N}
the relations
p\geq p_{0}
and
z\in\mathrm{D}_{pk_{0}}-\mathrm{U}_{p-1}
imply
G(0,z)\leq(1+\varepsilon)G_{p}(0,z).

Proof.

The proof contains several steps. We begin by noticing that for
z_{1}\in\mathrm{D}_{pk_{0}}
and
z_{2}\in\mathrm{U}_{p-1}
one has by (
4.7
) and (
4.10
)
9^{pk_{0}}<n_{1}\leq 2\cdot 9^{pk_{0}}
and
\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert\leq 3^{pk_{0}},
and
-4\cdot 9^{(p-1)k_{0}}\leq n_{2}\leq 4\cdot 9^{(p-1)k_{0}}
and
\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert\leq 3^{pk_{0}},
which implies

( 4.14.1
)

Any
(n,x)\in\mathrm{D}_{pk_{0}}-\mathrm{U}_{p-1}
satisfies
\frac{1}{2}\cdot 9^{pk_{0}}\leq n\leq\frac{5}{2}9^{pk_{0}},\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq 2\cdot 3^{pk_{0}}.

In particular,
z_{1}-z_{2}
belongs to the region
\mathrm{U}_{p}
so this establishes the first assertion in (
4.14
).

We now prove the second assertion of (
4.14
). First,
G(0,z)=G_{p}(0,z)+\mathds{E}{{}^{0}}\left(G(S_{T_{p}},z)\right).
Observe that
S_{T_{p}}
is in
\mathrm{F}_{p}
and, therefore, we need to estimate
G(z_{3},z)
for
z_{3}\in\mathrm{F}_{p}.
This will be done in the upcoming three lemmas.

We will pause momentarily the proof of the proposition in order to establish these lemmas and resume it once they have been proven.

Lemma ( 4.14.2
)

(a)

For every
\varepsilon>0,
there exists a
p_{0}=p_{0}(\varepsilon)
such that for all
p\geq p_{0},
all
k_{0}\in\mathds{N},
and all
z_{1}\in\mathrm{D}_{pk_{0}},z_{2}\in\mathrm{U}_{p-1}
and
z_{3}\in\mathrm{F}_{p}
for which
n_{1}-n_{2}-n_{3}\leq 2\cdot 3^{(p+1)k_{0}}
and
z_{3}
does not lie in the left-hand base of
\mathrm{F}_{p},
we have
G(z_{3},z_{1}-z_{2})\leq\varepsilon G(0,z_{1}-z_{2}).

(b)

There exists a constant
c>0
such that for
p\in\mathds{N}
and all
z_{1}\in\mathrm{D}_{pk_{0}},z_{2}\in\mathrm{U}_{p-1}
and
z_{3}\in\mathrm{F}_{p}
for which
n_{1}-n_{2}-n_{3}\geq 2\cdot 3^{(p+1)k_{0}}
and
z_{3}
does not lie in the left-hand base of
\mathrm{F}_{p}
then
G(z_{3},z_{1}-z_{2})\leq c9^{-k_{0}}G(0,z_{1}-z_{2}).

Proof of lemma.

Suppose the constants
c_{k}
(
1\leq k\leq 4
) of (
2.22
) are given. By (
4.14.1
) and the estimates on the Green’s function, we have
G(0,z_{1}-z_{2})\asymp 9^{-pk_{0}}.

We prove part (a) first. Assume
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}
are as stated. Consider first the case
n_{3}<4\cdot 9^{pk_{0}},
that is to say, consider first when
z_{3}
is not in the right-hand base of
\mathrm{F}_{p}.
Then
\left\lVert x_{3}\right\rVert=3^{(p+1)k_{0}},
which in turn yields
\frac{1}{3}\cdot 3^{(p+1)k_{0}}\leq\left\lVert x_{1}-x_{2}-x_{3}\right\rVert\leq 2\cdot 3^{(p+1)k_{0}}.
Suppose first that
n_{1}-n_{2}-n_{3}\leq\frac{1}{3}\cdot 3^{(p+1)k_{0}},
then
z_{1}-z_{2}-z_{3}
is in
\mathrm{S}_{1},
implying by (
2.22
)

\displaystyle G(z_{3},z_{1}-z_{2})

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}e^{-c_{2}\left\lVert z_{1}-z_{2}-z_{3}\right\rVert}\left\lVert z_{1}-z_{2}-z_{3}\right\rVert^{-1}\leq 3c_{1}e^{-\frac{c_{2}}{3}3^{(p+1)k_{0}}}3^{-(p+1)k_{0}}

\displaystyle=\left[3c_{1}e^{-\frac{c_{2}}{3}3^{(p+1)k_{0}}}3^{(p-1)k_{0}}\right]9^{-pk_{0}}\leq\left[3c_{1}e^{-\frac{c_{2}}{3}3^{pk_{0}}}3^{pk_{0}}\right]9^{-pk_{0}}.

Define
u_{1}(t)=3c_{1}e^{-\frac{c_{2}}{3}3^{t}}3^{t},
so that
G(z_{3},z_{1}-z_{2})\leq u_{1}(pk_{0})9^{-pk_{0}},
for
z_{1},

z_{2}
and
z_{3}
as stated. Next, suppose that
\frac{1}{3}\cdot 3^{(p+1)k_{0}}\leq n_{1}-n_{2}-n_{3}\leq 2\cdot 3^{(p+1)k_{0}}.
Then,

\displaystyle G(z_{3},z_{1}-z_{2})

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}\left(e^{-c_{2}\left\lVert z_{1}-z_{2}-z_{3}\right\rVert}+e^{-c_{2}\frac{\left\lVert x_{1}-x_{2}-x_{3}\right\rVert^{2}}{n_{1}-n_{2}-n_{3}}}\right)\left\lVert z_{1}-z_{2}-z_{3}\right\rVert^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq 3c_{1}\left(e^{-\frac{c_{2}}{3}3^{(p+1)k_{0}}}+e^{-\frac{c_{2}}{18}3^{(p+1)k_{0}}}\right)3^{-(p+1)k_{0}}

\displaystyle=\left[3c_{1}\left(e^{-\frac{c_{2}}{3}3^{(p+1)k_{0}}}+e^{-\frac{c_{2}}{18}3^{(p+1)k_{0}}}\right)3^{(p-1)k_{0}}\right]9^{-pk_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq\left[3c_{1}\left(e^{-\frac{c_{2}}{3}3^{pk_{0}}}+e^{-\frac{c_{2}}{18}3^{pk_{0}}}\right)3^{pk_{0}}\right]9^{-pk_{0}}=u_{2}(pk_{0})9^{-pk_{0}},

where
u_{2}(t)
is defined to be
3c_{1}\left(e^{-\frac{c_{2}}{3}3^{t}}+e^{-\frac{c_{2}}{18}3^{t}}\right)3^{t}.
Consider now the case where
z_{3}
is in the right-hand base of the cylinder, that is to say, assume
n_{3}=4\cdot 9^{pk_{0}}
and
\left\lVert x_{3}\right\rVert\leq 3^{(p+1)k_{0}}
but in this case,
-\frac{7}{2}\cdot 9^{pk_{0}}\leq n_{1}-n_{2}-n_{3}\leq-\frac{3}{2}\cdot 9^{pk_{0}}
and again, by (
2.22
),
G(z_{3},z_{1}-z_{2})\leq c_{1}e^{-c_{2}|n_{1}-n_{2}-n_{3}|}|n_{1}-n_{2}-n_{3}|^{-1}\leq\frac{2c_{1}}{3}e^{-\frac{2c_{2}}{3}9^{pk_{0}}}9^{-pk_{0}}.
Here we set
u_{3}(t)=\frac{2c_{1}}{3}e^{-\frac{2c_{2}}{3}9^{t}},
so that in this case
G(z_{3},z_{1}-z_{2})\leq u_{3}(pk_{0})9^{-pk_{0}}.
Notice that in all three cases above, we bounded
G(z_{3},z_{1}-z_{2})\leq u_{i}(pk_{0})9^{-pk_{0}},
and it is clear that
u_{i}(t)\to 0
as
t\to\infty
in each case. Then, there exists a
t_{0}
such that
\sup\limits_{t\geq t_{0}}\max\limits_{i=1,2,3}u_{i}(t)\leq\varepsilon.
We can define
p_{0}
to be the least integer greater than or equal to
t_{0};
this
p_{0}
works for all
k_{0}
since
pk_{0}\geq p.
This finishes part (a).

We now prove part (b). Suppose that
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}
are as stated in (b). This implies the relation
n_{1}-n_{2}-n_{3}\geq 2\cdot 3^{(p+1)k_{0}}.
By (
4.14.1
), we have
n_{1}-n_{2}-n_{3}\leq 7\cdot 9^{pk_{0}}
and, therefore
n_{1}-n_{2}-n_{3}
lies in an interval of the form
\mathrm{J}_{\alpha}=\left[(\alpha-1)3^{(p+1)k_{0}},\alpha 3^{(p+1)k_{0}}\right]
where
\alpha=3,\ldots,7\cdot 3^{(p-1)k_{0}}.
Regardless the value of
\alpha,
the relation
n_{1}-n_{2}-n_{3}\in\mathrm{J}_{\alpha}
implies
z_{1}-z_{2}-z_{3}\in\mathrm{S}_{2}
and these relations imply,

G(z_{3},z_{1}-z_{2})\leq c_{1}e^{-c_{2}\frac{\left\lVert x_{1}-x_{2}-x_{3}\right\rVert^{2}}{n_{1}-n_{2}-n_{3}}}(n_{1}-n_{2}-n_{3})^{-1}\leq 2c_{1}e^{-\frac{c_{2}}{9}\alpha^{-1}3^{(p+1)k_{0}}}\alpha^{-1}3^{-(p+1)k_{0}}.

Observe that for any constant
c>0,
the function
u_{c}(t)=t^{-1}e^{-ct^{-1}}
for
t>0
has an absolute and global maximum at
t=c.
Its maximum equals
u_{c}(c)=e^{-1}c^{-1}.
We deduce
G(z_{3},z_{1}-z_{2})\leq 2c_{1}e^{-1}\frac{9}{c_{2}}9^{-(p+1)k_{0}}=c^{\prime}9^{-k_{0}}9^{-pk_{0}},
the desired conclusion since
G(0,z_{1}-z_{2})\asymp 9^{-pk_{0}},
with any implicit constant independent of
p
and of
k_{0}.

∎

Lemma ( 4.14.3
)

For any
\varepsilon>0,
there exist indices
k_{0}=k_{0}(\varepsilon)\in\mathds{N}
and
p_{0}=p_{0}(\varepsilon)\in\mathds{N}
such that the relations
p\geq p_{0}
and
z\in\mathrm{D}_{pk_{0}}-\mathrm{U}_{p-1}
imply
\sup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z_{3}\in\mathrm{F}_{p}\\
n_{3}>-4\cdot 9^{pk_{0}}\end{subarray}}G(z_{3},z)\leq\varepsilon G(0,z).

Proof of lemma.

By (
4.14.2
), given
\varepsilon>0
there is a
p_{0}\in\mathds{N}
such that if
p\geq p_{0},

k_{0}\in\mathds{N}
and for all
z_{1}\in\mathrm{D}_{pk_{0}},

z_{2}\in\mathrm{U}_{p-1}
and
z_{3}\in\mathrm{F}_{p}
with
n_{3}>-4\cdot 9^{p},
we either have
G(z_{3},z_{1}-z_{2})\leq\varepsilon G(0,z_{1}-z_{2})
or
G(z_{3},z_{1}-z_{2})\leq c9^{-k_{0}}G(0,z_{1}-z_{2}),
where the constant
c
is universal. Choose
k_{0}\in\mathds{N}
such that
c9^{-k_{0}}\leq\varepsilon.
Hence,
G(z_{3},z_{1}-z_{2})\leq\varepsilon G(0,z_{1}-z_{2})
uniformly in
z_{3}\in\mathrm{F}_{p}
with
n_{3}>-4\cdot 9^{p}
for all
p\geq p_{0},
which is the conclusion to be reached.
∎

Lemma ( 4.14.4
)

For any
\varepsilon>0,
there exist indices
k_{0}=k_{0}(\varepsilon)\in\mathds{N}
and
p_{0}=p_{0}(\varepsilon)\in\mathds{N}
such that the relation
p\geq p_{0}
implies
\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(S_{T_{p}}\in\{z\in\mathrm{F}_{p}:n=-4\cdot 9^{p}\}\right)\leq\varepsilon.
Also, there is a universal constant
c>0
such that for all
p\in\mathds{N}
and all
z\in\mathrm{D}_{pk_{0}}-\mathrm{U}_{p-1},

\sup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z_{3}\in\mathrm{F}_{p}\\
n_{3}=-4\cdot 9^{p}\end{subarray}}G(z_{3},z)\leq cG(0,z).

Proof of lemma.

Set
\mathrm{A}_{p}=\{z\in\mathrm{F}_{p}:n=-4\cdot 9^{pk_{0}}\}.
Consider the network random walk
S
in
\Gamma_{2}(\lambda)
started at zero and define
B=\mathrm{pr}_{0}(S).
By (
2.17
),
\mathds{P}{}\left(S_{T_{p}}\in\mathrm{A}_{p}\right)\leq\mathds{P}{}\left(B\text{ ever reaches }-4\cdot 9^{pk_{0}}\right)=e^{-4\lambda 9^{pk_{0}}}.
This proved the first claim. To prove the second claim, use (
4.14.1
), if
z_{3}\in\mathrm{A}_{p}
and
z\in\mathrm{D}_{pk_{0}}-\mathrm{U}_{p-1}
then
G(z_{3},z)\asymp 9^{-pk_{0}}
with any implicit constant being universal in
p,k_{0},z,z_{3}.
Similarly,
G(0,z)\asymp 9^{-pk_{0}}
for
z\in\mathrm{D}_{pk_{0}}-\mathrm{U}_{p-1}
with any implicit constant universal. The second claim follows readily.
∎

We are ready to continue with the proof of Proposition (
4.14
). Let us write
\mathrm{A}_{p}=\{z\in\mathrm{F}_{p}:n_{3}=-4\cdot 9^{p}\}
for the left-hand base of
\mathrm{F}_{p}
and
\mathrm{B}_{p}=\mathrm{F}_{p}\setminus\mathrm{A}_{p}
for the remainder of the separating cylinder. For the time being, consider
\varepsilon>0
to be any positive number. By Lemmas (
4.14.2
) and (
4.14.4
), there are indices
k_{0}
and
p_{0},
only depending on
\varepsilon,
and a universal constant
c>0
(not depending on any index) satisfying for all
p\geq p_{0},

z_{1}\in\mathrm{D}_{pk_{0}},

z_{2}\in\mathrm{U}_{p-1}

\sup\limits_{z_{3}\in\mathrm{B}_{p}}G(z_{3},z_{1}-z_{2})\leq\varepsilon G(0,z_{1}-z_{2}),

\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(S_{T_{p}}\in\mathrm{A}_{p}\right)\leq\varepsilon
and for all
z\in\mathrm{D}_{pk_{0}}-\mathrm{U}_{p-1},

\sup\limits_{z_{3}\in\mathrm{A}_{p}}G(z_{3},z)\leq cG(0,z).
Write now, for
z\in\mathrm{D}_{pk_{0}}-\mathrm{U}_{p-1},

\displaystyle G(0,z)

\displaystyle=G_{p}(0,z)+\mathds{E}{{}^{0}}\left(G(S_{T_{p}},z)\mathds{1}_{\mathrm{A}_{p}}(S_{T_{p}})\right)+\mathds{E}{{}^{0}}\left(G(S_{T_{p}},z)\mathds{1}_{\mathrm{B}_{p}}(S_{T_{p}})\right)

\displaystyle\leq G_{p}(0,z)+cG(0,z)\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(S_{T_{p}}\in\mathrm{A}_{p}\right)+\varepsilon G(0,z)\mathds{P}{{}^{0}}\left(S_{T_{p}}\in\mathrm{B}_{p}\right)

\displaystyle\leq G_{p}(0,z)+(c+1)\varepsilon G(0,z).

Hence, for any
\varepsilon>0,
there are indices
k_{0}
and
p_{0},
depending only on
\varepsilon,
so that the relations
p\geq p_{0}
and
z\in\mathrm{D}_{pk_{0}}-\mathrm{U}_{p-1}
imply
G(0,z)\leq G_{p}(0,z)+\frac{\varepsilon}{1+\varepsilon}G(0,z),
which yields
G(0,z)\leq(1+\varepsilon)G_{p}(0,z).

∎

4.4
Dimension
d=2;
“logarithmic scale of crossings” of
\Gamma_{2}(\lambda)

We will establish a
lower bound
on the probability of how frequently two random walk paths in
\Gamma_{2}(\lambda)
will cross each other.

Invoking Proposition (
4.14
), for
\varepsilon=1
there exists two indices
k_{0}
and
p_{0},
depending only on
\varepsilon,
such that the conclusions of this proposition hold. For the remainder of this chapter, we assume both
k_{0}
and
p_{0}
are fixed and given as stated.

Theorem ( 4.15
)

Consider two independent network random walks
S
and
S^{\prime}
in
\Gamma_{2}(\lambda),
with respective starting points
o
and
o^{\prime}.
Define the random variables
T_{p}
and
T_{p}^{\prime}
in (
4.11
) using
S
and
S^{\prime},
accordingly; now introduce
M_{p}
as in (
4.13
). Consider now the
\sigma
-fields
\mathscr{E}_{p}=\mathscr{F}^{S}_{T_{p}}\vee\mathscr{F}^{S^{\prime}}_{T_{p}^{\prime}}.
Define the event
\mathrm{A}_{p}=\{M_{p}>0\}
and the random variable
q_{p+1}=\mathds{P}{}\left(\mathrm{A}_{p+1}\mid\mathscr{E}_{p}\right).
Then,
\mathrm{A}_{p}\in\mathscr{E}_{p}
and there exists a positive integer
P=P(o,o^{\prime})
and a universal constant
c>0
(depending neither on
o
nor on
o^{\prime}
) such that, almost surely,
q_{p}>\frac{c}{p}
for all
p\geq P.

Proof.

It is clear that
\mathrm{A}_{p}\in\mathscr{E}_{p}.
Consider
p_{1}=\max(p_{o},p_{o^{\prime}})
(
4.12
), that is,
p_{1}
is the first index such that
o
and
o^{\prime}
belong to
\mathrm{U}_{p}.
Suppose
p
is any integer
\geq\max(p_{0},p_{1}).
By (
5.3
), we have
q_{p+1}=\mathds{P}{}\left(\mathrm{A}_{p+1}\middle|S_{T_{p}},S_{T_{p}^{\prime}}^{\prime}\right).
It is now straightforward to check that

(
\ast
)

\textstyle q_{p+1}=\sum\limits_{(z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathrm{F}_{p}^{2}}\mathds{P}{}\left(\mathrm{A}_{p+1}\middle|S_{T_{p}}=z_{1},S_{T_{p}^{\prime}}^{\prime}=z_{2}\right)\mathds{1}_{\left\{S_{T_{p}}=z_{1},S_{T_{p}^{\prime}}^{\prime}=z_{2}\right\}}.

We will use (
4.3
). By the strong Markov property for a pair of independent Markov processes (
5.3
) gives (inside the expectation on the right of the first equality we use abuse of notation and assume
(S_{m})
and
(S_{m^{\prime}}^{\prime})
are independent network random walks started at zero)

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathds{E}{}\left(M_{p+1}\middle|S_{T_{p}}=z_{1},S_{T_{p}^{\prime}}^{\prime}=z_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\textstyle=\sum\limits_{z_{3}\in\mathrm{D}_{(p+1)k_{0}}}\mathds{E}{}\left(\sum\limits_{m=0}^{T_{p+1}}\sum\limits_{m^{\prime}=0}^{T_{p+1}^{\prime}}\mathds{1}_{\{S_{m}+z_{1}=z_{3}\}}\mathds{1}_{\left\{S_{m^{\prime}}^{\prime}+z_{2}=z_{3}\right\}}\right)

\displaystyle\textstyle=\sum\limits_{z_{3}\in\mathrm{D}_{(p+1)k_{0}}}G_{p+1}(0,z_{3}-z_{1})G_{p+1}(0,z_{3}-z_{2})

\displaystyle\textstyle\asymp\sum\limits_{z_{3}\in\mathrm{D}_{(p+1)k_{0}}}G(0,z_{3}-z_{1})G(0,z_{3}-z_{2}),\quad\text{by }(\ref{Proposition: Asymptotic bound between the Green function and the restricted Green function on d equals 2})

\displaystyle\textstyle\asymp\sum\limits_{z_{3}\in\mathrm{D}_{(p+1)k_{0}}}9^{-2(p+1)k_{0}}\asymp 1,\quad\text{ by }(\ref{Remark: Principal calculations for the number of crossings in dimension d equals 2})(k).

We shall now estimate the second moment of
M_{p}
with respect to the conditional measure. We have

\textstyle M_{p+1}^{2}=\sum\limits_{(z_{3},z_{4})\in\mathrm{D}_{(p+1)k_{0}}^{2}}\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}=T_{p}}^{T_{p+1}}\sum\limits_{m_{1}^{\prime},m_{2}^{\prime}=T_{p}^{\prime}}^{T_{p+1}^{\prime}}\mathds{1}_{\{S_{m_{1}}=z_{3},S_{m_{2}}=z_{4}\}}\mathds{1}_{\left\{S_{m_{1}^{\prime}}^{\prime}=z_{3},S_{m_{2}^{\prime}}^{\prime}=z_{4}\right\}}.

Now apply the conditional expectation, by the strong Markov property (
5.3
) and independence between
S
and
S^{\prime}
(we again employ abuse of notation and assume
S
and
S^{\prime}
are two independent network random walks started at zero after having used the strong Markov property for two independent Markov processes)

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathds{E}{}\left(M_{p+1}^{2}\middle|S_{T_{p}}=z_{1},S_{T_{p}^{\prime}}^{\prime}=z_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\textstyle=\sum\limits_{(z_{3},z_{4})\in\mathrm{D}_{(p+1)k_{0}}^{2}}\mathds{E}{}\left(\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}=0}^{T_{p+1}}\mathds{1}_{\{S_{m_{1}}=z_{3}-z_{1},S_{m_{2}}=z_{4}-z_{1}\}}\right)

\displaystyle\textstyle\times\mathds{E}{}\left(\sum\limits_{m_{1}^{\prime},m_{2}^{\prime}=0}^{T_{p+1}^{\prime}}\mathds{1}_{\left\{S_{m_{1}^{\prime}}^{\prime}=z_{3}-z_{2},S_{m_{2}^{\prime}}^{\prime}=z_{4}-z_{2}\right\}}\right)

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq B_{1}+B_{2}+B_{3}+B_{4},

in which

\begin{split}B_{1}&\textstyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{1}\leq m_{2}\\
m_{1}^{\prime}\leq m_{2}^{\prime}\\
(z_{3},z_{4})\in\mathrm{D}_{(p+1)k_{0}}^{2}\end{subarray}}\mathds{P}{}\left(S_{m_{1}}=z_{3}-z_{1},S_{m_{2}}=z_{4}-z_{1}\right)\mathds{P}{}\left(S_{m_{1}^{\prime}}^{\prime}=z_{3}-z_{2},S_{m_{2}^{\prime}}^{\prime}=z_{4}-z_{2}\right),\\
B_{2}&\textstyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{1}\leq m_{2}\\
m_{1}^{\prime}\geq m_{2}^{\prime}\\
(z_{3},z_{4})\in\mathrm{D}_{(p+1)k_{0}}^{2}\end{subarray}}\mathds{P}{}\left(S_{m_{1}}=z_{3}-z_{1},S_{m_{2}}=z_{4}-z_{1}\right)\mathds{P}{}\left(S_{m_{1}^{\prime}}^{\prime}=z_{3}-z_{2},S_{m_{2}^{\prime}}^{\prime}=z_{4}-z_{2}\right),\\
B_{3}&\textstyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{1}\geq m_{2}\\
m_{1}^{\prime}\leq m_{2}^{\prime}\\
(z_{3},z_{4})\in\mathrm{D}_{(p+1)k_{0}}^{2}\end{subarray}}\mathds{P}{}\left(S_{m_{1}}=z_{3}-z_{1},S_{m_{2}}=z_{4}-z_{1}\right)\mathds{P}{}\left(S_{m_{1}^{\prime}}^{\prime}=z_{3}-z_{2},S_{m_{2}^{\prime}}^{\prime}=z_{4}-z_{2}\right),\end{split}

and

\textstyle B_{4}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{1}\geq m_{2}\\
m_{1}^{\prime}\geq m_{2}^{\prime}\\
(z_{3},z_{4})\in\mathrm{D}_{(p+1)k_{0}}^{2}\end{subarray}}\mathds{P}{}\left(S_{m_{1}}=z_{3}-z_{1},S_{m_{2}}=z_{4}-z_{1}\right)\mathds{P}{}\left(S_{m_{1}^{\prime}}^{\prime}=z_{3}-z_{2},S_{m_{2}^{\prime}}^{\prime}=z_{4}-z_{2}\right).

We handle each sum separately.

(a)

By independent increments and writing
p^{k}(z,z^{\prime})=\mathds{P}{}\left(S_{k}=z^{\prime}\mid S_{0}=z\right),

\displaystyle B_{1}

\displaystyle\textstyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{1}\leq m_{2}\\
m_{1}^{\prime}\leq m_{2}^{\prime}\\
(z_{3},z_{4})\in\mathrm{D}_{(p+1)k_{0}}^{2}\end{subarray}}p^{m_{1}}(0,z_{3}-z_{1})p^{m_{2}-m_{1}}(0,z_{4}-z_{3})p^{m_{1}^{\prime}}(0,z_{3}-z_{2})p^{m_{2}^{\prime}-m_{1}^{\prime}}(0,z_{4}-z_{3})

\displaystyle\textstyle=\sum\limits_{(z_{3},z_{4})\in\mathrm{D}_{(p+1)k_{0}}^{2}}G(0,z_{3}-z_{1})G(0,z_{4}-z_{3})^{2}G(0,z_{3}-z_{2}),

the last equality being an immediate consequence of Lebesgue-Tonelli’s theorem and the definition of the Green’s function of
\Gamma_{2}(\lambda).
By virtue of (
4.14.1
) and (
4.9
) we can proceed further,

\displaystyle B_{1}

\displaystyle\textstyle\asymp\sum\limits_{(z_{3},z_{4})\in\mathrm{D}_{(p+1)k_{0}}^{2}}9^{-2(p+1)k_{0}}G(0,z_{4}-z_{3})^{2}\leq ck_{0}(p+1)\sum\limits_{z_{3}\in\mathrm{D}_{(p+1)k_{0}}}9^{-2(p+1)k_{0}}\asymp p.

(b)

Here we get

\displaystyle B_{2}

\displaystyle\textstyle=\sum\limits_{(z_{3},z_{4})\in\mathrm{D}_{(p+1)k_{0}}^{2}}G(0,z_{3}-z_{1})G(0,z_{4}-z_{3})G(0,z_{4}-z_{2})G(0,z_{3}-z_{4})

\displaystyle\textstyle\asymp\sum\limits_{z_{3}\in\mathrm{D}_{(p+1)k_{0}}}\sum\limits_{z_{4}\in\mathrm{D}_{(p+1)k_{0}}}9^{-2(p+1)k_{0}}G(0,z_{4}-z_{3})G(0,z_{3}-z_{4})

\displaystyle\textstyle\asymp\sum\limits_{z_{3}\in\mathrm{D}_{(p+1)k_{0}}}9^{-2(p+1)k_{0}}\asymp 1,

where the second
\asymp
is a consequence of (
4.9
).

(c)

Similarly to
B_{2},
we get
B_{3}\asymp 1.

(d)

As with
B_{1},

B_{4}\leq cp,
for some universal constant
c>0.

Therefore, we have shown that there exist two positive constants
c
and
c^{\prime},
such that, whenever
p\geq\max(p_{0},p_{1})
and
(z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathrm{F}_{p}^{2},

\mathds{E}{}\left(M_{p+1}\middle|S_{T_{p}}=z_{1},S_{T_{p}^{\prime}}^{\prime}=z_{2}\right)\geq c
and
\mathds{E}{}\left(M_{p+1}^{2}\middle|S_{T_{p}}=z_{1},S_{T_{p}^{\prime}}^{\prime}=z_{2}\right)\leq c^{\prime}p.
By (
4.3
), we finally reach that there exists a constant
c>0
and a
P=\max(p_{0},p_{1})
such that, for all
p\geq P
and all
(z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathrm{F}_{p}^{2},

\mathds{P}{}\left(\mathrm{A}_{p+1}\middle|S_{T_{p}}=z_{1},S_{T_{p}^{\prime}}^{\prime}=z_{2}\right)\geq\frac{c}{p},
comparing with the expression for
q_{p+1}
in (
\ast
), we have substantiated the claims.
∎

Corollary ( 4.15.1
)

Let
S
and
S^{\prime}
be two independent network random walks in
\Gamma_{2}(\lambda),
with starting points
S_{0}=o
and
S_{0}^{\prime}=o^{\prime}.
There is a universal constant
c>0
and an index
P=P(o,o^{\prime}),
such that for all
p\geq P,
the probability that the two random walk paths
(S_{m})_{m=T_{p-1},\ldots,T_{p}}
and
(S_{m^{\prime}}^{\prime})_{m^{\prime}=T_{p-1}^{\prime},\ldots,T_{p}^{\prime}}
cross each other inside
\mathrm{D}_{pk_{0}}
is at least
\frac{c}{p}.

The following corollary follows using (
4.4
) and (
4.15
).

Corollary ( 4.15.2
)

Any two independent network random walks on
\Gamma_{2}(\lambda)
will have infinitely many intersections.

Remark ( 4.16
)

There is a much simpler way to obtain Corollary (
4.15.2
). Denote
S=(B,X),
that is,
S_{n}=(B_{n},X_{n})
for
n\in\mathds{Z}_{+};
here
B
is the biased random walk on
\mathds{Z}
and
X
a random walk on
\mathds{Z}^{2}.
Define
\zeta_{k}=\tau_{\{k\}\times\mathds{Z}^{2}}(S)
for
k\in\mathds{Z}_{+},
which is the time at which the random walk
B
visits
k.
With these hitting times, consider
Y_{n}=X_{\zeta_{n}}.
Similarly, define
Y_{n}^{\prime}
using
S^{\prime}=(B^{\prime},X^{\prime}),
which is an independent copy of
S.
In a similar manner as theorem (
4.2
), it is seen that
Y_{n}=Y_{n}^{\prime}
for infinitely many indices
n.
Having this, it is now clear that
S_{n}=S_{n^{\prime}}^{\prime}
for infinitely many pairs
(n,n^{\prime}).
Of course, this result is much coarser than (
4.15.1
).

5
One endedness for
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)

Let
\mathrm{G}
be a directed infinite tree. A
ray
is an infinite path that does not repeat vertices; any two rays of
\mathrm{G}
will be disjoint or will eventually merge, this creates equivalence classes on the set of rays of
\mathrm{G}.
Any of these classes is called an
end
.

5.1
One endedness for
\Gamma_{1}(\lambda)

We refer the reader to
[
LP16
]
, see Section 6.5 , Section 9.2 and Section 10.3 for the definitions and notations of planar graph and duality of graphs.

Theorem ( 5.1
)

\mathsf{UST}
in
\Gamma_{1}(\lambda)
(
3.9
) has, almost surely, one end.

Proof.

It is easy to see that the dual network
\Gamma_{1}(\lambda)^{\dagger}
of
\Gamma_{1}(\lambda)
is the network image of the latter under the reflection
(n,x)\mapsto(-n,x).
In particular, the wired and free uniform spanning forest of
\Gamma_{1}(\lambda)^{\dagger}
coincide and the number of
\mathsf{USF}
trees in
\Gamma_{1}(\lambda)^{\dagger}
is the same as that of
\Gamma_{1}(\lambda);
that is to say, for almost every realisation, just one tree, by (
3.9
). Bearing this in mind and the duality between
\mathsf{UST}
in
\Gamma_{1}(\lambda)
with
\mathsf{UST}
in
\Gamma_{1}(\lambda)^{\dagger},
we see at once that the tree in
\Gamma_{1}(\lambda)
is almost surely one ended; for if it were two ended, it would split the plane into two disconnected regions making it impossible for the dual tree to be a tree.
∎

Remark ( 5.2
)

The core idea of the proof of previous theorem is implicit in
[
LP16
, Theorem 10.36]
.

5.2
One endedness for
\Gamma_{2}(\lambda)

We will prove the following theorem:
\mathsf{UST}
in
\Gamma_{2}(\lambda)
has, almost surely, one end.

The proof is rather involved and to facilitate the reading of it we are going to develop it in several steps and draw the conclusion at the end.

5.3
Some preliminary results on Markov processes

Consider a discrete time Markov process
(S_{n},\mathscr{F}_{n})_{n\in\mathds{Z}_{+}},
with values on a metrisable separable space
\mathrm{E},
endowed with Borels sets
\mathscr{E}
as
\sigma
-algebra. Let
\eta
be an a.s. finite stopping time for this Markov process. We may construct the
\sigma
-field
\mathscr{F}_{\eta}=\mathscr{F}_{\eta}^{S}
of all events
\mathrm{G}\in\mathscr{E}
such that
\mathrm{G}\cap\{\eta\leq n\}\in\mathscr{F}_{n}
(“stopped filtration up to time
\eta
”). In a similar way, we may construct
S_{\eta}:\omega\mapsto S_{\eta(\omega)}(\omega).
If no filtration is specified, it is assumed the canonical filtration is used
\mathscr{F}_{n}^{S}=\sigma(S_{k};0\leq k\leq n).
Consider now the product space
\mathrm{E}^{\mathds{Z}_{+}},
which is also a metrisable separable space, and its Borel
\sigma
-field coincides with
\displaystyle\bigotimes_{n\in\mathds{Z}_{+}}\mathscr{E}.
For any bounded measurable function
\varphi:\mathrm{E}^{\mathds{Z}_{+}}\to\mathds{R},
there exists a version of the conditional expectation
\mathds{E}{}\left(\varphi\left(S_{j}\right)_{j\in\mathds{Z}_{+}}\middle|S_{0}=x\right);
denote this function as
\mathds{E}{{}^{x}}\left(\varphi\left(S_{j}\right)_{j\in\mathds{Z}_{+}}\right).
The strong Markov property states that
\mathds{E}{}\left(\varphi\left((S_{j})_{j\geq\eta}\right)\middle|\mathscr{F}_{\eta}\right)=\mathds{E}{{}^{S_{\eta}}}\left(\varphi\left((S_{j})_{j\in\mathds{Z}_{+}}\right)\right).

The following results follow from well-known techniques (monotone-class, Dynkin’s theorem, etc.). The reader may consult
[
MD20
]
, propositions (1.12.3) and (1.12.4).

Proposition ( 5.3
)

Suppose
S=(S_{n})_{n\in\mathds{Z}_{+}}
and
S^{\prime}=(S_{n}^{\prime})_{n\in\mathds{Z}_{+}}
are two independent Markov processes, defined on some probability space
\left(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathds{P}\right)
with values on some metrisable, separable space
\mathrm{E},
endowed with Borel
\sigma
-field
\mathscr{E}.
Let
\psi:\mathrm{E}^{\mathds{Z}_{+}}\times\mathrm{E}^{\mathds{Z}_{+}}\to\mathds{R}
be a bounded measurable function, relative to the Borel sets of both
\mathds{R}
and
\mathrm{E}^{\mathds{Z}_{+}}\times\mathrm{E}^{\mathds{Z}_{+}}.
Define
v_{\psi}(x,x^{\prime})=\mathds{E}{}\left(\psi\left(S_{j},S_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime}\right)_{(j,j^{\prime})\in\mathds{Z}_{+}^{2}}\middle|S_{0}=x,S_{0}^{\prime}=x^{\prime}\right),
for
(x,x^{\prime})\in\mathrm{E}^{2},
that is
v_{\psi}
is a version of
\psi\left(S_{j},S_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime}\right)_{(j,j^{\prime})\in\mathds{Z}_{+}^{2}}
given
(S_{0},S_{0}^{\prime}).
Suppose
\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
are two stopping times,
\eta
relative to
S
and
\eta^{\prime},
to
S^{\prime}.
With
\psi
as before,
\mathds{E}{}\left(\psi(S_{j},S_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime})_{j\geq\eta,j^{\prime}\geq\eta^{\prime}}\middle|\mathscr{F}_{\eta}^{S}\vee\mathscr{F}_{\eta^{\prime}}^{S^{\prime}}\right)=v_{\psi}(S_{\eta},S_{\eta}^{\prime}).

Proposition ( 5.4
)

Let
\mathrm{E}=\mathds{R}^{d}
(or any separable, metrisable, Fréchet space). Suppose
(S_{n})_{n\in\mathds{Z}_{+}}
and
(S^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathds{Z}_{+}}
are two
\mathrm{E}
-valued stochastic processes with stationary independent increments. Suppose that
\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
are stopping times relative to
S
and
S^{\prime},
respectively. Then,
\mathscr{F}_{\eta}^{S}\vee\mathscr{F}_{\eta^{\prime}}^{S^{\prime}}
is independent of
\mathscr{G}_{\eta}^{S}\vee\mathscr{G}_{\eta^{\prime}}^{S^{\prime}},
with
\mathscr{G}_{\eta}^{S}=\sigma(S_{\eta+j}-S_{\eta};j\in\mathds{Z}_{+}),
and a corresponding definition for
\mathscr{G}_{\eta^{\prime}}^{S^{\prime}}
with
(S^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
replacing
(S,\eta).
In particular, if
\EuScript{A}
is an event depending on the paths
(S_{j})_{0\leq j\leq\eta}
and
(S_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime})_{0\leq j^{\prime}\leq\eta^{\prime}}
and
\EuScript{B},
depending on the paths
(S_{\eta+j}-S_{\eta})_{j\in\mathds{Z}_{+}}
and
(S_{\eta^{\prime}+j^{\prime}}^{\prime}-S_{\eta^{\prime}}^{\prime})_{j^{\prime}\in\mathds{Z}_{+}},
then
\EuScript{A}
and
\EuScript{B}
are independent.

5.3.1
Invariance of the chronology of Wilson’s algorithm rooted at infinity.

We start with a theorem related to the study of the chronological construction of Wilson’s algorithm rooted at infinity on general networks. Denote by
\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{G}}
the (metrisable compact) topological space of spanning subgraphs of the graph
\mathrm{G}
and recall the notations from the introduction. With these notations, we construct the following random object
\left(\mathfrak{F}^{\xi},\left(L_{k}^{\xi}\right)_{k\in\mathds{N}}\right)=\left(\mathfrak{F}^{\xi},L_{1}^{\xi},\ldots,L_{k}^{\xi},\ldots\right).
This random object is
\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{G}}\times\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathds{N}}=\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathds{Z}_{+}}
-valued. Notice that the sequence
\left(L_{k}^{\xi}\right)_{k\in\mathds{N}}
may be referred to as a “chronology of the
\mathsf{WSF}
-forest.” Wilson’s algorithm rooted at infinity gives
\mathfrak{F}^{\xi}\sim\mathfrak{F}^{\eta}.
Observe that if
\xi
and
\eta
are two orderings satisfying
\xi(j)=\eta(j)
for
1\leq j\leq M
then
\left(L_{1}^{\xi},\ldots,L_{M}^{\xi}\right)=\left(L_{1}^{\eta},\ldots,L_{M}^{\eta}\right)
a.s.. Furthermore, in the finite case we may run Wilson’s algorithm with stacks (
1.1
) to obtain that
\mathfrak{F}^{\xi}=\mathfrak{F}^{\eta}
a.s., thus in the finite case
\left(\mathfrak{F}^{\xi},L_{1}^{\xi},\ldots,L_{M}^{\xi}\right)=\left(\mathfrak{F}^{\eta},L_{1}^{\eta},\ldots,L_{M}^{\eta}\right).

Theorem ( 5.5
)

If
\xi
and
\eta
are two orderings of the vertex set of
\mathrm{G}
such that
\xi(j)=\eta(j)
for
1\leq j\leq M,
then
\left(\mathfrak{F}^{\xi},L_{1}^{\xi},\ldots,L_{M}^{\xi}\right)\sim\left(\mathfrak{F}^{\eta},L_{1}^{\eta},\ldots,L_{M}^{\eta}\right).
(The two random objects have the same law.)

The proof of this theorem follows from finite approximation, see
[
MD20
, Theorem (5.2.1)]
.

Corollary ( 5.5.1
)

The statistical properties of events depending on
\mathsf{WSF}
and its first branches are not affected by reordering vertices not yet searched. In other words, if
\varphi:\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{G}}^{M+1}\to\mathds{R}
is any bounded measurable function or any non negative measurable function, and
\xi,\eta:\mathds{N}\to\mathrm{V}
are two orderings of the vertices such that
\xi(j)=\eta(j)
for
1\leq j\leq M,
then
\mathds{E}{}\left(\varphi\left(\mathfrak{F}^{\xi},L_{1}^{\xi},\ldots,L_{M}^{\xi}\right)\right)=\mathds{E}{}\left(\varphi\left(\mathfrak{F}^{\eta},L_{1}^{\eta},\ldots,L_{M}^{\eta}\right)\right).

The importance of the previous corollary is that one can see a partial forest, and to estimate
probabilities
of successive branches, we can choose the next vertices to depend on the partial tree. (This is no surprise, since Wilson’s algorithm with stacks allows for a stronger result in the finite case: the next vertices may depend on the partial tree and the final tree cannot change.)

5.3.2
Preliminary estimates in the proof of one-endedness for
\Gamma_{2}(\lambda).

We denote, for
r>0,

\textstyle\mathrm{B}\left(x;r\right)=\{y\in\mathds{Z}^{d}\mid\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert\leq r\},\quad\mathrm{S}\left(x;r\right)=\{y\in\mathds{Z}^{d}\mid r\leq\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert\leq r+1\}.

We remark that there exists a constant
L=L(d)>0
such that for all
x\in\mathds{Z}^{d}
and all
r\geq 1,

\textstyle\max\big(\mathrm{card}\left(\mathrm{B}\left(x;r\right)\right),\mathrm{card}\left(\mathrm{S}\left(x;r\right)\right)\big)\leq L\cdot r^{d}.

Also,
\mathrm{B}\left(x;r\right)=x+\mathrm{B}\left(0;r\right)
and
\mathrm{S}\left(x;r\right)=x+\mathrm{S}\left(0;r\right).
Further, we show that
\partial\mathrm{B}\left(x;r\right)\subset\mathrm{S}\left(x;r\right).
To see this, suffices to show this when
x=0.
In this case, the relation
y\in\partial\mathrm{B}\left(0;r\right)
signifies there exists a
y^{\prime}\in\mathrm{B}\left(0;r\right)
such that
\left\lVert y-y^{\prime}\right\rVert=1,
and then
\left\lVert y\right\rVert\leq r+1.
The inequality
\left\lVert y\right\rVert\geq r
for
y\in\partial\mathrm{B}\left(0;r\right)
follows from definition of the vertex boundary of a set.

Proposition ( 5.6
)

For each
p\in\mathds{N},
define the sets
\mathrm{A}_{p}=[-p,p]\times\mathrm{S}\left(0;p\right),

\mathrm{B}_{p}=\{p\}\times\mathrm{B}\left(0;p\right)
and
\mathrm{C}_{p}=\{-p\}\times\mathrm{B}\left(0;p\right).
Then, there is a pair of constants
c,c^{\prime}>0
such that for all
p\in\mathds{N},

\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{A}_{p}\cup\mathrm{B}_{p}}G(z,0)\leq ce^{-c^{\prime}p}.
Furthermore, there exists a another pair of constants
c,c^{\prime}>0
and a positive number
B,
obeying the following: for every
b\geq B
we can split
\mathrm{C}_{p}=\mathrm{C}_{p,0}\cup\mathrm{C}_{p,1},
where
\mathrm{C}_{p,0}=\mathrm{C}_{p,0}(b)=\left(\{-p\}\times\mathrm{B}\left(0;bp^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)\right)\cap\mathrm{C}_{p}
and
\mathrm{C}_{p,1}=\mathrm{C}_{p,1}(b)=\mathrm{C}_{p}\setminus\mathrm{C}_{p,0},
and with this division,
\sup\limits_{p\in\mathds{N}}\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{C}_{p,1}}G(z,0)\leq ce^{-c^{\prime}b^{2}}.
In particular, for any
\varepsilon>0,
there exists an index
p_{1}=p_{1}(\varepsilon)\in\mathds{N}
and a positive number
b_{1}=b_{1}(\varepsilon)
such that, for any
p\geq p_{1}
and
b\geq b_{1},
we can divide
\mathrm{C}_{p}=\mathrm{C}_{p,0}\cup\mathrm{C}_{p,1}
as before, and with this division
\sup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\geq p_{1}\\
b\geq b_{1}\end{subarray}}\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{E}_{p}}G(z,0)\leq\varepsilon,
where
\mathrm{E}_{p}=\mathrm{A}_{p}\cup\mathrm{B}_{p}\cup\mathrm{C}_{p,1}.

Proof.

If
z\in\mathrm{A}_{p}\cup\mathrm{B}_{p}
then the bounds on the Green’s function (
2.22
) give at once
G(z,0)=G(0,-z)\leq c_{1}e^{-c_{2}\|z\|}\|z\|^{-\frac{d}{2}}\leq c_{1}e^{-c_{2}p}p^{-\frac{d}{2}}.
Now,
\mathrm{card}\left(\mathrm{A}_{p}\cup\mathrm{B}_{p}\right)\asymp p^{d}
and so, for a constant
c_{3}>0,

\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{A}_{p}\cup\mathrm{B}_{p}}G(z,0)\leq c_{3}e^{-c_{2}p}p^{\frac{d}{2}}\leq\left[c_{3}\sup\limits_{t\geq 1}e^{-\frac{c_{2}}{2}t}t^{\frac{d}{2}}\right]e^{-\frac{c_{2}}{2}p}=c_{4}e^{-\frac{c_{2}}{2}p}.
Now, for any
b>0,
we may construct
\mathrm{C}_{p,0}
and
\mathrm{C}_{p,1}
as in the statement; if
b\geq p^{\frac{1}{2}},
then
\mathrm{C}_{p,1}=\varnothing,
which is fine. Write
\mathrm{C}_{p,1}\subset\bigcup\limits_{bp^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq k\leq p}\{-p\}\times\mathrm{S}\left(0;k\right),
so that
\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{C}_{p,1}}G(z,0)\leq\sum\limits_{bp^{\frac{1}{2}}<k\leq p}\mathrm{card}\left(\mathrm{S}\left(0;k\right)\right)c_{1}e^{-c_{2}\frac{k^{2}}{p}}p^{-\frac{d}{2}}\leq\sum\limits_{bp^{\frac{1}{2}}<k\leq p}c_{5}e^{-c_{2}\frac{k^{2}}{p}}p^{-\frac{d}{2}}k^{d},
for a constant
c_{5}>0.
The function
t\mapsto e^{-c\frac{t^{2}}{p}}t^{d}
has a maximum at
t=\left(\frac{d}{2c}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}p^{\frac{1}{2}}
and it is decreasing for
t\geq\left(\frac{d}{2c}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}p^{\frac{1}{2}};
any value
b\geq\left(\frac{d}{2c}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
allows bounding
\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{C}_{p,1}}G(z,0)\leq c_{5}\int\limits_{bp^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{p}dt\ e^{-c_{2}\frac{t^{2}}{p}}p^{-\frac{d}{2}}t^{d-1}=c_{5}\int\limits_{b}^{\sqrt{p}}dt\ e^{-c_{2}t^{2}}t^{d-1}\leq c_{6}e^{-\frac{c_{2}}{2}b^{2}}.
Hence, putting all together, we have established that, for any
p\in\mathds{N}
and
b\geq\left(\frac{d}{2c}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{A}_{p}\cup\mathrm{B}_{p}}G(z,0)\leq ce^{-c^{\prime}p}
and
\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{C}_{p,1}}G(z,0)\leq Ce^{-C^{\prime}b^{2}},
where all these constants
c,c^{\prime},C,C^{\prime}>0
are independent of both
p
and
b.
The desired results are now clear.
∎

Proposition ( 5.7
)

Let
\varepsilon>0
and we maintain the notations and conclusions of (
5.6
). Then, there exists a positive integer
p_{2}=p_{2}(\varepsilon)
and a family of sets
(\mathrm{C}_{p,0}^{\prime})_{p\geq p_{2}},
such that
\mathrm{C}_{p,0}^{\prime}\subset\mathrm{C}_{p,0},

\mathrm{card}\left(\mathrm{C}_{p,0}^{\prime}\right)\leq\varepsilon\cdot\mathrm{card}\left(\mathrm{C}_{p,0}\right)
and
\sup\limits_{p\geq p_{2}}\max\limits_{z\in\mathrm{C}_{p,0}}\left\lVert z-\mathrm{C}_{p,0}^{\prime}\right\rVert<\infty.

The proof follows from elementary techniques and can be found in
[
MD20
, Proposition (5.2.3)]
.

For the next proposition, the reader should recall the definition of
\mathsf{LE}.

Proposition ( 5.8
)

Let
S
and
S^{\prime}
be two independent transient irreducible Markov chains on the same countable state space (having same transition density), with initial states
o
and
o^{\prime},
respectively. Let
\mathrm{U}
be any subset of the states containing
o
and
o^{\prime}.
Denote by
T
and
T^{\prime}
the exit times of
\mathrm{U}
by
S
and
S^{\prime},
respectively. If the event
\EuScript{H}_{\mathrm{U}}
defined by “there exists
0\leq m\leq T
and
0\leq m^{\prime}\leq T^{\prime}
with
S_{m}=S_{m^{\prime}}^{\prime}
” has positive probability, then
\mathds{P}{}\left(\mathsf{LE}(S_{m^{\prime}}^{\prime})_{m^{\prime}=0,\ldots,T^{\prime}}\cap(S_{m})_{m=0,\ldots,T}\neq\varnothing\right)\geq 2^{-8}\mathds{P}{}\left(\EuScript{H}_{\mathrm{U}}\right).
(This inequality obviously also holds if
\EuScript{H}_{\mathrm{U}}
has probability zero.)

We only provide a reference for this proposition: see Remark 1.3 and Lemma 4.1 of
[
LPS03
]
.

In (
4.15
) we proved a
lower bound
for the probability that two independent sections of the network random walk intersect. That was an involved theorem. The following proposition provides an
upper bound
for the probability that two network random walks intersect in a
narrow
strip.

Proposition ( 5.9
)

Let
d=2.
Consider
k_{0}
and
p_{0}
as in (
4.14
) with
\varepsilon=1
and define the “strips”

\textstyle\mathrm{J}_{p}^{-}=[4\cdot 9^{pk_{0}}-2\cdot 3^{pk_{0}},4\cdot 9^{pk_{0}}]\times\mathds{Z}^{2},\quad\mathrm{J}_{p}^{+}=[4\cdot 9^{pk_{0}},4\cdot 9^{pk_{0}}+2\cdot 3^{pk_{0}}]\times\mathds{Z}^{2}.

With
S
and
S^{\prime}
being two independent network random walks in
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)
started at
o
and
0,
respectively, denote by
I_{p}^{\pm}(o)
the number of intersections of the paths
S
and
S^{\prime}
inside the strip
\mathrm{J}_{p}^{\pm}.
Then, there exists a constant
c>0
such that for all
\delta>0,
there exists an index
p_{3}=p_{3}(\delta)
such that, for all
p\geq p_{3},

\max\limits_{\left\lVert o\right\rVert\leq\delta}\mathds{P}{}\left(I_{p}^{\pm}(o)>0\right)\leq c3^{-pk_{0}}.

Proof.

Take
p_{3,1}=p_{3,1}(\delta)
the first integer
s
such that the ball
\mathrm{B}\left(0;\delta+1\right)
is a subset of
\mathrm{U}_{s-1}
defined by (
4.10
). Assume
p\geq p_{3,1}.
Write
I_{p}^{\pm}
instead of
I_{p}^{\pm}(o)
for simplicity and we will show the bounds are universal, provided
p
is large enough (depending on
\delta
); since the argument is very similar, suffices to consider the case of
I_{p}^{+}.
We have
I_{p}^{+}=\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{J}_{p}^{+}}\sum\limits_{m,m^{\prime}=0}^{\infty}\mathds{1}_{\{S_{m}=z\}}\mathds{1}_{\{S_{m^{\prime}}^{\prime}=z\}},
and by independence,
\mathds{P}{}\left(I_{p}^{+}>0\right)\leq\mathds{E}{}\left(I_{p}^{+}\right)=\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{J}_{p}^{+}}G(o,z)G(0,z).
Write now

\textstyle\begin{split}\mathrm{J}_{p,0}^{+}&=[4\cdot 9^{pk_{0}},4\cdot 9^{pk_{0}}+2\cdot 3^{pk_{0}}]\times\mathrm{B}\left(0;3^{pk_{0}}\right)\\
\mathrm{J}_{p,t}^{+}&=[4\cdot 9^{pk_{0}},4\cdot 9^{pk_{0}}+2\cdot 3^{pk_{0}}]\times\left(\mathrm{B}\left(0;3^{(t+1)pk_{0}}\right)\setminus\mathrm{B}\left(0;3^{tpk_{0}}\right)\right)\quad(t\in\mathds{N}),\end{split}

so that
\mathrm{J}_{p}^{+}
is the union of the pairwise disjoint sets
\mathrm{J}_{p,t}^{+}
(
t\in\mathds{Z}_{+}
).

It easily follows that
G(o,z)\asymp G(0,z)
for
z\in\mathrm{J}_{p}^{+}
and all
p
large enough with any implicit constant universal. Denote now
p_{3}=\max(p_{3,1},p_{3,2},p_{3,3}).
There exists
c>0
such that
G(o,z)\leq cG(0,z).
Substitute this into the equation above,
\mathds{P}{}\left(I_{p}^{+}>0\right)\leq\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{J}_{p}^{+}}G(o,z)G(0,z)\leq c\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{J}_{p}^{+}}G(0,z)^{2}=c\sum\limits_{t\in\mathds{Z}_{+}}\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{J}_{p,t}^{+}}G(0,z)^{2}.
For
t=0,
by (
2.22
),
\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{J}_{p,0}^{+}}G(0,z)\leq c_{1}^{2}9^{-2pk_{0}}\mathrm{card}\left(\mathrm{J}_{p,0}^{+}\right)\asymp 3^{-4pk_{0}}\times 3^{pk_{0}}\cdot 3^{2pk_{0}}=3^{-pk_{0}}.
For
t=1,
and with
\mathrm{card}\left(\mathrm{B}\left(x;r\right)\right)\leq Lr^{2}
for
x\in\mathds{Z}^{2}
and
r\geq 1,
we have

\displaystyle\textstyle\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{J}_{p,1}^{+}}G(0,z)^{2}

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq c_{1}^{2}\sum\limits_{n=4\cdot 9^{pk_{0}}}^{4\cdot 9^{pk_{0}}+2\cdot 3^{pk_{0}}}\sum\limits_{r=1}^{2\cdot 3^{pk_{0}}}\sum\limits_{r3^{pk_{0}}\leq\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq(r+1)3^{pk_{0}}}e^{-2c_{2}\frac{\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}}{n}}n^{-2}

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq 2c_{1}^{2}9^{-2pk_{0}}\cdot 3^{pk_{0}}\sum\limits_{r=1}^{2\cdot 3^{pk_{0}}}L(r+1)^{2}9^{pk_{0}}e^{-c_{2}r^{2}}\leq\left[2c_{1}^{2}L\sum\limits_{r=1}^{\infty}(r+1)^{2}e^{-c_{2}r^{2}}\right]3^{-pk_{0}}.

For
t\geq 2
and
z=(n_{z},x_{z})\in\mathrm{J}_{p,t}^{+},
the condition
3^{tpk_{0}}\leq\left\lVert x_{z}\right\rVert\leq 3^{(t+1)pk_{0}}
guarantees the bound
G(0,z)\leq c_{1}e^{-c_{2}\left\lVert z\right\rVert},
and we obtain bounds that are negligible compared with the two bounds already obtained. Indeed,

\textstyle\sum\limits_{t\geq 2}\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{J}_{p,0}^{+}}G(0,z)^{2}\textstyle\leq c_{1}^{2}L\sum\limits_{t\geq 2}\sum\limits_{n=4\cdot 9^{pk_{0}}}^{4\cdot 9^{pk_{0}}+2\cdot 3^{pk_{0}}}e^{-c_{2}3^{tpk_{0}}}3^{2(t+1)pk_{0}}\leq\left[2c_{1}^{2}L\sum\limits_{t\geq 2}e^{-\frac{c_{2}}{2}3^{tpk_{0}}}3^{(2t+3)pk_{0}}\right]e^{-\frac{c_{2}}{2}3^{2pk_{0}}}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Before continuing with the next proposition, we introduce the following terminology. A set
\mathrm{H}
of the form
\mathrm{H}=\{h\}\times\mathds{Z}^{d}
will be called a
splitting hyperplane
(
splitting plane
if
d=2
) for the path
(v_{n})
(either finite or infinite) if for one, and exactly one value of
m,
we have
\mathrm{pr}_{0}v_{m}=h.
In this case, we will call
h
to be a
splitting level
for this path
(v_{m}).

Proposition ( 5.10
)

Let
S
be the network random walk on
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)
started at
S_{0}.
Suppose that
\zeta\leq\eta
are two stopping times for
S,
with
\zeta<\infty
almost surely (
\eta=\infty
with positive probability not excluded). Denote by
\EuScript{S}_{h}(\zeta,\eta)
the event that
h\in\mathds{Z}
is a splitting level for the path
S=(S_{m})_{\zeta\leq m\leq\eta}
(whenever
\eta=\infty,
replace
m\leq\eta
by
m<\eta
). On
\EuScript{S}_{h}(\zeta,\eta),
denote by
m_{0}
to be the unique positive integer
m
for which
\mathrm{pr}_{0}S_{m}=h.
Then, on the event
\EuScript{S}_{h}(\zeta,\eta),

\mathsf{LE}(S)=\mathsf{LE}(S_{m})_{\zeta\leq m\leq m_{0}}\vee\mathsf{LE}(S_{m})_{m_{0}\leq m\leq\eta}.

The proof is very easy and therefore, omitted.

The next proposition shows that splitting levels do occur with a universal overwhelming probability if one let appropriate scales to be considered. In other words, if the random walk moves enough so that the initial and last state considered are
n
levels apart,
at the very least
, one in roughly every
\sqrt{n}
consecutive levels will be splitting. The gist of its proof lies in random walk in one dimension, see (
2.17
).

Proposition ( 5.11
)

Consider the network random walk
S
on
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)
started at zero. For every positive integer
a,
every pair
\zeta_{p}<\eta_{p}
of stopping times for
S
such that, for almost every realisation,
\zeta_{p}\leq\tau_{\{a\}\times\mathds{Z}^{2}}(S),

\tau_{\{a+p^{2}\}\times\mathds{Z}^{2}}(S)\leq\eta_{p},
and for
h\in\mathds{Z}_{+},
consider the event
\EuScript{S}_{h}(\zeta_{p},\eta_{p})
that
h
is a splitting level for
(S_{m})_{m=\zeta_{p},\ldots,\eta_{p}}.
There exists a universal constant
c>0
and an index
p_{4}\in\mathds{N}
such that, for all
p\geq p_{4},
all systems
(a,\zeta_{p},\eta_{p})
as above and all integers
b
satisfying
a\leq b
and
b+p\leq a+p^{2},
we have
\mathds{P}{}\left(\bigcup\limits_{h=b}^{b+p}\EuScript{S}_{h}(\zeta_{p},\eta_{p})\right)\geq 1-e^{-cp}.

Proof.

Let
\EuScript{K}_{p}
be the event “some
h=b,\ldots,b+p
is a splitting level for the path
(S_{m})_{m\in\mathds{Z}_{+}}
.” We have
\EuScript{K}_{p}\subset\bigcup\limits_{h=b}^{b+p}\EuScript{S}_{h}(\zeta_{p},\eta_{p})
and therefore
\mathds{P}{}\left(\bigcup\limits_{h=b}^{b+p}\EuScript{S}_{h}(\zeta_{p},\eta_{p})\right)\geq\mathds{P}{}\left(\EuScript{K}_{p}\right).
The result follows at once from (
2.17
).
∎

Proposition ( 5.12
)

Let
d=2.
Consider
S
and
S^{\prime}
two independent network random walks in
\Gamma_{2}(\lambda)
started at
o
and
0,
respectively. Define the following stopping time
\sigma_{p}=\tau_{\mathrm{H}_{p}}(S),
where
\mathrm{H}_{p}=\{4\cdot 9^{pk_{0}}\}\times\mathds{Z}^{2},
in other words,
\sigma_{p}
is the hitting time of the “plane”
\mathrm{H}_{p}
by
S.
Define
\sigma_{p}^{\prime}
similarly, using
S^{\prime}
in lieu of
S.
Denote
L^{\prime}=\mathsf{LE}(S_{m^{\prime}}^{\prime})_{m^{\prime}=\sigma_{2(p-1)}^{\prime},\ldots,\sigma_{2p}^{\prime}},
which is the loop-erasure of the section of
S^{\prime}
between the planes
\mathrm{H}_{2(p-2)}
and
\mathrm{H}_{2p};
with this, define
\beta_{p}(o)=\sum\limits_{m=\sigma_{2(p-1)}}^{\sigma_{2p}}\sum\limits_{z^{\prime}\in L^{\prime}}\mathds{1}_{\{S_{m}=z\}},
which is the number of times the section
(S_{m})_{m=\sigma_{2(p-1)},\ldots,\sigma_{2p}}
crosses the path
L^{\prime}.
Then, there exists a constant
c>0
such that for every
\delta>0,
there exists a
p_{5}=p_{5}(\delta)\in\mathds{N}
satisfying
\min\limits_{\left\lVert o\right\rVert\leq\delta}\mathds{P}{}\left(\beta_{p}(o)>0\right)\geq\frac{c}{p},
for all
p\geq p_{5}.

Proof.

Define
M_{p}(o)
as in (
4.13
) using
S
and
S^{\prime}.
Define
p_{5,1}=p_{5,1}(\delta)\in\mathds{N}
to be the first index
p
such that
\mathrm{B}\left(0;\delta+1\right)\subset\mathrm{U}_{p-1}.
Notice that for
p\geq p_{5,1},

T_{p}\leq\sigma_{p}
and
T_{p}^{\prime}\leq\sigma_{p}^{\prime}.
Since
\sigma_{p-1}
and
\sigma_{p-1}^{\prime}
are the hitting times of the plane
\mathrm{H}_{p-1},
we find that

\textstyle M_{p}(o)=\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{D}_{pk_{0}}}\sum\limits_{m=\sigma_{p-1}}^{T_{p}}\sum\limits_{m^{\prime}=\sigma_{p-1}^{\prime}}^{T_{p}^{\prime}}\mathds{1}_{\{S_{m}=z\}}\mathds{1}_{\{S_{m^{\prime}}^{\prime}=z\}}\leq\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{D}_{pk_{0}}}\sum\limits_{m=\sigma_{p-1}}^{\sigma_{p}}\sum\limits_{m^{\prime}=\sigma_{p-1}^{\prime}}^{\sigma_{p}^{\prime}}\mathds{1}_{\{S_{m}=z\}}\mathds{1}_{\{S_{m^{\prime}}^{\prime}=z\}}\mathop{=}\limits^{\mathrm{def.}}N_{p}(o).

Thus,
\mathds{P}{}\left(M_{p}(o)>0\right)\leq\mathds{P}{}\left(N_{p}(o)>0\right)
and Theorem (
4.15
) gives
\mathds{P}{}\left(M_{p}(o)>0\right)\geq\frac{c}{p},
provided
p\geq P(o,0),
the constant
c
being universal. Define
\displaystyle p_{5,2}=p_{5,2}(\delta)=\max_{\left\lVert o\right\rVert\leq\delta}P(o,0).
Finally, (
5.8
) gives
\mathds{P}{}\left(\beta_{p}(o)>0\right)\geq 2^{-8}\mathds{P}{}\left(\tilde{N}_{p}(o)>0\right),
where
\tilde{N}_{p}(o)
is the number of intersection between the two sections
(S_{m})_{m=\sigma_{2(p-1)},\ldots,\sigma_{2p}}
and
(S_{m^{\prime}}^{\prime})_{m^{\prime}=\sigma_{2(p-1)}^{\prime},\ldots,\sigma_{2p}^{\prime}}.
Clearly
\tilde{N}_{p}(o)\geq N_{p}(o),
and the result follows with
p_{5}=\max(p_{5,1},p_{5,2}).

∎

Proposition ( 5.13
)

Let
d=2.
With the hypotheses and notations of (
5.12
). Define
\alpha_{p}(o)
to be the number of intersections of
\mathsf{LE}(S_{m^{\prime}}^{\prime})_{m^{\prime}=0,\ldots,\sigma_{2p}^{\prime}}
and
(S_{m})_{m=0,\ldots,\sigma_{2p}}.
For every
\delta>0,

\lim\limits_{p\to\infty}\max\limits_{\left\lVert o\right\rVert\leq\delta}\mathds{P}{}\left(\alpha_{p}(o)=0\right)=0.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon>0.
We can then find the indices
p_{1}(\varepsilon)
and
p_{2}(\varepsilon),
and a constant
b_{1}(\varepsilon)
satisfying the conclusions of (
5.6
) and (
5.7
), we take
b=b_{1}(\varepsilon).
The
\delta
in the hypothesis, together with (
5.9
) and (
5.12
) give two indices
p_{3}(\delta)
and
p_{5}(\delta),
satisfying the corresponding conclusions of these results. And (
5.11
) gives yet another index
p_{4}
satisfying the outcome of that proposition. Observe that the choice of
p_{3}(\delta)
guarantees that the closed ball
\mathrm{B}\left(0;\delta+1\right)
is contained in
\mathrm{U}_{p_{3}(\delta)-1}.
Let
P=\max(p_{1}(\varepsilon),p_{2}(\varepsilon),p_{3}(\delta),p_{4},p_{5}(\delta)).

Write
\alpha_{p}
in place of
\alpha_{p}(o)
for simplicity, all the bounds will be shown to be independent of
\delta.
Assume
p\geq P.
Define the events

(A)

\textstyle\begin{split}\EuScript{L}_{p}^{\prime}&\textstyle=\bigcup\limits_{h=4\cdot 9^{2(p-1)k_{0}}}^{4\cdot 9^{2(p-1)k_{0}}+2\cdot 9^{(p-1)k_{0}}}\left\{h\text{ is a splitting level for }\left(S^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}\right)_{m^{\prime}=\sigma_{2(p-1)}^{\prime},\ldots,\sigma_{2p}^{\prime}}\right\}\\
\EuScript{R}_{p}^{\prime}&\textstyle=\bigcup\limits_{h=4\cdot 9^{2pk_{0}}-2\cdot 9^{pk_{0}}}^{4\cdot 9^{2pk_{0}}}\left\{h\text{ is a splitting level for }\left(S^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}\right)_{m^{\prime}=\sigma_{2(p-1)}^{\prime},\ldots,\sigma_{2p}^{\prime}}\right\},\end{split}

that is, the events where one of the
2\cdot 9^{(p-1)k_{0}}
left-most levels between the planes
\mathrm{H}_{2(p-1)}
and
\mathrm{H}_{2p}
and one of the
2\cdot 9^{pk_{0}}
right-most levels in the same region is a splitting level for the stated section of
S^{\prime}.
Denote by
\EuScript{R}_{p}^{\prime\complement}
and
\EuScript{L}_{p}^{\prime\complement}
their corresponding complements; we also define
\beta_{p}=\beta_{p}(o)
from (
5.12
). Then,

\displaystyle\mathds{P}{}\left(\alpha_{p}=0\right)

\displaystyle=\mathds{P}{}\left(\{\alpha_{p}=0\}\cap\EuScript{L}_{p}^{\prime}\right)+\mathds{P}{}\left(\{\alpha_{p}=0\}\cap\EuScript{L}_{p}^{\prime\complement}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}{}\left(\{\alpha_{p}=0\}\cap\EuScript{L}_{p}^{\prime}\cap\{\beta_{p}=0\}\right)+\mathds{P}{}\left(\{\alpha_{p}=0\}\cap\EuScript{L}_{p}^{\prime}\cap\{\beta_{p}>0\}\right)+\mathds{P}{}\left(\EuScript{L}_{p}^{\prime\complement}\right).

We analyse now what happens on the intersection
\{\alpha_{p}=0\}\cap\EuScript{L}_{p}^{\prime}\cap\{\beta_{p}>0\}.
Assume that this event occurs. Then, there exists a splitting level
h\in\left\{4\cdot 9^{2(p-1)k_{0}},\ldots,4\cdot 9^{2(p-1)k_{0}}+2\cdot 9^{(p-1)k_{0}}\right\}
for the section
(S^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}})_{m^{\prime}=\sigma_{2(p-1)}^{\prime},\ldots,\sigma_{2p}^{\prime}}.
Denote by
h_{p}^{\prime}
the first of such splitting levels and by
m_{p}^{\prime}
the unique index for which
\mathrm{pr}_{0}S_{m_{p}^{\prime}}^{\prime}=h_{p}^{\prime}.
According to (
5.10
), we have

(B)

\textstyle\mathsf{LE}\left(S^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}\right)_{m^{\prime}=\sigma_{2(p-1)}^{\prime},\ldots,\sigma_{2p}^{\prime}}=\mathsf{LE}(S^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}})_{m^{\prime}=\sigma_{2(p-1)}^{\prime},\ldots,m_{p}^{\prime}}\vee\mathsf{LE}(S^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}})_{m^{\prime}=m_{p}^{\prime},\ldots,\sigma_{2p}^{\prime}}.

Since
\sigma_{2(p-1)}^{\prime}
is the hitting time of the set
\mathrm{H}_{2(p-1)},
it is then obvious that
h_{p}^{\prime}
is also a splitting level for the section
(S_{m^{\prime}}^{\prime})_{m^{\prime}=0,\ldots,\sigma_{2p}^{\prime}}
and so, we also have

(C)

\textstyle\mathsf{LE}(S^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}})_{m^{\prime}=0,\ldots,\sigma_{2p}^{\prime}}=\mathsf{LE}(S^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}})_{m^{\prime}=0,\ldots,m_{p}^{\prime}}\vee\mathsf{LE}(S^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}})_{m^{\prime}=m_{p}^{\prime},\ldots,\sigma_{2p}^{\prime}}.

That
\alpha_{p}=0
entails that the section
(S_{m})_{m=0,\ldots,\sigma_{2p}}
does not intersect the set in (
C
) and the relation
\beta_{p}>0
means that it does intersect the set in (
B
). Therefore, on the event under consideration, the intersection of
(S_{m})_{m=0,\ldots,\sigma_{2p}}
with
\mathsf{LE}(S^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}})_{m^{\prime}=\sigma_{2(p-1)}^{\prime},\ldots,\sigma_{2p}^{\prime}}
occurs at some
S_{m^{\prime}}^{\prime}
with
m^{\prime}\in\left\{\sigma_{2(p-1)}^{\prime},\ldots,m_{p}^{\prime}\right\};
in particular,
S
and
S^{\prime}
intersect inside the strip
\mathrm{J}_{2(p-1)}^{+}
(notation of (
5.9
)). In other words, with the notation
I_{p}^{+}
of (
5.9
),
\mathds{P}{}\left(\{\alpha_{p}=0\}\cap\EuScript{L}_{p}^{\prime}\cap\{\beta_{p}>0\}\right)\leq\mathds{P}{}\left(I_{2(p-1)}^{+}>0\right).
Whence, by the arguments of the previous two paragraphs,

(D)

\textstyle\mathds{P}{}\left(\alpha_{p}=0\right)\leq\mathds{P}{}\left(\{\alpha_{p}=0\}\cap\EuScript{L}_{p}^{\prime}\cap\{\beta_{p}=0\}\right)+\mathds{P}{}\left(\EuScript{L}_{p}^{\prime\complement}\right)+\mathds{P}{}\left(I_{2(p-1)}^{+}>0\right).

Now observe that

\displaystyle\{\alpha_{p}=0\}\cap\EuScript{L}_{p}^{\prime}\cap\{\beta_{p}=0\}

\displaystyle=\big(\{\alpha_{p}=0,\alpha_{p-1}=0\}\cap\EuScript{L}_{p}^{\prime}\cap\{\beta_{p}=0\}\big)

\displaystyle\cup\big(\{\alpha_{p}=0,\alpha_{p-1}>0\}\cap\EuScript{L}_{p}^{\prime}\cap\{\beta_{p}=0\}\big)

\displaystyle\subset\big(\{\alpha_{p-1}=0\}\cap\EuScript{L}_{p}^{\prime}\cap\{\beta_{p}=0\}\big)\cup\{\alpha_{p}=0,\alpha_{p-1}>0\},

and from this it follows that

\textstyle\mathds{P}{}\left(\{\alpha_{p}=0\}\cap\EuScript{L}_{p}^{\prime}\cap\{\beta_{p}=0\}\right)\leq\mathds{P}{}\left(\{\alpha_{p-1}=0\}\cap\EuScript{L}_{p}^{\prime}\cap\{\beta_{p}=0\}\right)+\mathds{P}{}\left(\alpha_{p}=0,\alpha_{p-1}>0\right).

By (
5.4
),
\{\alpha_{p-1}=0\}
and
\EuScript{L}_{p}^{\prime}\cap\{\beta_{p}=0\}
are independent events. Write now

\textstyle\{\alpha_{p}=0,\alpha_{p-1}>0\}=\big(\{\alpha_{p}=0,\alpha_{p-1}>0\}\cap\EuScript{R}_{p-1}^{\prime}\big)\cup\left(\left\{\alpha_{p}=0,\alpha_{p-1}>0\right\}\cap\EuScript{R}_{p-1}^{\prime\complement}\right);

as done before, on the set
\{\alpha_{p}=0,\alpha_{p-1}>0\}\cap\EuScript{R}_{p-1}^{\prime},
the two random walks
S
and
S^{\prime}
intersect in the strip
\mathrm{J}_{2(p-1)}^{-},
yielding
\mathds{P}{}\left(\{\alpha_{p}=0,\alpha_{p-1}>0\}\cap\EuScript{R}_{p-1}^{\prime}\right)\leq\mathds{P}{}\left(I_{2(p-1)}^{-}>0\right).
With the aforementioned considerations and substituting into (
D
), we reach

\displaystyle\mathds{P}{}\left(\alpha_{p}=0\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}{}\left(\alpha_{p-1}=0\right)\mathds{P}{}\left(\beta_{p}=0\right)

\displaystyle+\mathds{P}{}\left(I_{2(p-1)}^{-}>0\right)+\mathds{P}{}\left(\EuScript{R}_{p-1}^{\prime\complement}\right)+\mathds{P}{}\left(\EuScript{L}_{p}^{\prime\complement}\right)+\mathds{P}{}\left(I_{2(p-1)}^{+}>0\right).

By virtue of propositions (
5.9
) and (
5.11
), we have
\mathds{P}{}\left(I_{2(p-1)}^{-}>0\right)+\mathds{P}{}\left(\EuScript{R}_{p-1}^{\prime\complement}\right)+\mathds{P}{}\left(\EuScript{L}_{p}^{\prime\complement}\right)+\mathds{P}{}\left(I_{2(p-1)}^{+}>0\right)\leq ce^{-c^{\prime}p}
and by (
5.12
)
\mathds{P}{}\left(\beta_{p}=0\right)\leq 1-\frac{c^{\prime\prime}}{p},
for
p\geq P,
where the constants
c,c^{\prime}
and
c^{\prime\prime}
are all universal. We can conclude then that
\mathds{P}{}\left(\alpha_{P+p}=0\right)\leq\prod\limits_{q=0}^{p}\left(1-\frac{c^{\prime\prime}}{P+q}\right)+ce^{-c^{\prime}P}\sum\limits_{q=0}^{p}e^{-c^{\prime}q}.
The conclusion of the proposition is now clear given the last inequality and bearing in mind that all estimates are uniform in
\left\lVert o\right\rVert\leq\delta.

∎

Proposition ( 5.14
)

Let
d=2.
With the hypotheses of (
5.13
), let
\tilde{\alpha}_{p}(o)
be the number of intersections of
\mathsf{LE}(S_{m^{\prime}}^{\prime})_{m^{\prime}\in\mathds{Z}_{+}}
and
(S_{m})_{m=0,\ldots,\sigma_{2p}}.
Then, for every
\delta>0,

\lim_{p\to 0}\max\limits_{\left\lVert o\right\rVert\leq\delta}\mathds{P}{}\left(\tilde{\alpha}_{p}(o)=0\right)=0.

Proof.

We use the notation of (
5.11
). Simply write

\displaystyle\mathds{P}{}\left(\tilde{\alpha}_{p}(o)=0\right)

\displaystyle=\mathds{P}{}\left(\tilde{\alpha}_{p}(o)=0,\alpha_{p}(o)=0\right)+\mathds{P}{}\left(\tilde{\alpha}_{p}(o)=0,\alpha_{p}(o)>0\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}{}\left(\alpha_{p}(o)=0\right)+\mathds{P}{}\left(\left\{\tilde{\alpha}_{p}(o)=0,\alpha_{p}(o)>0\right\}\cap\EuScript{R}_{p}\right)

\displaystyle+\mathds{P}{}\left(\left\{\tilde{\alpha}_{p}(o)=0,\alpha_{p}(o)>0\right\}\cap\EuScript{R}_{p}^{\complement}\right).

Exactly as in the proof of (
5.13
), the two relations
\tilde{\alpha}_{p}(o)=0
and
\alpha_{p}(o)>0
on the event
\EuScript{R}_{p}
imply that two independent random walks will intersect in the strip
\mathrm{J}_{2p}^{-},
hence the result follows immediately upon invoking (
5.9
), (
5.11
) and (
5.13
).
∎

5.3.3
The proof of the main theorem.

Theorem ( 5.15
)

For almost every realisation of
\mathsf{UST}
in
\Gamma_{2}(\lambda),
the tree has one end.

Proof.

Denote by
\mathsf{UST}
the measure of uniform spanning tree on the network
\Gamma_{2}(\lambda).
When we define an event on spanning trees, we use
\mathsf{UST}
to refer to the tree itself. We know that
\mathsf{UST}
has at least one end, up to a
\mathsf{UST}
-null event. Consider
\mathscr{N}=\{\mathsf{UST}\text{ has at least two ends}\}.
We aim at showing
\mathsf{UST}(\mathscr{N})=0.
For a vertex
z\in\mathds{Z}^{3},
consider
\mathscr{N}_{z}=\{\text{there are two disjoint rays starting at }z\text{ in }\mathsf{UST}\}.
Then,
\mathscr{N}=\bigcup\limits_{z\in\mathds{Z}^{3}}\mathscr{N}_{z}.
Further, the translation invariance of the probability kernel of the network random walk of
\Gamma_{2}(\lambda)
shows that
\mathsf{UST}(\mathscr{N}_{z})
is a value independent of
z\in\mathds{Z}^{3}.
Hence,
to show
\mathscr{N}
is
\mathsf{UST}
-null, it suffices to show
\mathscr{N}_{0}
is
\mathsf{UST}
-null.

Consider now a set of vertices
\mathrm{K}
of
\mathds{Z}^{3}
with the following property (a “cutset” between
0
and infinity): every ray from
0
must cross
\mathrm{K}
at some vertex. Recall an “edge of
\mathrm{K}
” is an edge of the subgraph induced on
\mathrm{K}.
Then,
\mathscr{N}_{0}
is contained in the event

\textstyle\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{K}}=\left\{\text{there are two disjoint paths in }\mathsf{UST}\text{ from }\mathrm{K}\text{ to 0 that use no edge of }\mathrm{K}\right\}.

In particular, we have the following:
\mathsf{UST}(\mathscr{N}_{0})\leq\inf\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathrm{K}\text{ is a cutset}\\
\text{between }0\text{ and }\infty\end{subarray}}\mathsf{UST}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{K}}).

As in proposition (
5.6
), define the sets
\mathrm{A}_{p}=[-p,p]\times\mathrm{S}\left(0;p\right),

\mathrm{B}_{p}=\{p\}\times\mathrm{B}\left(0;p\right)
and
\mathrm{C}_{p}=\{-p\}\times\mathrm{B}\left(0;p\right).
Their union
\mathrm{K}_{p}
is a cutset between
0
and
\infty.
In particular,

(A)

\textstyle\mathsf{UST}(\mathscr{N}_{0})\leq\inf\limits_{p\in\mathds{N}}\mathsf{UST}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{K}_{p}}).

Construct
\mathfrak{T}
the
\mathsf{UST}
of
\mathds{Z}^{3}
using Wilson’s algorithm rooted at infinity and following some starting at zero predefined ordering of the vertices of
\mathds{Z}^{3},
and assume it is defined on some probability space
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathds{P}).
(For instance, we can follow the order in which we start with
0,
and having searched all vertices such that
\left\lVert z\right\rVert_{1}=k,
we search the vertices satisfying
\left\lVert z\right\rVert_{1}=k+1
arranging them lexicographically.) Thus, we have a family of independent network random walks
(S^{z})_{z\in\mathds{Z}^{3}}
defined on this probability space with
S^{z}
started at
z.
Since
0
is the first vertex searched,
L_{0}=\mathsf{LE}(S^{0}_{m})_{m\in\mathds{Z}_{+}}\subset\mathfrak{T}
(both trees canonically identified with their sets of edges). Up to a
\mathds{P}
-null event,
L_{0}
is an infinite branch. Thus,
\{\mathfrak{T}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{K}_{p}}\}=\{\text{some vertex of }\mathrm{K}_{p}\text{ is connected to $0$ in }\mathfrak{T}\setminus L_{0}\}.
Denote
\EuScript{C}(z)=\{z\text{ is connected to $0$ in }\mathfrak{T}\setminus L_{0}\},
so that
\{\mathfrak{T}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{K}_{p}}\}=\bigcup\limits_{z\in\mathrm{K}_{p}}\EuScript{C}(z).
By virtue of (
5.6
) we reach the existence of an index
p_{1}(\varepsilon)
and a number
b_{1}(\varepsilon)
such that we may divide
\mathrm{C}_{p}=\mathrm{C}_{p,0}\cup\mathrm{C}_{p,1}
as in this proposition (taking
b=b_{1}(\varepsilon)
) and for all
p\geq p_{1}(\varepsilon),

\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{E}_{p}}G(z,0)\leq\varepsilon,
with the notation
\mathrm{E}_{p}=\mathrm{A}_{p}\cup\mathrm{B}_{p}\cup\mathrm{C}_{p,1}
of the proposition (in particular,
G
is the Green’s function of
\Gamma_{2}(\lambda)
). Next,

\textstyle\mathsf{UST}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{K}_{p}})=\mathds{P}{}\left(\mathfrak{T}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{K}_{p}}\right)\leq\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{E}_{p}}\mathds{P}{}\left(\EuScript{C}(z)\right)+\mathds{P}{}\left(\bigcup\limits_{z\in\mathrm{C}_{p,0}}\EuScript{C}(z)\right).

By corollary (
5.5.1
), when calculating
\mathds{P}{}\left(\EuScript{C}(z)\right)
we may assume that the order in which
\mathfrak{T}
was constructed is
(0,z,\ldots).
If the order in the construction of
\mathfrak{T}
were
(0,z,\ldots),
then
\EuScript{C}(z)
would be the event where
S^{z}
hits
L_{0}
for the first time at zero, and then
\EuScript{C}(z)
would be contained in the event where
\tau_{0}(S^{z})<\infty.
Then,
\mathds{P}{}\left(\EuScript{C}(z)\right)\leq G(z,0).
Substituting this inequality above we obtain that, for all
p\geq p_{1}(\varepsilon),

(B)

\textstyle\mathsf{UST}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{K}_{p}})\leq\varepsilon+\mathds{P}{}\left(\bigcup\limits_{z\in\mathrm{C}_{p,0}}\EuScript{C}(z)\right).

Using (
5.7
), there is an index
p_{2}(\varepsilon)
and a family of sets
(\mathrm{C}_{p,0}^{\prime})_{p\geq p_{2}(\varepsilon)},
for which the conclusions of this proposition hold. In particular, we may define
\delta_{0}<\infty
as follows

\textstyle\delta_{0}=\sup\limits_{p\geq p_{2}(\varepsilon)}\max\limits_{z\in\mathrm{C}_{p,0}}\left\lVert z-\mathrm{C}_{p,0}^{\prime}\right\rVert.

For each
z^{\prime}\in\mathrm{C}_{p,0}^{\prime},
consider the set
\mathrm{C}_{p,0}(z^{\prime})=\{-p\}\times\mathrm{B}\left(z^{\prime};\delta_{0}\right).
The definition of
\delta_{0}
shows at once
\mathrm{C}_{p,0}\subset\bigcup\limits_{z^{\prime}\in\mathrm{C}_{p,0}^{\prime}}\mathrm{C}_{p,0}(z^{\prime}).
Then,
\mathds{P}{}\left(\bigcup\limits_{z\in\mathrm{C}_{p,0}}\EuScript{C}(z)\right)\leq\sum\limits_{z^{\prime}\in\mathrm{C}_{p,0}^{\prime}}\mathds{P}{}\left(\bigcup\limits_{z\in\mathrm{C}_{p,0}(z^{\prime})}\EuScript{C}(z)\right).
By definition,
\bigcup\limits_{z\in\mathrm{C}_{p,0}(z^{\prime})}\EuScript{C}(z)
is an event depending solely on
\mathfrak{T}
and
L_{0}.
Thus, (
5.5.1
) shows that its probability is independent of the ordering of the vertices of
\mathds{Z}^{3}\setminus\{0\},
in particular, we may assume that
\mathfrak{T}
was constructed using the ordering
(0,z^{\prime},\ldots).
Consider now the following event
\EuScript{H}(z^{\prime})=\left\{S^{z^{\prime}}_{\tau_{L_{0}}\left(S^{z^{\prime}}\right)}\neq 0\right\},
in other words,
\EuScript{H}(z^{\prime})
is the event where
S^{z^{\prime}}
hits
L_{0}
for the first time anywhere except zero (recall
S^{z^{\prime}}
will hit
L_{0}
a.s.), which is the same as the event where
z^{\prime}
is not connected to
0
in
\mathfrak{T}\setminus L_{0}
(with the assumed order
(0,z^{\prime},\ldots)
of
\mathds{Z}^{3}
). Then,
1-\mathds{P}{}\left(\EuScript{H}(z^{\prime})\right)=\mathds{P}{}\left(S^{z^{\prime}}_{\tau_{L_{0}}\left(S^{z^{\prime}}\right)}=0\right)\leq\mathds{P}{}\left(\tau_{0}\left(S^{z^{\prime}}\right)<\infty\right)\leq G(z^{\prime},0).
Since
z^{\prime}\in\mathrm{C}_{p,0}^{\prime}\subset\mathrm{C}_{p,0}=\{-p\}\times\mathrm{B}\left(0;bp^{\frac{1}{2}}\right),
we have by theorem (
2.22
),
G(z^{\prime},0)\leq c_{1}\left\lVert z^{\prime}\right\rVert^{-1}\leq c_{1}p^{-1}.
Then,
\mathds{P}{}\left(\bigcup\limits_{z\in\mathrm{C}_{p,0}(z^{\prime})}\EuScript{C}(z)\right)\leq\mathds{P}{}\left(\bigcup\limits_{z\in\mathrm{C}_{p,0}(z^{\prime})}\EuScript{C}(z)\cap\EuScript{H}(z^{\prime})\right)+c_{1}p^{-1}.
Summing over
z^{\prime}\in\mathrm{C}_{p,0}^{\prime},

\textstyle\sum\limits_{z^{\prime}\in\mathrm{C}_{p,0}^{\prime}}\mathds{P}{}\left(\bigcup\limits_{z\in\mathrm{C}_{p,0}(z^{\prime})}\EuScript{C}(z)\right)\leq\sum\limits_{z^{\prime}\in\mathrm{C}_{p,0}^{\prime}}\mathds{P}{}\left(\bigcup\limits_{z\in\mathrm{C}_{p,0}(z^{\prime})}\EuScript{C}(z)\cap\EuScript{H}(z^{\prime})\right)+c_{1}p^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{card}\left(\mathrm{C}_{p,0}^{\prime}\right).

By (
5.7
), we have that for
p\geq p_{2}(\varepsilon),

\mathrm{card}\left(\mathrm{C}_{p,0}^{\prime}\right)\leq cp\cdot\varepsilon,
where
c
is a universal constant. By (
B
), we have shown hitherto the existence of a universal constant
c
such that for all
p\geq\max(p_{1}(\varepsilon),p_{2}(\varepsilon)),

(C)

\textstyle\mathsf{UST}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{K}_{p}})\leq c\cdot\varepsilon+\sum\limits_{z^{\prime}\in\mathrm{C}_{p,0}^{\prime}}\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{C}_{p,0}(z^{\prime})}\mathds{P}{}\left(\EuScript{C}(z)\cap\EuScript{H}(z^{\prime})\right).

By virtue of (
A
) and (
C
), we reduce the proof of the theorem to showing following:
there exists a function
\varphi(p)
such that

(D)

\textstyle\sum\limits_{z^{\prime}\in\mathrm{C}_{p,0}^{\prime}}\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{C}_{p,0}(z^{\prime})}\mathds{P}{}\left(\EuScript{C}(z)\cap\EuScript{H}(z^{\prime})\right)\leq\varphi(p)

and
\displaystyle\varphi(p)\to 0
via some subsequence.

We begin by estimating
\mathds{P}{}\left(\EuScript{C}(z)\cap\EuScript{H}(z^{\prime})\right).
Again by (
5.5.1
), to calculate the probability of the event
\EuScript{C}(z)\cap\EuScript{H}(z^{\prime})
we may assume that
\mathfrak{T}
was constructed using the ordering
(0,z^{\prime},z,\ldots)
of
\mathds{Z}^{3}.
Assuming this ordering of
\mathds{Z}^{3},
and on the event
\EuScript{C}(z)\cap\EuScript{H}(z^{\prime}),
the first three steps of the construction of
\mathfrak{T}
proceed as follows:

1)

The first branch at zero is created, we call it
L_{0}.

2)

The random walk
S^{z^{\prime}}
runs, it hits the first branch at zero at some non zero vertex. Denote by
L_{z^{\prime}}
this second branch; by definition,
