, Lemma 2.6]

For every
L>0
there exists a constant
c>0
such that for every
z\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1},
and every
\mathrm{W}\subset\mathds{Z}^{d+1}
with
\mathrm{card}\left(\mathrm{W}\right)\leq L,
we have
\left\langle\mathrm{W}\cup\{z\}\right\rangle_{\eta}\leq\left\langle\mathrm{W}\right\rangle_{\eta}\min\limits_{w\in\mathrm{W}}\left\langle wz\right\rangle_{\eta}\leq c\left\langle\mathrm{W}\cup\{z\}\right\rangle_{\eta}.

Proof.

The proof of this proposition proceeds word by word like that of Lemma 2.6 in
[
BKPS04
]
. ∎

We may now generalise theorem (
6.2
) to any finite subset of vertices. We will only prove an upper bound.

Theorem ( 6.5
)

Let
d\geq 3.
For any
\mathrm{W}\subset\mathds{Z}^{d+1},
denote by
\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{W}}
the event that all the points in
\mathrm{W}
are in the same
\mathsf{USF}
component. For every integer
L>0,
there exists a constant
c>0,
depending only on
L
and dimension, such that for whatever subset
\mathrm{W}
with at most
L
vertices,
\mathsf{USF}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{W}})\leq c\left\langle\mathrm{W}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}.

Proof.

We apply the same reasoning as that used in the proof of Theorem 4.3 of
[
BKPS04
]
. We will apply induction on the cardinality of
\mathrm{W}.
The case of two points is precisely theorem (
6.2
). Assume then the result holds for
\mathrm{card}\left(\mathrm{W}\right)-1\geq 2.
Let
z_{1},z_{2}
be two different vertices of
\mathrm{W}
and denote
\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{W}}(z_{1},z_{2})
the event which is the intersection of
\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{W}}
with the event where the
\mathsf{USF}
-path connecting
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
is edge-disjoint from the
\mathsf{USF}
-rays starting at the vertices of
\mathrm{V}=\mathrm{W}\setminus\{z_{1},z_{2}\}.
Let
\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{W}}^{z}(z_{1},z_{2})
denote the event which is the intersection of
\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{W}}(z_{1},z_{2})
and the event where the ray at
z_{1}
meets the ray at
z_{2}
at the vertex
z.
Then

\textstyle\mathsf{USF}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{W}}(z_{1},z_{2}))\leq\sum\limits_{z\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}}\mathsf{USF}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{W}}^{z}(z_{1},z_{2})).

To calculate the probability of
\mathsf{USF}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{W}}^{z}(z_{1},z_{2}))
we may assume that we construct the
\mathsf{USF}
-forest
\mathfrak{F}
following Wilson’s algorithm rooted at infinity by first ordering all the vertices in
\displaystyle\mathrm{V}z\mathop{=}^{\mathrm{def.}}\mathrm{V}\cup\{z\},
followed by the vertices
z_{1}
and
z_{2},
and then the rest of the vertices of
\mathds{Z}^{d+1}.
We see that

\textstyle\left\{\mathfrak{F}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{W}}^{z}(z_{1},z_{2})\right\}\subset\left\{\mathfrak{F}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{V}z}\right\}\cap\left\{\tau_{z}\left(S^{z_{1}}\right)<\infty\right\}\cap\left\{\tau_{z}\left(S^{z_{2}}\right)<\infty\right\},

and
\left\{\mathfrak{F}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{V}z}\right\}
is an event depending solely in the random walks
S^{v}
for
v\in\mathrm{V}z.
By independence and induction,

\displaystyle\mathsf{USF}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{W}}^{z}(z_{1},z_{2}))

\displaystyle=\mathds{P}{}\left(\mathfrak{F}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{W}}^{z}(z_{1},z_{2})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}{}\left(\mathfrak{F}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{V}z}\right)\mathds{P}{}\left(\tau_{z}\left(S^{z_{1}}\right)<\infty\right)\mathds{P}{}\left(\tau_{z}\left(S^{z_{2}}\right)<\infty\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{USF}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{V}z})G(z_{1},z)G(z_{2},z)

\displaystyle\leq c\left\langle\mathrm{V}z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}G(z_{1},z)G(z_{2},z)

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq c^{\prime}\left\langle\mathrm{V}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}\sum\limits_{v\in\mathrm{V}}\left\langle vz\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}G(z_{1},z)G(z_{2},z),

the last inequality by virtue of (
6.4
). Thus,

(
\ast
)

\textstyle\mathsf{USF}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{W}}(z_{1},z_{2}))\leq c\left\langle\mathrm{V}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}\sum\limits_{v\in\mathrm{V}}\sum\limits_{z\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}}\left\langle vz\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}G(z_{1},z)G(z_{2},z).

We isolate some calculations first.

( 6.5.1
)

For any
a\in\mathds{R}
and
r>2,
we have
\sum\limits_{\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}\leq r}\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{a}\asymp 1+r^{d+2+a},
the implicit constants depending only on
a
and dimension.

Proof of (
6.5.1
).

Divide the cases
a<0
and
a\geq 0,
use the fact that there exists an integer
k\geq 1
such that
2^{k-1}\leq r<2^{k}
and use that

\textstyle\bigcup\limits_{j=1}^{k-1}\left\{2^{j-1}\leq\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}<2^{j}\right\}\subset\left\{\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}\leq r\right\}\subset\bigcup\limits_{j=1}^{k}\left\{2^{j-1}\leq\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}<2^{j}\right\}.

Keep in mind that
\left\langle 0\right\rangle_{\eta}=1.

∎

( 6.5.2
)

There exists a universal constant
c>0
such that for any two vertices
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1},
we have

\textstyle\sum\limits_{z\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}}\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}G(z_{1},z)G(z_{1},z)\leq c\left\langle 0z_{1}z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}.

Proof of (
6.5.2
).

By symmetry, we may assume
\left\langle z_{1}\right\rangle_{\eta}\leq\left\langle z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}.
Write
z_{i}=(n_{i},x_{i}),
for
i=1,2.
Then,

\textstyle\sum\limits_{z\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}}\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}G(z_{1},z)G(z_{2},z)=\sum\limits_{\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}>\frac{1}{2}\left\langle z_{1}\right\rangle_{\eta}}\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}G(z_{1},z)G(z_{2},z)+I,

and we may bound easily

\displaystyle\textstyle\sum\limits_{\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}>\frac{1}{2}\left\langle z_{1}\right\rangle_{\eta}}\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}G(z_{1},z)G(z_{2},z)

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq\left\langle z_{1}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}\sum\limits_{z\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}}G(z_{1},z)G(z_{2},z)

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq c\left\langle z_{1}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}\left\langle z_{1}-z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}\leq c\left\langle 0z_{1}z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)},

where the penultimate inequality is obtained from (
6.2.2
) and the last inequality by definition of the spread. The rest of the calculation is to bound
I;
this will be done in several cases. By definition

\textstyle I=\sum\limits_{\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\langle z_{1}\right\rangle_{\eta}}\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}G(z_{1},z)G(z_{2},z).

We will handle four cases, we will make use of the Green’s function estimates (
2.22
). Notice that
G(z^{\prime},z^{\prime\prime})\leq c\left\lVert z^{\prime}-z^{\prime\prime}\right\rVert^{-\frac{d}{2}}
in all cases and that
\left\langle x\right\rangle\leq\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}
for every
z\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}
(the left hand side is the spread relative to the Euclidean metric (
4.5
)).

Case 1.

Here we assume that
\left\langle z_{1}\right\rangle_{\eta}=|n_{1}|^{\frac{1}{2}}
and
\left\langle z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}=|n_{2}|^{\frac{1}{2}}.
In this case,
\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\langle z_{1}\right\rangle_{\eta}
implies that
|n|\leq\frac{1}{4}|n_{1}|\leq\frac{1}{4}|n_{2}|
and
\left\lVert x-x_{1}\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert x\right\rVert+\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert\leq\frac{3}{2}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert\leq\frac{3}{2}|n_{1}|^{\frac{1}{2}},
and similarly
\left\lVert x-x_{2}\right\rVert\leq\frac{3}{2}|n_{2}|^{\frac{1}{2}},
obtaining
I\leq c|n_{1}|^{-\frac{d}{2}}|n_{2}|^{-\frac{d}{2}}\sum\limits_{\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\langle z_{1}\right\rangle_{\eta}}\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}.
We may apply (
6.5.1
) to reach
I\leq c\left\langle z_{1}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-d}\left\langle z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-d}\left\langle z_{1}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{4}\leq c\left\langle z_{1}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}\left\langle z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}\leq c\left\langle 0z_{1}z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}.
This finishes
Case 1.

Case 2.

Here we assume
\left\langle z_{1}\right\rangle_{\eta}=|n_{1}|^{\frac{1}{2}}
and
\left\langle z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}=\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert.
Here we have
G(z_{1},z)\leq c|n_{1}|^{-\frac{d}{2}}.
We may split
I
into the sum over
n
and over
x.
This gives
I\leq c|n_{1}|^{-\frac{d}{2}}\sum\limits_{\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq\frac{1}{2}|n_{1}|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-(d-2)}\sum\limits_{|n|\leq\frac{|n_{1}|}{4}}G(z_{2},z).
For every
\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq\frac{1}{2}|n_{1}|^{\frac{1}{2}},
we have
\left\lVert x-x_{2}\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert x\right\rVert+\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert\leq\frac{3}{2}\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert
and
\left\lVert x-x_{2}\right\rVert\geq\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert-\left\lVert x\right\rVert\geq\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert.
Next, break the inner sum into two parts, the first one being over all
n
such that
|n-n_{2}|\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert
and the second one being over all other
n.
In the first part, we have a bound of the form
G(z_{2},z)\leq ce^{-c^{\prime}\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert},
thus
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}|n|\leq\frac{|n_{1}|}{4}\\
|n-n_{2}|\leq\frac{\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert}{2}\end{subarray}}G(z_{2},z)\leq c\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert^{2}e^{-c^{\prime}\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert}\leq c^{\prime\prime}\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert^{-(d-2)}.
We focus now in the second part of this inner sum. Here, we have
\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert\leq|n-n_{2}|\leq|n|+|n_{2}|\leq\frac{5}{4}\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert^{2}.
Thus, we can bound
G(z_{2},z)\leq ce^{-c^{\prime}\frac{\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert^{2}}{k}}k^{-\frac{d}{2}}
for some
1\leq k\leq\frac{5}{4}\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert^{2}.
Then, we may bound the second part of the inner sum of
I
as follows
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}|n|\leq\frac{|n_{1}|}{4}\\
|n-n_{2}|>\frac{\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert}{2}\end{subarray}}G(z_{2},z)\leq c\sum\limits_{1\leq k\leq\frac{5}{4}\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert^{2}}e^{-c^{\prime}\frac{\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert^{2}}{k}}k^{-\frac{d}{2}}.
By virtue of (
2.14
), we have
\sum\limits_{1\leq k\leq\frac{5}{4}\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert^{2}}e^{-c^{\prime}\frac{\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert^{2}}{k}}k^{-\frac{d}{2}}\leq c\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert^{2(-\frac{d}{2}+1)}=c\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert^{-(d-2)}.
Therefore, we may bound
I
as follows

I\leq c|n_{1}|^{-\frac{d}{2}}\sum\limits_{\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq\frac{1}{2}|n_{1}|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-(d-2)}\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert^{-(d-2)}.

We have
\sum\limits_{\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq\frac{1}{2}|n_{1}|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-(d-2)}\leq 1+c\int\limits_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}|n_{1}|^{\frac{1}{2}}}dt\ t^{-(d-2)}t^{d-1}=1+c\int\limits_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}|n_{1}|^{\frac{1}{2}}}dt\ t\leq c^{\prime}|n_{1}|.
Substituting above yields
I\leq c|n_{1}|^{-\frac{d}{2}+1}\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert^{-(d-2)}=c\left\langle z_{1}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}\left\langle z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}\leq c\left\langle 0z_{1}z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}.

This completed
Case 2.

Case 3.

Here we assume
\left\langle z_{1}\right\rangle_{\eta}=\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert
and
\left\langle z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}=|n_{2}|^{\frac{1}{2}}.
Then
|n-n_{2}|\asymp|n_{2}|
since
|n|\leq\frac{\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert}{2}\leq\frac{|n_{2}|}{2}
and we may bound
G(z,z_{2})\leq c\left\lVert z-z_{2}\right\rVert^{-\frac{d}{2}}\leq c^{\prime}|n_{2}|^{-\frac{d}{2}}.
This gives
I\leq c|n_{2}|^{-\frac{d}{2}}\sum\limits_{\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\langle z_{1}\right\rangle_{\eta}}\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}G(z_{1},z).
We may now employ the same split of this sum as in
Case 2
, deducing

\textstyle I\leq c|n_{2}|^{-\frac{d}{2}}\sum\limits_{\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert}\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-(d-2)}\sum\limits_{|n|\leq\frac{1}{4}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert^{2}}G(z_{1},z).

We may break again the inner sum over those
n
for which
|n-n_{1}|\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert
and over all other
n,
and we will find that

\textstyle\sum\limits_{|n|\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert}G(z_{1},z)\leq c\left[\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}|n|\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert\\
|n-n_{1}|\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert\end{subarray}}e^{-c^{\prime}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert}+\sum\limits_{1\leq k\leq\frac{5}{4}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert^{2}}e^{-c^{\prime}\frac{\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert^{2}}{k}}k^{-\frac{d}{2}}\right].

And again, we apply (
2.11
) and (
2.14
), obtaining

\displaystyle\textstyle\sum\limits_{\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert}\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-(d-2)}

\displaystyle\leq c\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\textstyle\sum\limits_{1\leq k\leq\frac{5}{4}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert^{2}}e^{-c^{\prime}\frac{\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert^{2}}{k}}k^{-\frac{d}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq c\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert^{2(-\frac{d}{2}+1)}=c\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert^{-(d-2)}.

It is also clear that
\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert e^{-c^{\prime}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert}\leq c\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert^{-(d-2)}.
Substituting yields
I\leq c|n_{2}|^{-\frac{d}{2}}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert^{2}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert^{-(d-2)}.
Since we assume
\left\langle z_{1}\right\rangle_{\eta}\leq\left\langle z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta},
we have
\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert^{2}\leq|n_{2}|,
so that
|n_{2}|^{-\frac{d}{2}}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert^{2}\leq|n_{2}|^{-\frac{d-2}{2}}=\left\langle z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}.
The proof of
Case 3
is finished since
\left\langle z_{1}\right\rangle_{\eta}=\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert
and the definition of spread shows at once
\left\langle z_{1}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}\left\langle z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}\leq\left\langle 0z_{1}z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}.

Case 4.

Here we assume
\left\langle z_{1}\right\rangle_{\eta}=\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert
and
\left\langle z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}=\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert.

Consider first the set of
z\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}
such that
\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\langle z_{1}\right\rangle_{\eta}
and
|n-n_{1}|\leq\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert.
For
z
in this set,
G(z_{1},z)\leq ce^{-c^{\prime}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert},
this entails

\textstyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert\\
|n-n_{1}|\leq\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert\end{subarray}}\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}G(z_{1},z)G(z_{2},z)\leq ce^{-c^{\prime}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert}\sum\limits_{\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert}\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-(d-2)}\sum\limits_{|n|\leq\frac{1}{4}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert^{2}}G(z_{2},z)

Recall we assume
\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert,
and, as done in
Case 2
and
Case 3

\sum\limits_{|n|\leq\frac{1}{4}\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert^{2}}G(z_{2},z)\leq c\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert^{-(d-2)}.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\textstyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert z_{1}\right\rVert\\
|n-n_{1}|\leq\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert\end{subarray}}\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}G(z_{1},z)G(z_{2},z)

\displaystyle\leq c\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert^{2}e^{-c^{\prime}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert}\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert^{-(d-2)}

\displaystyle\leq c^{\prime\prime}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert^{-(d-2)}\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert^{-(d-2)}\leq c^{\prime\prime}\left\langle 0z_{1}z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}.

There remains to handle the terms defining
I
for which
\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert
and
|n-n_{1}|>\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert.
Let
J
be the least integer for which
\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert\leq 2^{J}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert^{\frac{1}{2}}.
In other words,
J
is the least integer for which
\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert\leq 4^{J+1}.
In particular,
4^{J}<\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert\leq 4^{J+1}.
Next, observe that the relation
\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert
implies
|n-n_{1}|\leq|n|+|n_{1}|\leq\frac{5}{4}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert^{2}\leq 5\cdot 4^{J}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert.
Define

\textstyle\begin{split}\mathrm{A}_{j}&\textstyle=\left\{z;\ 4^{j-1}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert<|n-n_{1}|\leq 4^{j}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert,\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert\right\},\quad 1\leq j\leq J-1\\
\mathrm{A}_{J}&\textstyle=\left\{z;\ 4^{J-1}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert<|n-n_{1}|\leq 5\cdot 4^{J}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert,\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert\right\}.\end{split}

We can write
I\leq c\left\langle 0z_{1}z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{J}\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{A}_{j}}\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}G(z_{1},z)G(z_{2},z).
If
z\in\mathrm{A}_{j},
then
G(z_{1},z)\leq ce^{-c^{\prime}\frac{\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert}{4^{j}}}(4^{j}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert)^{-\frac{d}{2}},
and since
\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert\asymp 4^{J},

G(z_{1},z)\leq c\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{-c^{\prime}4^{J-j}}4^{-j\frac{d}{2}}.
Then,

\textstyle\sum\limits_{j=1}^{J}\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{A}_{j}}\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}G(z_{1},z)G(z_{2},z)\leq c\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert^{-\frac{d}{2}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{J}e^{-c^{\prime}4^{J-j}}4^{-j\frac{d}{2}}\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{A}_{j}}\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}G(z_{2},z).

We may break the inner sum in terms of
n
and
x
(for details, see
Case 2
and
Case 3
) and this will give
\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{A}_{j}}\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}G(z_{2},z)\leq\sum\limits_{\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert}\left\langle x\right\rangle^{-(d-2)}\sum\limits_{|n|\leq\frac{1}{4}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert^{2}}G(z_{2},z)\leq c\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert^{2}\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert^{-(d-2)}.
Whence,

\textstyle\sum\limits_{j=1}^{J}\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{A}_{j}}\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}G(z_{1},z)G(z_{2},z)\leq c\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert^{-\frac{d}{2}}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert^{2}\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert^{-(d-2)}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{J}e^{-c^{\prime}4^{J-j}}4^{-j\frac{d}{2}}.

Finally, in the inner sum reverse the order of summation,
\sum\limits_{j=1}^{J}e^{-c^{\prime}4^{J-j}}4^{-j\frac{d}{2}}=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{J-1}e^{-c^{\prime}4^{k}}4^{(k-J)\frac{d}{2}}\leq 4^{-J\frac{d}{2}}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}4^{k\frac{d}{2}}e^{-c^{\prime}4^{k}}\asymp\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert^{-\frac{d}{2}}.
Thus,

\displaystyle\textstyle\sum\limits_{j=1}^{J}\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{A}_{j}}\left\langle z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}G(z_{1},z)G(z_{2},z)

\displaystyle\leq c\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert^{-\frac{d}{2}}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert^{2}\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert^{-(d-2)}\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert^{-\frac{d}{2}}

\displaystyle=c\left\lVert x_{1}\right\rVert^{-(d-2)}\left\lVert x_{2}\right\rVert^{-(d-2)}

\displaystyle=c\left\langle z_{1}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}\left\langle z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}\leq c\left\langle 0z_{1}z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}.

This completes
Case 4.

With the four cases established, we have reached the conclusion of (
6.5.2
).
∎

A corollary of (
6.5.2
) is that for any vertex
v\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1},

\textstyle\sum\limits_{z\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}}\left\langle vz\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}G(z_{1},z)G(z_{2},z)\leq c\left\langle\{0,z_{1}-v,z_{2}-v\}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}=c\left\langle vz_{1}z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}.

Substituting this inequality in (
\ast
) allows deducing, with the aid of (
6.4
),

\textstyle\mathsf{USF}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{W}}(z_{1},z_{2}))\leq c\left\langle\mathrm{V}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}\sum\limits_{v\in\mathrm{V}}\left\langle vz_{1}z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)}\leq c^{\prime}\left\langle\mathrm{W}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)},

where the last inequality follows form the fact that the union of a tree on
\{v,z_{1},z_{2}\}
and a tree on
\mathrm{V}
gives a tree on
\mathrm{W}=\mathrm{V}\cup\{z_{1},z_{2}\}.
We will show now that

\textstyle\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{W}}=\bigcup\limits_{z_{1}\neq z_{2}}\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{W}}(z_{1},z_{2}),

where the vertices
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
run on
\mathrm{W}.
For each vertex
w\in\mathrm{W},
denote by
R^{w}
the
\mathsf{USF}
-ray from
w.
For every
z_{1}\neq z_{2},
two vertices of
\mathrm{W},
their corresponding rays
R^{z_{1}}
and
R^{z_{2}}
will meet at a vertex
v(z_{1},z_{2}).
Furthermore, on the event
\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{W}},
the set of vertices belonging to all
R^{w},
for
w\in\mathrm{W},
is non empty and thus, there exists a vertex
z^{*}
satisfying the following:
z^{*}
is the closest vertex in the graph-distance of the
\mathsf{USF}
-tree, amongst all vertices belonging to all rays
R^{w}
(
w\in\mathrm{W}
), to each one of the vertices
w\in\mathrm{W}.
In other words,
z^{*}
is the vertex where all the rays meet. Consider now a pair
z_{1}\neq z_{2}
such that the graph-distance of
v(z_{1},z_{2})
and
z^{*}
is maximal. Then,
\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{W}}(z_{1},z_{2})
occurs. The proof of theorem (
6.5
) is complete.
∎

7
The separation between components: transitions in dimensions
d=2,6,10,...

Given a vertex
z
of
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda),
denote by
\mathfrak{T}_{z}
the component of
z
in the
\mathsf{USF}
-forest, regarded as spanning subgraph. Construct now
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)/\mathsf{USF}
from
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)
by shorting all edges of all
\mathfrak{T}_{z}
as
z
runs on the vertices of
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda).
Therefore,
\mathfrak{T}_{z}
is a vertex of the new network
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)/\mathsf{USF}.
Define
D:\mathds{Z}^{d}\to\mathds{Z}_{+}
by

\textstyle D(z,z^{\prime})=d_{\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)/\mathsf{USF}}\left(\mathfrak{T}_{z},\mathfrak{T}_{z^{\prime}}\right),

the right hand side being the graph-distance of
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)/\mathsf{USF}.
We call this the
separation
between the components of
z
and
z^{\prime}
relative to the chemical distance of the graph
\mathds{Z}^{d+1}.
Another way of describing
D(z,z^{\prime})
is as follow: it is the minimal number of edges of
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)
that do not belong to any
\mathsf{USF}
-tree in a path connecting
z
and
z^{\prime}.
In this section we are going to show that, for almost every realisation,

\textstyle\max\limits_{z,z^{\prime}\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}}D(z,z^{\prime})=\left\lceil\frac{d-2}{4}\right\rceil.

This section is substantially based on
[
BKPS04
]
.

7.1
Random relations and stochastic dimension

We will be considering relations between vertices of
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda).
If
R
is one such relation, we will use the common notation
z_{1}Rz_{2}
to mean
(z_{1},z_{2})\in R.
Also, the
composition
of two relations
L
and
R
is, by definition, the set of pairs
(z_{1},z_{2})
such that there exists a
z
satisfying
z_{1}Lz

and

zRz_{2}.
We write
LR
to denote the composition of
L
and
R
(in this order). We will say that a random relation
R
on the vertices of
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)
has
stochastic dimension

\alpha\in[0,d+1),
relative to the metric
\eta
(
6.1
), and write
\dim_{\eta}(R)=\alpha
if there exists a constant
c=c(R)>0
such that for all vertices
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4}
of
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)

\textstyle c\mathds{P}{}\left(z_{1}Rz_{2}\right)\geq\left\langle z_{1}z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d+1-\alpha)},

and

\textstyle\mathds{P}{}\left(z_{1}Rz_{2},z_{3}Rz_{4}\right)\leq c\left\langle z_{1}z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d+1-\alpha)}\left\langle z_{3}z_{4}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d+1-\alpha)}+c\left\langle z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}z_{4}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d+1-\alpha)}.

Remark ( 7.1
)

That a random relation
R
has stochastic dimension
d+1
is the same as saying that
\displaystyle\inf_{z_{1},z_{2}}\mathds{P}{}\left(z_{1}Rz_{2}\right)>0.

Remark ( 7.2
)

Let
d\geq 3.
Denote by
U_{\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)}
the random relation: “
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
are in the same
\mathsf{USF}
-component.” Then,
U_{\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)}
has stochastic dimension
three
, relative to
\eta,
this is a consequence of the definition and of theorems (
6.2
) and (
6.5
). Indeed, write
z\sim z^{\prime}
to mean
zU_{\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)}z^{\prime}.
Then, (
6.2
) shows that
c\mathds{P}{}\left(z_{1}\sim z_{2}\right)\geq\left\langle z_{1}z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d+1-3)}.
Next, observe that

\textstyle\mathds{P}{}\left(z_{1}\sim z_{2},z_{3}\sim z_{4}\right)\leq\mathds{P}{}\left(z_{1}\sim z_{2},z_{3}\sim z_{4},z_{1}\sim z_{3}\right)+\mathds{P}{}\left(z_{1}\sim z_{2},z_{3}\sim z_{4},z_{1}\not\sim z_{3}\right).

The event
\{z_{1}\sim z_{2},z_{3}\sim z_{4},z_{1}\sim z_{3}\}
is the event where all the four vertices are in the same
\mathsf{USF}
-tree, this the probability of this event is bounded above by a universal multiple of
\left\langle z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}z_{4}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d+1-3)}
by (
6.5
). To estimate the probability of the event
\{z_{1}\sim z_{2},z_{3}\sim z_{4},z_{1}\not\sim z_{3}\}
we may construct the
\mathsf{USF}
-forest
\mathfrak{F}
using Wilson’s algorithm rooted at infinity with ordering of vertices
(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4},\ldots).
If
S^{z}
denotes the random walk in this construction of
\mathfrak{F},
then

\displaystyle\mathds{P}{}\left(z_{1}\sim z_{2},z_{3}\sim z_{4},z_{1}\not\sim z_{3}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}{}\left(S^{z_{1}}\cap S^{z_{2}}\neq\varnothing,S^{z_{3}}\cap S^{z_{4}}\neq\varnothing\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}{}\left(S^{z_{1}}\cap S^{z_{2}}\neq\varnothing\right)\mathds{P}{}\left(S^{z_{3}}\cap S^{z_{4}}\neq\varnothing\right)

\displaystyle\leq c\left\langle z_{1}z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d+1-3)}\left\langle z_{3}z_{4}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d+1-3)},

the last inequality by virtue of lemmas (
6.2.1
) and (
6.2.2
).

Remark ( 7.3
)

We are going to base the results of this section on those of the paper
[
BKPS04
]
, making the necessary adaptations according to our conveniences. We will see that, in a sense, the stochastic dimension relative to
\eta
is the same the stochastic dimension they defined (that is, relative to the Euclidean distance)
minus
one.

We introduce the following annuli: for
n<N
two integers and
z\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1},

\textstyle\mathrm{A}_{n}^{N}(z)=\{z^{\prime}\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}\mid 2^{n}\leq\left\langle z-z^{\prime}\right\rangle_{\eta}<2^{N}\}.

Notice
\mathrm{card}\left(\mathrm{A}_{n}^{N}(z)\right)\asymp 2^{N(d+2)}.

Proposition ( 7.4
)

(
[
BKPS04
, Lemma 2.8]
)

Let
L
and
R
be two independent random relations of
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda).
Suppose that
\dim_{\eta}(L)=d+1-\alpha
and
\dim_{\eta}(R)=d+1-\beta,
both exist. Denote
\gamma=\alpha+\beta-d-2.
For
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1},
and
1\leq n\leq N,
let
S_{z_{1},z_{2}}=S_{z_{1},z_{2}}(n,N)\mathop{=}\limits^{\mathrm{def.}}\sum\limits_{z\in A_{n}^{N}(z_{1})}\mathds{1}_{\{z_{1}Lz\}}\mathds{1}_{\{zRz_{2}\}}.
If
\left\langle z_{1}z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}<2^{n-1}
and
N\geq n,

\mathds{P}{}\left(S_{z_{1},z_{2}}>0\right)\geq c\frac{\sum\limits_{k=n}^{N}2^{-k\gamma}}{\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N}2^{-k\gamma}},
where
c
is a constant that may depend solely on
L,

R
and dimension.

Proof.

We may repeat word by word, with very minor notational modifications, the proof of Lemma 2.8 of
[
BKPS04
]
bearing in mind that the only substantial thing that changes is
\mathrm{card}\left(\mathrm{A}_{k}^{k+1}(z_{1})\right)\asymp 2^{k(d+2)}.
Also keep in mind that their Lemma 2.6 is our Proposition (
6.4
). Thus, their formula (2.6) changes to

\textstyle\mathds{E}{}\left(S_{z_{1},z_{2}}\right)\asymp\sum\limits_{k=n}^{N}2^{k(d+2)}2^{-k\alpha}2^{-k\beta}=\sum\limits_{k=n}^{N}2^{-k\gamma},

the rest of the proof goes on without major modification except that we need to replace
d
by
d+2
in their formula (2.7).
∎

Proposition ( 7.5
)

(
[
BKPS04
, Corollary 2.9]
)

Let
L
and
R
be two independent random relations of
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda).
Suppose that
\dim_{\eta}(L)=d+1-\alpha
and
\dim_{\eta}(R)=d+1-\beta,
both exist. Denote
\gamma=\max(0,\alpha+\beta-d-2).
There exists a constant
c>0
such that for all
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1},

\textstyle\mathds{P}{}\left(z_{1}LRz_{2}\right)\geq c\left\langle z_{1}z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\gamma}.

Proof.

The proof of Corollary 2.9 of
[
BKPS04
]
applies almost verbatim: in their second sentence replace “lemma” with “Proposition (
7.4
).”
∎

Proposition ( 7.6
)

(
[
BKPS04
, Lemma 2.10]
)

Let
\alpha,\beta\in[0,d+1)
satisfy
\alpha+\beta>d+2.
Let
\gamma=\alpha+\beta-d-2.
Then,

\textstyle\sum\limits_{z\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}}\left\langle z_{1}z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\alpha}\left\langle z_{2}z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\beta}\asymp\left\langle z_{1}z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\gamma}

for all vertices
z_{1},z_{2}
of
\mathds{Z}^{d+1},
and with any implicit constant being universal.

Proof.

The proof of Lemma 2.10 of
[
BKPS04
]
applies without changes except to notation.
∎

Proposition ( 7.7
)

(
[
BKPS04
, Lemma 2.11]
)

Let
M
be a positive integer and let
\alpha,\beta\in[0,d+1)
satisfy
\alpha+\beta>d+2.
Denote
\gamma=\alpha+\beta-d-2.
There exists a constant
c>0
such that for all subsets
\mathrm{V}
and
\mathrm{W}
of
\mathds{Z}^{d+1}
with at most
M
points, we have

\textstyle\sum\limits_{z\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}}\left\langle\mathrm{V}\cup\{z\}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\alpha}\left\langle\mathrm{W}\cup\{z\}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\beta}\leq c\left\langle\mathrm{V}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\alpha}\left(\min\limits_{(v,w)\in\mathrm{V}\times\mathrm{W}}\left\langle vw\right\rangle_{\eta}\right)^{-\gamma}\left\langle\mathrm{W}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\beta}\leq c\left\langle\mathrm{V}\cup\mathrm{W}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\gamma}.

Proof.

We may use the same proof as that of Lemma 2.11 of
[
BKPS04
]
keeping in mind that their Lemma 2.6 is our Proposition (
6.4
) and, Lemma 2.10 is Proposition (
7.6
).
∎

Proposition ( 7.8
)

(
[
BKPS04
, Lemma 2.12]
)

Let
\alpha,\beta\in[0,d+1)
satisfy
\alpha+\beta>d+2.
Set
\gamma=\alpha+\beta-d-2.

(a)

There exists a constant
c>0
such that for all vertices
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1},

\textstyle\sum\limits_{z\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}}\left\langle z_{1}z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\alpha}\left\langle z_{2}z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\beta}\left\langle z_{3}z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\gamma}\leq c\left\langle z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\gamma}

(b)

There exists a constant
c>0
such that for all vertices
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4}\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1},

\textstyle\sum\limits_{z\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}}\left\langle z_{1}z_{2}z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\alpha}\left\langle z_{3}z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\beta}\left\langle z_{4}z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\gamma}\leq c\left\langle z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}z_{4}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\gamma}

Proof.

The proof is essentially the same as that of Lemma 2.12 of
[
BKPS04
]
and their formula (2.9), except we need to use (
6.5.1
) in some step. For convenience of the reader, we repeat the proof.

We begin by establishing the first item. By changing the indices should the need arise, we may assume that
\left\langle z_{1}z_{3}\right\rangle_{\eta}\leq\left\langle z_{2}z_{3}\right\rangle_{\eta}.
Denote by
\mathrm{A}
the set of
z\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}
satisfying
\left\langle z_{3}z\right\rangle_{\eta}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\langle z_{1}z_{3}\right\rangle_{\eta}.
When
z\in\mathrm{A}
we have,
\left\langle z_{1}z\right\rangle_{\eta}\geq\left\langle z_{1}z_{3}\right\rangle_{\eta}-\left\langle z_{3}z\right\rangle_{\eta}\geq\frac{1}{2}\left\langle z_{1}z_{3}\right\rangle_{\eta}
and, similarly, since
\left\langle z_{1}z_{3}\right\rangle_{\eta}\leq\left\langle z_{2}z_{3}\right\rangle_{\eta},
we also have
\left\langle z_{2}z\right\rangle_{\eta}\geq\frac{1}{2}\left\langle z_{2}z_{3}\right\rangle_{\eta}.
Therefore,

\textstyle\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{A}}\left\langle z_{1}z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\alpha}\left\langle z_{2}z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\beta}\left\langle z_{3}z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\gamma}\leq 2^{\alpha}\left\langle z_{1}z_{3}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\alpha}2^{\beta}\left\langle z_{2}z_{3}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\beta}\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{A}}\left\langle z_{3}z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\gamma}.

Apply (
6.5.1
) to obtain the bound
\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{A}}\left\langle z_{3}z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\gamma}\leq c\left\langle z_{1}z_{3}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{d+2-\gamma}.
Substitute above,

\displaystyle\textstyle\sum\limits_{z\in\mathrm{A}}\left\langle z_{1}z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\alpha}\left\langle z_{2}z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\beta}\left\langle z_{3}z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\gamma}

\displaystyle\leq c\left\langle z_{1}z_{3}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\alpha}\left\langle z_{2}z_{3}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\beta}\left\langle z_{1}z_{3}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{d+2-\gamma}

\displaystyle=c\left\langle z_{1}z_{3}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\gamma}\left\langle z_{2}z_{3}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\gamma}\frac{\left\langle z_{1}z_{3}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{d+2-\alpha}}{\left\langle z_{2}z_{3}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{d+2-\alpha}}\leq c\left\langle z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\gamma},

because
\gamma>0
and
\left\langle z_{1}z_{3}\right\rangle_{\eta}\leq\left\langle z_{2}z_{3}\right\rangle_{\eta}.
We now estimate the sum over the complement of
\mathrm{A}.
Here we apply (
7.6
), by definition of
\mathrm{A},
the relation
z\notin\mathrm{A}
implies
\left\langle z_{3}z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\gamma}\leq 2^{\gamma}\left\langle z_{1}z_{3}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\gamma}.
Thus,

\displaystyle\textstyle\sum\limits_{z\notin\mathrm{A}}\left\langle z_{1}z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\alpha}\left\langle z_{2}z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\beta}\left\langle z_{3}z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\gamma}

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq 2^{\gamma}\left\langle z_{1}z_{3}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\gamma}\sum\limits_{z\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}}\left\langle z_{1}z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\alpha}\left\langle z_{2}z\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\beta}

\displaystyle\leq c\left\langle z_{1}z_{3}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\gamma}\left\langle z_{1}z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\gamma}\leq c\left\langle z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-\gamma},

by virtue of (
7.6
). Combining these two estimates we obtain the first item of the proposition.

Now we prove the second item. By (
6.4
), we obtain
\left\langle z_{1}z_{2}z\right\rangle_{\eta}\geq c\left\langle z_{1}z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}\min\{\left\langle z_{1}z\right\rangle_{\eta},\left\langle z_{2}z\right\rangle_{\eta}\}.
Use this, the first item of the proposition, and recall that
\left\langle z_{1}z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}\left\langle z_{2}z_{3}z_{4}\right\rangle_{\eta}\geq\left\langle z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}z_{4}\right\rangle_{\eta}
which holds by definition of the spread.
∎

Theorem ( 7.9
)

(
[
BKPS04
, Theorem 2.4]
)

Let
L
and
R
be two independent random relations on the vertices of
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda).
Assume that their stochastic dimensions (relative to
\eta
) exist. Then, their composition
LR
also has stochastic dimension relative to
\eta
and it satisfies
\dim_{\eta}(LR)=\min\left(\dim_{\eta}(L)+\dim_{\eta}(R)+1,d+1\right).

Proof.

Set
\alpha
and
\beta
to be defined as follows
\dim_{\eta}(L)=d+1-\alpha
and
\dim_{\eta}(R)=d+1-\beta.
Notice that
\gamma\mathop{=}\limits^{\mathrm{def.}}\alpha+\beta-d-2=d+1-\big(\dim_{\eta}(L)+\dim_{\eta}(R)+1\big).
The arguments in the proof of Theorem 2.4 of
[
BKPS04
]
can be modified very easily by recalling that Corollary 2.9 in their paper is (
7.5
), Lemma 2.10 is (
7.6
), Lemma 2.11 is (
7.7
) and equation (2.9) is part (b) of (
7.8
). Also, notice that their condition “
\dim_{S}(\mathcal{L})+\dim_{S}(\mathcal{R})>d
” changes to “
\dim_{\eta}(L)+\dim_{\eta}(R)+1>d+1
”, the latter being equivalent to
\gamma>0,
and similarly when the symbol
>
is replaced by
\leq.

∎

7.2
Tail triviality of random relations

Recall that an event in any probability space is said to
trivial
if its probability is either zero or one, equivalently, if its probability
p
satisfies
p=p^{2}.
It is easy to see that the set of trivial events is a
\sigma
-field, called the
trivial

\sigma
-field (relative to the given probability measure). A
\sigma
-algebra is said to be
trivial
if it is contained in the trivial
\sigma
-field. If
R
is a random relation on the vertices of
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda),
we denote by
\mathscr{F}^{R}_{\mathrm{A}},
where
\mathrm{A}\subset\mathds{Z}^{d+1}\times\mathds{Z}^{d+1},
the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the events
\{zRz^{\prime}\}
as
(z,z^{\prime})\in\mathrm{A}.
If
z
is a vertex, we define the
tail
\sigma
-field based at
z
on the left of
R,
denoted by
\mathscr{L}_{z}^{R},
to be the
\sigma
-field generated by the events
\{zRz^{\prime}\}
as
z^{\prime}\to\infty,
in other words,
\mathscr{L}_{z}^{R}
is the intersection of all
\mathscr{F}_{\{z\}\times\mathrm{K}^{\complement}}^{R}
as
\mathrm{K}
runs on all finite subsets of
\mathds{Z}^{d+1}.
For simplicity, we will say “left tail at
z
” to refer to
\mathscr{L}_{z}^{R}.
In a similar manner we define the
tail
\sigma
-field
\mathscr{R}_{z}^{R}
based at
z
on the right of
R,
to be the intersection of all
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{K}^{\complement}\times\{z\}}^{R}
as
\mathrm{K}
runs on all finite subsets of
\mathds{Z}^{d+1};
we will talk about “right tail at
z
” to refer to this
\sigma
-algebra. We also define the
(proper) tail

\sigma
-algebra of
R
to be the intersection of all
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{K}_{1}^{\complement}\times\mathrm{K}_{2}^{\complement}}^{R}
as
\mathrm{K}_{1}
and
\mathrm{K}_{2}
run over all finite subsets of
\mathds{Z}^{d+1},
we denote it as
\mathscr{T}^{R}.
Finally, we define the
restricted composition (relative to
\eta
)

L\diamond R
of two relations
L
and
R
(in that order) to be the set of all pairs
(z_{1},z_{2})
such that there exists a
z\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}
satisfying the relations
z_{1}Lz
and
zRz_{2}
and
z
satisfies

\textstyle\left\langle z_{1}z_{2}\right\rangle_{\eta}\leq\min(\left\langle z_{1}z\right\rangle_{\eta},\left\langle z_{2}z\right\rangle_{\eta}).

Theorem ( 7.10
)

(
[
BKPS04
, Theorem 3.3]
)

Let
L
and
R
be two independent random relations on the vertices of
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda).

(a)

If all left tail
\sigma
-fields
\mathscr{L}^{L}_{z}
of
L
are trivial and
R
has a trivial tail
\sigma
-field
\mathscr{T}^{R},
then the restricted composition
L\diamond R
has trivial left tail
\sigma
-fields
\mathscr{L}^{L\diamond R}_{z}.

(b)

If
\dim_{\eta}(L)
and
\dim_{\eta}(R)
both exist, then
L\diamond R
has also stochastic dimension (relative to
\eta
) and we have
\dim_{\eta}(LR)=\min\left(\dim_{\eta}(L)+\dim_{\eta}(R)+1,d+1\right).

(c)

If
\dim_{\eta}(L)
and
\dim_{\eta}(R)
both exist, all left tail
\sigma
-fields
\mathscr{L}^{L}_{z}
of
L
are trivial, all right tail
\sigma
-algebras
\mathscr{R}^{R}_{z}
of
R
are trivial and
\dim_{\eta}(L)+\dim_{\eta}(R)\geq d,
then
\inf\limits_{z_{1},z_{2}}\mathds{P}{}\left(z_{1}L\diamond Rz_{2}\right)=1.

Let now
m\geq 2
and
(R_{i})_{i=1,\ldots,m}
be independent random relations on the vertices of
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)
such that
\dim_{\eta}(R_{i})
exists for each
1\leq i\leq m.

(d)

Suppose that
\sum\limits_{i=1}^{m}\dim_{\eta}(R_{i})+m\geq d+2,
all left tails
\sigma
-fields
\mathscr{L}^{R_{1}}_{z}
are trivial, each of
\mathscr{T}^{R_{2}},\ldots,\mathscr{T}^{R_{m-1}}
are trivial tail
\sigma
-algebras, and all right tail
\sigma
-algebras
\mathscr{R}^{R_{m}}_{z}
are also trivial. Then
\mathds{P}{}\left(z_{1}R_{1}\cdots R_{m}z_{2}\right)=1
for all
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
vertices of
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda).

Proof.

The proofs of Theorem 3.3 and Corollary 3.4 of
[
BKPS04
]
carry over
mutatis mutandis
to our context.
∎

7.3
The
\mathsf{WSF}
connectedness relation

Let
\Gamma
be any network. We will consider the following relation
U_{\Gamma}
on the vertices of
\Gamma:
“
v
and
v^{\prime}
belong to the same
\mathsf{WSF}
-component.” We have the following very general result regarding tail triviality of
U_{\Gamma}.

Theorem ( 7.11
)

Suppose
\Gamma
is any network satisfying the usual assumptions. Assume further that

(a)

The network random walk of
\Gamma
is transient.

(b)

The network possesses the Liouville property
(
\mathsf{LP}
)
§
3.2
.

Then, all left and right tail
\sigma
-algebras of
U_{\Gamma}
are trivial. If, additionally to the previous hypotheses,

(c)

The
\mathsf{WSF}
of
\Gamma
is one ended, that is to say, for almost every realisation, every tree in the
\mathsf{WSF}
has one end.

Then, the tail
\sigma
-field of
U_{\Gamma}
is also trivial.

For the proof see Theorem 4.5 of
[
BKPS04
]
, together with Remark 4.6.

We need one last ingredient. Consider two random subsets of vertices,
A
and
Z,
of a network
\Gamma.
It is said that
Z

stochastically dominates

A
if there exists a coupling
\mu
of
A
and
Z
such that
\mu(A\subset Z)=1.

Theorem ( 7.12
)

(
[
BKPS04
, Theorem 4.1]
)

Let
\Gamma
be any network. Let
\mathfrak{F},

{\mathfrak{F}}_{0},\ldots,{\mathfrak{F}}_{m}
be independent samples of the
\mathsf{WSF}
of the network
\Gamma.
If
x
is a vertex of
\Gamma,
denote by
\mathrm{V}_{x}^{\mathfrak{F}}
the vertex set of the component of
x
in
\mathfrak{F}.
Fix any vertex
o
of
\Gamma
and set
\mathrm{V}_{0}=\mathrm{V}_{o}^{\mathfrak{F}}.
For
j\geq 1,
define inductively
\mathrm{V}_{j}
to be the union of all vertex components of
\mathfrak{F}
that are contained in, or adjacent to,
\mathrm{V}_{j-1};
in other words,
\mathrm{V}_{j}
is the union of
\mathrm{V}_{x}^{\mathfrak{F}}
as
x
runs on
\overline{\mathrm{V}}_{j-1}
(closure in
\Gamma
). Let
\mathrm{Q}_{0}=\mathrm{V}_{o}^{\mathfrak{F}_{0}}.
For
j\geq 1,
define inductively
\mathrm{Q}_{j}
to be the union of all vertex components of
\mathfrak{F}_{j}
that intersect
\mathrm{Q}_{j-1};
in other words,
\mathrm{Q}_{j}
is the union of all
\mathrm{V}_{x}^{\mathfrak{F}_{j}}
such that
\mathrm{V}_{x}^{\mathfrak{F}_{j}}\cap\mathrm{Q}_{j-1}\neq\varnothing.
Then,
\mathrm{V}_{m}
stochastically dominates
\mathrm{Q}_{m}.

Proof.

It is a simple adaptation of Theorem 4.1 of
[
BKPS04
]
, indeed in their first sentence change
B_{R}
and replace it with
B_{R}=\{v;d(v,o)<R\},
where
d
is the graph-distance of
\Gamma.
The remainder of their proof proceeds verbatim.
∎

7.4
The main theorem

Theorem ( 7.13
)

With the notation at the beginning of the section,
\max\limits_{z,z^{\prime}\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}}D(z,z^{\prime})=\left\lceil\frac{d-2}{4}\right\rceil,\quad a.s.

Proof.

The proof follows the lines of that of Theorem 1.1 and Proposition 5.1 of
[
BKPS04
]
. For convenience of the reader, we provide full details.

When
d=1,2,
we know that
\mathsf{USF}
of
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)
is a tree and thus the separation between components is zero, this agrees with the formula given. Assume then that
d\geq 3.

Let
m=\left\lceil\frac{d-2}{4}\right\rceil.
Consider
m+1
independent copies of the
U_{\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)}
relation. By (
7.2
), we know that
\dim_{\eta}\left(U_{\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)}\right)=3.
Next, by (
7.11
), bearing in mind that
\mathsf{USF}
is one ended (see sect.
5
) this random relation possesses trivial tail
\sigma
-field as well as trivial left tail and right tail
\sigma
-algebras. We may apply item (d) of (
7.10
) to conclude that, if
R
is the composition of the
m+1
copies of
U_{\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)}
then
\inf\limits_{z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}}\mathds{P}{}\left(z_{1}Rz_{2}\right)=1.
Finally, define
S
to be the relation
D(z,z^{\prime})\leq m.
By virtue of (
7.12
), we know that
\mathds{P}{}\left(z_{1}Sz_{1}\right)\geq\mathds{P}{}\left(z_{1}Rz_{2}\right)
and thus,
\mathds{P}{}\left(D(z,z^{\prime})\leq m,\quad\forall z,z^{\prime}\right)=1.

The proof of the cases
3\leq d\leq 6
is complete, so it only remains to prove the lower bound for the cases
d\geq 7.
To prove the lower bounds we will establish first

(
\ast
)

\mathds{P}{}\left(D(z,z^{\prime})\leq k\right)\leq c\left\langle zz^{\prime}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{4k-(d-2)}

for all
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}
and
k\in\mathds{N},
the constant
c
depending on
k
and
d,
but not on
z
or
z^{\prime}.
When
k\geq m,
the criterion holds since then
4k\geq d-2\geq 5
and
\left\langle zz^{\prime}\right\rangle_{\eta}\geq 1.
Assume
k<m,
so that
4k-(d-2)\leq-1.
Consider a finite sequence
(z_{j},z_{j}^{\prime})_{j=0,\ldots,k}
of pairs of vertices of
\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)
with
z_{0}=z,

z_{k}^{\prime}=z^{\prime}
and
z_{j}^{\prime}\sim z_{j+1}
for
j=0,\ldots,k-1.
Let
\EuScript{A}=\EuScript{A}(z_{j},z_{j}^{\prime})
be the event in which
z_{j}^{\prime}
is a vertex of
\mathfrak{T}_{z_{j}}
for
j=0,\ldots,k
and for each of these
j,

z_{j}^{\prime}
is not a vertex of
\mathfrak{T}_{z_{i}}
for
i\neq j.
Then,

\textstyle\{D(z,z^{\prime})=k\}\subset\bigcup\limits_{(z_{j},z_{j}^{\prime})}\EuScript{A}(z_{j},z_{j}^{\prime})

where the pairs
(z_{j},z_{j}^{\prime})
run over all sequences
(z_{j},z_{j}^{\prime})
as stated. To estimate the probability of a particular
\EuScript{A}(z_{j},z_{j}^{\prime})
we may construct the
\mathsf{USF}
-forest using Wilson’s rooted at infinity, beginning with the random walks started at
z_{0},z_{0}^{\prime},\ldots,z_{k},z_{k}^{\prime}.
For the event
\EuScript{A}(z_{j},z_{j}^{\prime})
to hold, it is necessary that for each
j=0,\ldots,k,
the random walk started at
z_{j}
must intersect the path of the random walk started at
z_{j}^{\prime}.
Hence, if
S^{z}
denotes the random walk started at
z
(with the different random walks mutually independent),

\textstyle\mathds{P}{}\left(\EuScript{A}\right)\leq\prod\limits_{j=0}^{k}\mathds{P}{}\left(S^{z_{j}}\cap S^{z_{j}^{\prime}}\neq\varnothing\right)=\prod\limits_{j=0}^{k}\sum\limits_{z\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}}G(z_{j},z)G(z_{j}^{\prime},z)\asymp\prod\limits_{j=0}^{k}\left\langle z_{j}z_{j}^{\prime}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)},

where
\asymp
follows from (
6.2.2
). Since
z_{j}^{\prime}\sim z_{j+1},
we can bound

\textstyle\mathds{P}{}\left(D(z,z^{\prime})=k\right)\leq c\sum\limits_{(z_{j})_{j=0}^{k+1}}\prod\limits_{j=0}^{k}\left\langle z_{j}z_{j+1}\right\rangle_{\eta}^{-(d-2)},

the sum extending over all finite paths
(z_{j})
starting at
z
and ending at
z^{\prime}.
We may finally break up this sum, starting with the sum over all
z_{k}
and applying (
7.6
) with
\alpha=\beta=d-2,
so that
\gamma=\alpha+\beta-d-2=d-6>0,
and then continue with the sum over
z_{k-1},
and so on until
z_{1},
this gives the desired bound (
\ast
). Having establish (
\ast
), we can terminate Theorem (
7.13
). Indeed, consider
z\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1},
the event
\displaystyle\left\{\max_{z^{\prime}\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}}D(z,z^{\prime})=k\right\}
belongs to the left-tail at
z
relative to the relation
U_{\Gamma_{d}(\lambda)},
as such it is a trivial event (
7.11
). It is clear from (
\ast
) that this event has probability zero, therefore the event
\displaystyle\left\{\max_{z,z^{\prime}\in\mathds{Z}^{d+1}}D(z,z^{\prime})=k\right\}
also have probability zero.
∎

*
